TL;DR: This paper proposes a hand-object masked training and HOI-dynamics-aware decoder to overcome spurious correlations in HOI recognition, along with a new CI-HOI benchmark (DEHOI testbed) for disentangled evaluation, achieving improved performance across multiple HOI-related tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video-language models rely on spurious correlations among hands, objects, or environmental context rather than reasoning from the actual appearance and dynamics of hands and objects for HOI recognition.

Method: (i) Hand-object masked training: enables robust reasoning from partial hand/object observations; (ii) HOI-dynamics-aware decoder: learns hand- and object-centric embeddings via auxiliary predictions of location and semantics; (iii) CI-HOI evaluation benchmark with DEHOI testbed: separates hand- and object-related observations via inpainting for disentangled evaluation.

Result: The approach quantitatively and qualitatively demonstrates more effective use of hand- and object-centric information, with improvements on DEHOI, standard action recognition, object state recognition, and robot manipulation action recognition.

Conclusion: The proposed training strategy (hand-object masked training + HOI-dynamics-aware decoder) improves HOI understanding by enabling more effective exploitation of hand- and object-centric information, outperforming existing models on DEHOI, standard action recognition, object state recognition, and robot manipulation action recognition.

Abstract: Hand-object interaction (HOI) recognition requires capturing both hand manipulations and object transformations. However, existing video-language models often fall into shortcuts by relying on spurious correlations among hands, objects, or environmental context, rather than reasoning from the appearance and dynamics of hands and objects themselves. To address this limitation, we propose a new learning paradigm that combines (i) hand-object masked training, which enables robust reasoning from partial hand or object observations, and (ii) an HOI-dynamics-aware decoder that explicitly learns hand- and object-centric embeddings through auxiliary predictions of their locations and semantics, enhancing sensitivity to both cues. To systematically evaluate such cue-specific reasoning, we introduce Cue-Isolated HOI (CI-HOI), a new evaluation that assesses models' ability to predict actions from hand- and object-related cues independently. To enable CI-HOI, we curate the DEHOI testbed, which separates hand- and object-related observations for disentangled HOI evaluation through inpainting. Using DEHOI, we demonstrate both quantitatively and qualitatively that our training strategy exploits hand- and object-centric information more effectively than existing models. Our approach improves over existing models on DEHOI, standard action recognition, object state recognition, and even robot manipulation action recognition, leading to more robust HOI understanding.

</details>


### [54] [Beyond wheelchairs and blindfolds: Investigating disability stereotypes in T2I models with INCLUDE-BENCH](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08515)
*Sophia Lichtenberg,Albert Gatt,Judith Masthoff*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: INCLUDE-BENCH is the first large-scale benchmark for evaluating disability-related bias in text-to-image models, revealing that these models consistently default to wheelchair depictions for mobility-impaired/disability prompts, produce less diverse outputs for disability-conditioned generations, show stronger alignment with stereotypical portrayals, and reflect real-world stereotypical associations as measured by the introduced Stereotype Content Model (SCM) Score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disability remains systematically underexplored in bias evaluation of T2I models, and current practices fail to align with sociologically grounded definitions of stereotyping, limiting principled assessment of representational harms toward people with disabilities.

Method: The authors introduce INCLUDE-BENCH, a large-scale benchmark with 119K generated images across multiple bias dimensions and both static and dynamic contexts. They evaluate 15 open-source and 2 closed-source T2I models, and introduce the Stereotype Content Model (SCM) Score for evaluation.

Result: (1) Mobility-impaired and default disability prompts predominantly yield wheelchair depictions across all models; (2) disability-conditioned generations consistently exhibit less diversity; (3) stereotypical portrayals demonstrate stronger disability-text alignment; (4) T2I models reflect real-world stereotypical associations via the SCM Score.

Conclusion: Disability bias in T2I models is systematic and pervasive; the SCM Score provides a sociologically grounded metric for evaluating representational harms toward people with disabilities, highlighting the need for more inclusive and diverse image generation capabilities.

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) models have been shown to exhibit social biases. Prior work has mainly focused on gender, skin tone, and cultural representation within restricted occupational associations, and emerging benchmarks increasingly incorporate these dimensions. However, disability remains systematically underexplored. Current evaluation practices often fail to align with sociologically grounded definitions of stereotyping, limiting principled assessment of representational harms toward people with disabilities (PWD). To address this, we introduce INCLUDE-BENCH, the first large-scale benchmark for evaluating disability-related bias in T2I models. INCLUDE-BENCH comprises 119K generated images based on prompt design across multiple bias dimensions and both static and dynamic contexts. We evaluate 15 open-source and 2 closed-source models. Our key findings reveal that: (1) mobility-impaired and default disability prompts predominantly yield wheelchair depictions across all models; (2) disability-conditioned generations consistently exhibit less diversity; (3) stereotypical portrayals demonstrate stronger disability-text alignment; and (4) we introduce the Stereotype Content Model (SCM) Score, demonstrating that T2I models reflect real-world stereotypical associations.

</details>


### [55] [Whareformer: Learning to Track What is Where in Long Egocentric Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08537)
*Jacob Chalk,Saptarshi Sinha,Dima Damen,Yannis Kalantidis,Diane Larlus*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Whareformer is the first learning-based solution for the OSNOM egocentric video tracking task. It uses a transformer model with updatable memory and track assignment to jointly reason over object appearance and 3D location, achieving SOTA on three benchmarks while trained on only 56 videos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The OSNOM task requires tracking objects moved by the camera wearer in egocentric videos even when they leave the field of view or become occluded, but no learning-based solution existed prior to this work.

Method: Whareformer uses a transformer-based architecture with two components: (1) an updatable memory of established tracks storing evolving appearance and 3D location representations, and (2) a track assignment module that associates observations with existing tracks in a feed-forward manner. A dedicated New Track token is employed to reason about novel objects.

Result: Trained on only 56 videos, Whareformer achieves SOTA performance on 260 long test videos from EPIC-KITCHENS-100 (unseen), IT3DEgo, and HD-EPIC datasets, with significant absolute improvements over prior work.

Conclusion: Relative distance design and evolving track representations enable data-efficient training (56 videos) while generalizing well to large-scale unseen test data across multiple datasets.

Abstract: The recently established 'Out of Sight, Not out of Mind' (OSNOM) task for egocentric videos focuses on tracking objects that are moved by the camera wearer, online, maintaining knowledge of instance locations throughout the video even when they leave the field of view or become heavily occluded. In this paper, we propose the first learning-based solution to the OSNOM task: Whareformer, a transformer-based model with two components: an updatable memory of established tracks and a track assignment module that associates observations with existing tracks in a feed-forward manner. Whareformer jointly reasons over evolving object appearance (what) and updated 3D location (where), and employs a dedicated New Track token to reason about novel objects.
  Thanks to its design choices of using relative distances and evolving track representations, Whareformer is trained on a small set of 56 videos but achieves SOTA performance on 260 long test videos from three datasets: EPIC-KITCHENS-100 (unseen videos), IT3DEgo, and HD-EPIC, with significant absolute improvements over prior work.

</details>


### [56] [VocaDet: Sample-Driven Open-Vocabulary Object Detection and Segmentation via Visual Tokenization and Vector Database Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08541)
*ZhiXin Sun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VocaDet is a sample-driven open-vocabulary object detection and segmentation framework that learns concepts from user-provided positive/negative samples without retraining, using DINOv3 features, agglomerative clustering to create visual tokens stored in a vector database, and background filtering for efficient retrieval-based recognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing open-vocabulary detection methods rely on text prompts, limited visual examples, or expensive feature matching, making them difficult to scale to large and continuously expanding object repositories without retraining.

Method: VocaDet uses DINOv3 as a visual feature extractor, applies agglomerative clustering with adaptive sensitivity to generate multi-granularity visual tokens, stores position-debiased representations and spatial topology in a vector database, and performs efficient retrieval-based matching during inference with a background filtering mechanism.

Result: Experiments on UA-DETRAC dataset show effective open-vocabulary detection performance without conventional detector training, supporting continuously expandable recognition as more samples are accumulated.

Conclusion: VocaDet demonstrates that transforming continuous visual representations into discrete visual vocabularies with retrieval-based recognition enables scalable and expandable open-vocabulary detection without model retraining.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary object detection and segmentation aim to recognize arbitrary objects beyond predefined categories. Although recent vision-language and reference-based approaches have significantly advanced this field, they often rely on text prompts, limited visual examples, or expensive feature matching procedures, making them difficult to scale to large and continuously expanding object repositories. In this work, we propose VocaDet, a sample-driven open-vocabulary object detection and segmentation framework that learns object concepts directly from user-provided positive and negative sample collections without model retraining. The key idea is to transform continuous visual representations into discrete visual vocabularies and perform efficient retrieval-based recognition through a scalable vector database. Specifically, we employ DINOv3 as the visual feature extractor and apply agglomerative clustering with adaptive clustering sensitivity to generate multi-granularity visual tokens. These visual tokens, together with position-debiased representations and spatial topology information, are stored as expandable object memories in a vector database. During inference, query images are converted into visual tokens and efficiently matched against the stored object memories for object localization and segmentation. Furthermore, a background filtering mechanism is introduced to remove frequently occurring background patterns and reduce redundant retrieval operations in practical fixed-camera scenarios. Experiments on the UA-DETRAC dataset demonstrate that VocaDet achieves effective open-vocabulary detection performance without conventional detector training, while supporting continuously expandable recognition capability as additional positive and negative samples are accumulated.

</details>


### [57] [Switch-Reasoner: Learn When to Think in Multitask Mixtures via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08572)
*Yiyang Fang,Pei Fu,Jinjie Li,Jian Liang,Wenke Huang,Ruijie Luo,Shaojie Zhang,Jian Luan,Yi R. Fung,Mang Ye*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Switch-Reasoner is a GRPO-based framework that enables MLLMs to adaptively choose between direct answering and explicit reasoning, achieving better accuracy-efficiency trade-offs across heterogeneous multitask settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs default to a fixed Think-then-Answer paradigm which is inefficient for heterogeneous multitask settings—simple inputs waste resources on unnecessary reasoning, while complex tasks benefit from it.

Method: Switch-Reasoner uses GRPO to treat thinking as a virtual tool invocation, allowing models to adaptively select Direct Mode or Thinking Mode, stabilized by a dual-level regulation mechanism that balances both modes overall and provides sample-level supervision based on relative benefit.

Result: On 11 multimodal tasks, Switch-Reasoner reduces unnecessary reasoning while maintaining strong performance, achieving a better accuracy-efficiency trade-off.

Conclusion: Adaptive reasoning selection improves efficiency in MLLMs without sacrificing accuracy, and the dual-level regulation mechanism effectively stabilizes mode selection during post-training.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) often follow a fixed Think-then-Answer paradigm, which is inefficient in heterogeneous multitask settings because simple inputs may not require explicit reasoning while difficult ones can benefit substantially from it. Learning when to think is also unstable during post-training, where imbalanced rollouts can drive the model toward always-thinking or always-direct behavior. We propose Switch-Reasoner, a GRPO-based framework that learns to adaptively select reasoning modes for MLLMs. It treats thinking as a virtual tool invocation and allows the model to either answer directly or invoke explicit reasoning before answering. To stabilize this decision, we introduce a dual-level regulation mechanism that balances the overall use of Thinking Mode and Direct Mode while providing sample-level supervision based on the relative benefit of the two choices. Experiments on 11 multimodal tasks show that Switch-Reasoner reduces unnecessary reasoning while maintaining strong performance, achieving a better accuracy-efficiency trade-off.

</details>


### [58] [When Structured Sparse Autoencoders Learn Consistent Concepts Across Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08605)
*Weiduo Liao,Yunqiao Yang,Ying Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: S^2AE improves sparse autoencoders for vision-language models by enforcing concept consistency through structured sparsity regularization (exclusive sparsity for inter-group disentanglement + group sparsity for intra-group consistency), achieving 6.06% higher mIoU, lower l0 norm, and >99% explained variance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vanilla SAEs in VLMs struggle to learn modality-consistent concepts due to fragmented coverage in the visual modality, where concepts occupy disjoint spatial regions.

Method: Groups image patches via attention similarity and spatial proximity, then applies structured sparsity regularization—exclusive sparsity for inter-group disentanglement and group sparsity for intra-group consistency—to enforce semantic and spatial concept alignment in SAE latent features.

Result: On Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct: 6.06% avg improvement in semantic alignment (mIoU), 60.81 in representational efficiency (lower l0 norm), >99% explained variance; cross-modal gains of 3.08% in semantic consistency and 2.37% in monosemanticity scores.

Conclusion: S^2AE enhances neuronal monosemanticity via visual structural priors, producing more coherent and disentangled multimodal representations.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have emerged as a promising technique for mechanistic interpretability by learning a set of sparse latent features in large models, each of which encodes a distinct concept. However, in vision-language models (VLMs), vanilla SAEs struggle to learn modality-consistent concepts, with concepts often exhibiting fragmented coverage (i.e., disjoint regions) in the visual modality. To address this challenge, we propose a Structured Sparse AutoEncoder ($S^2AE$) that enforces concept consistency from both semantic and spatial perspectives in the visual modality. Specifically, we group image patches based on Transformer attention similarity and spatial proximity, and introduce a structured sparsity regularization when training the vanilla SAE. The regularization consists of exclusive sparsity for inter-group concept disentanglement and group sparsity for intra-group concept consistency, which drives the latent neurons by SAEs to specialize in distinct, semantically grounded concepts. Evaluated on the \texttt{Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct} model, the method achieves 6.06% average improvement in semantic alignment (mIoU) and 60.81 in representational efficiency (lower l0 norm) while maintaining near-perfect reconstruction fidelity with an Explained Variance above 99%. Cross-modal analysis further demonstrates that $S^2AE$ enhances neuronal monosemanticity by this visual structural prior, achieving a 3.08% average gain in semantic consistency and a 2.37% average gain in monosemanticity scores for both modalities of multimodal features, thereby fostering more coherent and disentangled representations.

</details>


### [59] [Do Transformations Reveal the Truth? Generative Residual Learning for Generalized AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08674)
*Kutub Uddin,Nusrat Tasnim,Awais Khan,Mohammad Umar Farooq,Khalid Malik*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes GenRes and GenRes++, two frameworks for detecting AI-generated images using generative residual learning via neural tensor networks and attention mechanisms, achieving state-of-the-art performance across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid advancement of generative AI has enabled highly realistic deepfake media, causing threats like misinformation, identity theft, fraud, and manipulation of public opinion. Detecting AI-generated images (AIGI) is challenging due to diverse generative methods and subtle artifacts.

Method: GenRes uses a neural tensor network for generative residual learning to model fine-grained relational features between original and transformed samples. GenRes++ extends this with a learnable attention mechanism to aggregate relational features across multiple transformed samples. Both use PE-Core as a feature extractor for generalized, semantically rich embeddings.

Result: Comprehensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets show GenRes++ outperforms existing methods, with improved cross-domain performance and detection of AIGI from unseen generative methods.

Conclusion: The proposed frameworks effectively address the challenge of detecting AI-generated images, particularly under cross-domain and unseen generative method scenarios, by leveraging relational feature learning and attention-based aggregation.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of generative AI has enabled the creation of highly realistic deepfake media, posing significant threats, including misinformation, digital identity theft, fraud, and manipulation of public opinion. AI-generated image (AIGI) detection is reliably challenging due to the diversity of generative methods and the subtle artifacts they leave behind. In this work, we propose GenRes, a novel framework for generative residual learning via a neural tensor network, which models fine-grained relational features between original and transformed samples to enhance generalization. To address scenarios involving multiple generative transformations, we introduce GenRes++, which employs a learnable attention mechanism to aggregate relational features across multiple transformed samples and enables the model to focus on the most informative cues. Both models leverage PE-Core as a feature extractor, providing generalized and semantically rich embeddings that improve cross-domain performance and enable the detection of AIGI generated by unseen methods. Comprehensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed GenRes++ approach outperforms existing methods.

</details>


### [60] [Multi-Resolution Feature Stem for Diabetic Retinopathy lesion segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08679)
*Indranil Dutta,Taehee Jeong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Higher input resolution boosts microaneurysm detection but degrades hemorrhage segmentation in DR lesion analysis. A Multi-Resolution Feature Stem with UNet++ resolves this trade-off by processing multiple scales in parallel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DR lesions vary dramatically in size, creating conflicting architectural demands. The common assumption that higher input resolution is uniformly beneficial has not been systematically tested across different lesion types.

Method: Systematic experiments with U-Net, UNet++, Vision Transformers, DeepLabV3+ at 512×512 and 1024×1024 resolutions. Proposed Multi-Resolution Feature Stem — an input-level pyramid integrated with UNet++ for parallel multi-scale processing.

Result: Higher resolution improves microaneurysm segmentation but counter-intuitively degrades performance on large hemorrhages. The proposed architecture resolves this trade-off efficiently.

Conclusion: Input resolution has opposing, lesion-dependent effects in DR segmentation. Multi-resolution input processing is an effective strategy to handle this variability without sacrificing contextual information.

Abstract: Diabetic Retinopathy (DR) is a leading cause of preventable blindness worldwide, requiring automated lesion segmentation using deep learning models for early detection and monitoring. However, DR lesions vary dramatically in size from tiny microaneurysms to large hemorrhages and exudates. This variability creates conflicting demands on the model architecture and input resolution, posing a challenge for effective design. This work investigates the impact of input resolution on different lesion types. Through systematic experimentation with multiple architectures (U-Net, UNet++, Vision Transformers, DeepLabV3+) at $512 \times 512$ and $1024 \times 1024$ resolutions, we identify a critical, counter-intuitive phenomenon where increasing input resolution has opposing effects on different lesion types. We demonstrate that while higher resolution is essential for resolving fine-grained microaneurysms, it can unexpectedly degrade performance on larger hemorrhages. This finding challenges the common assumption that higher resolution is uniformly beneficial. To address this, we propose a novel Multi-Resolution Feature Stem, an input-level pyramid integrated with a UNet++ backbone. This architecture processes multiple scales in parallel, capturing fine-grained details without sacrificing contextual information. This work contributes crucial empirical evidence of this complex, resolution-dependent behavior and a practical, parameter-efficient architecture that successfully resolves this trade-off.

</details>


### [61] [SAM-MT: Real-Time Interactive Multi-Target Video Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08688)
*Ruiqi Shen,Chang Liu,Henghui Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAM-MT extends SAM2 to real-time multi-target video segmentation by using explicit queries, decoupled masked attention, and sparse memory, achieving latency independent of target count with >36 FPS for 10 targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VOS methods for multi-target settings replicate single-target processing per object, causing reduced FPS and unbounded latency as target count increases.

Method: SAM-MT transforms SAM2 into an interactive framework with explicit queries for individual targets and shared global context, decoupled masked attention to prevent cross-target interference, sparse memory for temporal evolution, and occlusion/overlap handling strategies.

Result: Achieves real-time speed (>36 FPS for 10 targets) with latency decoupled from target count, while maintaining SAM2's robust single-target segmentation performance.

Conclusion: SAM-MT enables real-time multi-target VOS without sacrificing performance, effectively overcoming the latency scaling issue in prior approaches.

Abstract: Modern Video Object Segmentation (VOS) involves tracking and segmenting user-specified targets. While recent approaches have achieved remarkable performance in single-target scenarios, extending them to multi-target settings typically involves replicating the single-target processing for each individual object, resulting in reduced frame rates (FPS) with unbounded latency as target count increases. Built upon Segment Anything 2 (SAM2), we propose SAM-MT, which addresses this by transforming the model into an interactive framework for real-time Multi-Target video segmentation. SAM-MT uses explicit queries to represent different individual targets, in parallel with a shared representation for global context. It employs decoupled masked attention to keep individual identities distinct from cross-target interference, and sparse memory for stable temporal evolution, along with specialized strategies for occlusion handling and overlap prevention. SAM-MT successfully decouples latency from the number of targets, achieving real-time speed on par with single-target baselines (>36 FPS for 10 targets) while maintaining SAM2's robust video segmentation performance.

</details>


### [62] [HumanForge: A Human-Centric Deepfake Video Benchmark with Multi-Agent Forgery Rationales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08705)
*Wenbo Xu,Zhimin Chen,Xiaojie Liang,Hengrui Liu,Wei Lu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HumanForge is a large-scale, multi-paradigm human-centric video forgery dataset with 18K+ high-fidelity segments, built via a Gen2Anno multi-agent pipeline (6 agents), featuring structured omni-annotations. Benchmarks show SOTA detectors and LMMs struggle with zero-shot generalization and fine-grained reasoning on this dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video forgery benchmarks focus narrowly on face-swapping or global text-to-video synthesis, neglecting human-object/human-human interactions and multi-modal alignment. HumanForge addresses this gap with a unified benchmark covering diverse human-centric forgery paradigms.

Method: Proposes Gen2Anno, a modular active multi-agent pipeline (LangGraph-based) with 6 specialized agents (source profiling, MoE-based reference analysis, closed-loop forensic verification, etc.) to automatically construct and annotate 18K+ high-fidelity video segments with binary decisions, fine-grained artifact categories, and spatio-temporal localization—without manual labeling or hallucinated prompts.

Result: Extensive evaluation using SOTA traditional detectors and LMMs reveals significant challenges in zero-shot generalization and fine-grained reasoning on HumanForge, indicating the dataset successfully captures difficult forgery scenarios.

Conclusion: HumanForge and Gen2Anno advance human-centric video forensics by providing a comprehensive benchmark and automated annotation pipeline. The dataset reveals current model limitations, driving future research in fine-grained, multi-modal forgery detection.

Abstract: Rapid advancements in video diffusion models and temporal editing tools have enabled the generation of highly realistic human-centric videos, posing unprecedented challenges to digital content forensics. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on either face-swapping or global text-to-video synthesis, overlooking the crucial dimensions of human-object or human-human interactions and multi-modal alignment. To address these limitations, we introduce HumanForge, a unified, large-scale, and multi-paradigm human-centric video forgery dataset. To construct and annotate this dataset without labor-intensive manual labeling or hallucinated monolithic prompts, we propose Gen2Anno, a modular active multi-agent pipeline built on LangGraph. Gen2Anno coordinates six specialized agents-ranging from source profiling to MoE-based reference analysis and closed-loop forensic verification-to generate over 18K high-fidelity video segments and produce structured, contrastive omni-annotations containing binary decisions, fine-grained artifact categories, and spatio-temporal localization. Extensive benchmarks using state-of-the-art traditional detectors and Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) demonstrate the significant challenges of zero-shot generalization and fine-grained reasoning on HumanForge. Code and dataset will be publicly released.

</details>


### [63] [LTM: Large-scale Terrain Model for Wildfire-prone Landscapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08711)
*Xiao Fu,Yue Hu,Meida Chen,Peter Anthony Beerel,Barath Raghavan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multi-modal reconstruction framework that uses outdated Digital Elevation Models as geometric priors with physics-based pixel-pixel alignment to produce high-fidelity depth maps in real-time for wildfire-prone terrain, outperforming existing methods in accuracy and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wildfire-prone regions require accurate 3D terrain maps for emergency response, but existing methods are either too expensive (airborne LiDAR) or struggle with sparse visual features and limited image overlap (image-based methods).

Method: A multi-modal reconstruction framework that leverages outdated DEMs as geometric priors, using physics-based pixel-pixel alignment between images and DEM data to eliminate expensive feature matching procedures, evaluated on a large-terrain simulator based on a real wildfire-prone area.

Result: Produces high-fidelity depth maps with real-time performance, showing significant improvements in reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency over existing techniques.

Conclusion: Offers a scalable solution for wildfire response by combining low-cost image-based methods with legacy DEM data, achieving both accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Accurate 3D terrain maps are essential for emergency response when assessing wildfire hazards. However, wildfire-prone regions often span vast areas where conventional reconstruction methods underperform. Airborne LiDAR systems provide high-resolution terrain data, but they are expensive and infrequently updated. Image-based methods offer a lower-cost alternative, but struggle due to sparse visual features and limited image overlap. We propose a multi-modal reconstruction framework leveraging outdated Digital Elevation Models (DEMs) as geometric priors for image-based 3D reconstruction. Our key innovation is physics-based pixel-pixel alignment between images and DEM data, dramatically reducing computational complexity by eliminating expensive feature matching procedures. To validate our approach, we developed a large-terrain simulator based on a real wildfire-prone area, generating realistic images enabling a comprehensive evaluation. Given posed images and legacy DEMs, our method produces high-fidelity depth maps while maintaining real-time performance. We find significant improvements in reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency over existing techniques, offering a scalable solution for wildfire response.

</details>


### [64] [Pose-to-Biomechanics: Bridging 3D Human Pose Estimation and Biomechanical Attribute Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08725)
*Ayda Eghbalian,Kevin Desai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BioModule is a lightweight plug-in temporal transformer that attaches to any 3D pose estimator to predict biomechanical attributes (e.g., joint loads, muscle activations) from standard 17-joint 3D skeletons, using a newly aligned dataset pairing Human3.6M keypoints with Human3.6Mplus biomechanical labels, without modifying the upstream pose model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3D pose estimators are optimized for geometric keypoint accuracy but fail to provide biomechanical quantities (e.g., how the body moves, loads, and activates) needed for rehabilitation, sports science, ergonomics, and clinical movement analysis. There is a gap between pose estimation and biomechanically meaningful motion analysis.

Method: BioModule is a lightweight plug-in temporal transformer that operates downstream of any 3D pose estimator, taking standard 17-joint 3D skeletons as input. It is estimator-agnostic and requires no upstream model modification. A large-scale aligned dataset was constructed by pairing Human3.6M video/3D keypoints with Human3.6Mplus biomechanical labels, with verified anatomical correspondence for frame-accurate cross-modal supervision.

Result: BioModule was benchmarked across seven state-of-the-art 3D pose estimators, providing the first systematic analysis of how upstream pose estimation quality propagates to downstream biomechanical prediction fidelity.

Conclusion: BioModule serves as a compact, modular bridge between vision-based pose estimation and biomechanically meaningful human motion analysis, enabling existing pose estimators to be extended toward physically interpretable motion analysis without modification.

Abstract: Recent progress in 3D human pose estimation has made markerless recovery of skeletal motion increasingly accurate and scalable. However, most pose estimators remain optimized for geometric keypoint accuracy, while many real-world applications in rehabilitation, sports science, ergonomics, and clinical movement analysis require biomechanical quantities that describe how the body moves, loads, and activates. In this work, we propose BioModule, a lightweight plug-in temporal transformer that attaches downstream of any 3D pose estimator and predicts biomechanical attributes from standard 17-joint 3D skeletons. BioModule is estimator-agnostic and requires no modification of the upstream pose model, enabling existing pose estimators to be extended toward physically interpretable motion analysis.
  To train and evaluate BioModule, we construct a large-scale aligned dataset pairing Human3.6M video and 3D keypoints with the biomechanical label space of Human3.6Mplus. We establish and verify anatomical correspondence between coordinate systems of the two datasets, enabling frame-accurate cross-modal supervision. Using this aligned supervision, BioModule predicts biomechanical quantities. We further benchmark BioModule across seven state-of-the-art 3D pose estimators, providing the first systematic analysis of how upstream pose estimation quality propagates to downstream biomechanical prediction fidelity. The results position BioModule as a compact, modular bridge between vision-based pose estimation and biomechanically meaningful human motion analysis.

</details>


### [65] [WaspMOT: A Benchmark for Long-Term Multi-Object Tracking of Trichogramma Wasps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08729)
*Tomasz Stanczyk,Yuan Gao,Hardik Agarwal,Seongroo Yoon,Tiantao Zhang,Vincent Calcagno,Francois Bremond*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WaspMOT is a new benchmark for long-duration multi-object tracking using Trichogramma wasps, showing that current tracking methods suffer significant trajectory fragmentation over extended sequences, with a simple spatial stitching baseline consistently improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MOT benchmarks are dominated by short video sequences that do not adequately evaluate long-term identity preservation. The authors introduce WaspMOT to address this gap, focusing on consistent identity assignment across thousands of frames.

Method: The benchmark includes 10 sequences of ~12,000 frames each (over 8 minutes at 25 FPS) of Trichogramma wasps, with dense MOTChallenge annotations and oracle detections. Five tracking-by-detection methods (ByteTrack, BoT-SORT, C-BIoU, OC-SORT, McByte) were evaluated under a unified protocol, along with a simple spatial tracklet stitching baseline.

Result: All evaluated methods exhibit significant trajectory fragmentation, demonstrating difficulty in long-term identity preservation even with perfect (oracle) detections. The spatial tracklet stitching baseline consistently improves performance across methods.

Conclusion: WaspMOT reveals limitations of current tracking approaches that are not observable on conventional short-video benchmarks, and provides a new resource for studying long-term association in multi-object tracking.

Abstract: Multi-object tracking (MOT) has achieved strong performance on benchmarks dominated by short video sequences. However, such datasets do not adequately evaluate long-term identity preservation, where objects must be tracked consistently over extended durations. We introduce WaspMOT, a benchmark designed to address this gap through long-duration tracking of Trichogramma wasps in controlled ecological experiments. The dataset contains 10 sequences of approximately 12,000 frames each (over 8 minutes at 25 FPS), with dense MOTChallenge annotations and oracle detections to isolate association performance.
  Unlike existing benchmarks, WaspMOT forms a closed-set tracking scenario where all individuals remain present throughout the sequence, requiring consistent identity assignment across thousands of frames despite abrupt jumps, occlusions, and highly similar appearance. We establish a benchmark by evaluating five tracking-by-detection methods, including ByteTrack, BoT-SORT, C-BIoU, OC-SORT, and McByte, under a unified protocol. Results show that all methods suffer from significant trajectory fragmentation, highlighting the difficulty of long-term identity preservation even with perfect detections. A simple spatial tracklet stitching baseline consistently improves performance, indicating that substantial gains remain possible.
  WaspMOT provides a new benchmark for studying long-term association and reveals limitations of current tracking approaches that are not observable on conventional datasets. The benchmark will be made publicly available at the project repository: https://github.com/tstanczyk95/WaspMOT/ .

</details>


### [66] [OpenCoF: Learning to Reason Through Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08763)
*Xinyan Chen,Ziyu Guo,Renrui Zhang,Dongzhi Jiang,Hongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OpenCoF introduces a framework for Chain-of-Frame (CoF) reasoning in video generation, including the OpenCoF-17K dataset spanning 11 task families and Wan-CoF, a fine-tuned video model. Wan-CoF outperforms the Wan2.2-I2V-A14B baseline across four benchmarks, and adding explicit visual and textual reasoning tokens further improves spatial-temporal reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video generation models are trained on general video corpora and lack diverse supervision and dedicated designs for Chain-of-Frame (CoF) reasoning, where reasoning unfolds across temporally connected frames, offering a distinct reasoning path from Chain-of-Thought (CoT).

Method: The authors create the OpenCoF-17K dataset (11 task families) for CoF reasoning, fine-tune Wan2.2-I2V-A14B to produce Wan-CoF, and further equip it with visual and textual reasoning tokens to capture low-level visual cues and high-level semantic priors for enhanced spatial-temporal reasoning.

Result: Wan-CoF achieves considerable gains over the Wan2.2-I2V-A14B baseline across four video reasoning benchmarks. Analysis reveals that explicit reasoning tokens contribute differently across model depth, denoising steps, space, and time.

Conclusion: Stronger video reasoning requires both broad temporal supervision (via diverse CoF datasets) and explicit mechanisms (visual/textual tokens) for organizing intermediate reasoning states. The dataset, model, and code are open-sourced to facilitate future research.

Abstract: Reasoning has become a core capability for large models, especially when reliable decisions require understanding logical consequences. Recent video generation models offer a reasoning path distinct from previous Chain-of-Thought (CoT): reasoning can unfold through temporally connected frames, known as Chain-of-Frame (CoF) reasoning. However, existing video generators are primarily trained on general video corpora, still lacking diverse supervision and dedicated designs for CoF reasoning. To address this gap, we introduce OpenCoF, a framework comprising the OpenCoF-17K dataset, a reasoning video dataset spanning 11 task families, and Wan-CoF, a fine-tuned video model for studying whether diverse temporal supervision improves CoF behavior. Across four video reasoning benchmarks, Wan-CoF achieves considerable gains over the Wan2.2-I2V-A14B baseline. Building on this, we empirically explore more advanced designs for CoF capabilities, i.e., equipping the model with visual and textual reasoning tokens. This mechanism respectively captures low-level visual cues and high-level semantic priors for spatial and temporal reasoning. Through performance comparisons and attention analysis, we examine how these tokens contribute across model depth, denoising steps, space, and time. Our results suggest that stronger video reasoning requires both broad temporal supervision and explicit mechanisms for organizing intermediate reasoning state. We open-source the dataset, model, and code to facilitate future research on reasoning-oriented video generation.

</details>


### [67] [Enhancing In-context Panoramic Generation via Geometric-aware Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08765)
*Haoran Feng,Ruiyang Zhang,Longyi Zhang,Dizhe Zhang,Lu Qi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Canvas360 is a two-stage framework for in-context panoramic generation with geometry-aware pretraining and task-specific fine-tuning, supported by a 1M-pair dataset (Canvas360Dataset) and techniques like parallel depth generation, velocity circular padding, and similarity loss regularization, achieving strong panoramic fidelity and competitive results across multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of large-scale, high-quality training data tailored to in-context panoramic tasks (style transfer, inpainting, outpainting, editing). The authors aim to address this gap and improve geometry-aware panoramic generation.

Method: Canvas360 uses a two-stage framework: (1) geometry-aware pretraining with parallel depth generation, velocity circular padding, and similarity loss regularization; (2) downstream task-specific fine-tuning via token-level concatenation in a unified in-context generation framework. A 1M-pair panoramic dataset (Canvas360Dataset) is curated for supervision.

Result: Canvas360 improves panoramic image fidelity, achieves strong performance on the panorama-specific FAED metric, and delivers competitive or leading results across quantitative evaluations on diverse in-context generation tasks.

Conclusion: Canvas360 surpasses prior methods in both task coverage and modeling flexibility for in-context panoramic generation, demonstrating the effectiveness of combining geometry-aware pretraining with a unified downstream framework.

Abstract: In this work, we present Canvas360, a two-stage framework for in-context panoramic generation that combines geometry-aware pretraining with downstream task-specific fine-tuning. To address the lack of large-scale, high-quality training data tailored to in-context panoramic tasks, we propose Canvas360Dataset, a collection of 1M high-quality paired panoramic samples for style transfer, inpainting, outpainting, and editing, enabling effective supervision across diverse in-context generation scenarios. On the modeling side, Canvas360 enhances text-to-panorama generation through parallel depth generation, velocity circular padding, and similarity loss regularization, enabling the model to learn geometry-aware representations, capture object distortion details, and improve geometric consistency and global coherence. Furthermore, empowered by strong panoramic priors, Canvas360 enables a unified in-context panoramic generation framework that supports diverse downstream tasks via token-level concatenation, surpassing prior methods in both task coverage and modeling flexibility. Extensive experiments show that Canvas360 improves panoramic image fidelity, achieving particularly strong performance on the panorama-specific FAED metric and competitive or leading results across the reported quantitative evaluations. More information can be found on our project page: https://zry000.github.io/Canvas360/

</details>


### [68] [OPSD-V: On-Policy Self-Distillation for Post-Training Few-Step Autoregressive Video Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08766)
*Hongyu Liu,Chun Wang,Feng Gao,Xuanhua He,Yue Ma,Ziyu Wan,Yong Zhang,Xiaoming Wei,Qifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OPSD-V is an on-policy self-distillation method that uses real long-video temporal context to provide dense trajectory-level supervision, reducing error accumulation and motion degradation in few-step autoregressive video diffusion models without altering inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing few-step AR video generators suffer from error accumulation and weakened motion dynamics during long autoregressive rollout despite producing long videos with low latency.

Method: Introduces real long-video data as temporal context; student generates chunks conditioned on its own KV cache while teacher uses cleaner AR-consistent temporal cache with real-video context, providing dense denoising-level corrective targets under on-policy AR cache dynamics.

Result: Consistent improvements in visual quality, motion dynamics, and VBenchLong scores; user study shows 66.0% overall preference (82.5% excluding ties) over base models (Self-Forcing and LongLive).

Conclusion: OPSD-V effectively reduces long-horizon degradation in few-step AR video models without changing the sampler, denoising steps, or inference-time cache mechanism.

Abstract: We propose OPSD-V, an on-policy self-distillation paradigm for post-training few-step autoregressive (AR) video diffusion models. Existing few-step AR video generators can produce long videos with low latency, but still suffer from error accumulation and weakened motion dynamics during long autoregressive rollout. OPSD-V reduces long-horizon degradation while preserving the original few-step inference path. The key idea is to introduce real long-video data as temporal context during training and use it to provide dense trajectory-level supervision. Specifically, the student follows the exact inference-time rollout, generating each chunk conditioned on its own previously generated KV cache. In parallel, the teacher is evaluated at the same student-visited denoising states, but uses a cleaner AR-consistent temporal cache in which older history can be replaced by real-video context. This provides dense denoising-level corrective targets under on-policy AR cache dynamics, without changing the sampler, number of denoising steps, or inference-time cache mechanism. We apply OPSD-V to representative few-step AR video models, including Self-Forcing and LongLive. Experiments show consistent improvements in visual quality, motion dynamics, and VBenchLong scores. A user study with 10 participants comparing 20 video pairs shows that OPSD-V is preferred over the base models in 66.0% of overall-preference judgments (82.5% excluding ties).

</details>


### [69] [Geometry and Gradient-based Partitioning for Panoramic Outdoor Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08769)
*Weijian Chen,Weibo Yao,Yuhang Zhang,Xiaolin Tang,Guo Wang,Weijun Zhang,Xitong Gao,Yihao Chen,Hongde Qin,Lu Qi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PanoLOG is a coarse-to-fine framework for large-scale outdoor scene reconstruction from panoramic images using 3D Gaussian Splatting. It introduces a Geometry and Gradient-based Partitioning Strategy (G²PS) to enable block-wise parallel training, plus a new benchmark dataset Pano360.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling 3DGS to large outdoor scenes is costly in data acquisition and computation. Panoramic images reduce capture effort but their 360° field of view makes existing local-frustum-based partitioning strategies fail, causing global training which is inefficient.

Method: Two-stage coarse-to-fine framework: (1) Global coarse stage uses sky-sphere modeling and panoramic monocular depth supervision for reliable geometry; (2) Refinement stage uses G²PS (Geometry and Gradient-based Partitioning Strategy) which builds adaptive bounding volumes via parallax-driven uncertainty and assigns cameras via gradient-based importance scoring.

Result: G²PS achieves state-of-the-art rendering quality while maintaining scalable, block-parallel training. The Pano360 benchmark is introduced as the first large-scale panoramic dataset for outdoor scene reconstruction.

Conclusion: The proposed method enables efficient block-parallel training for panoramic 3DGS reconstruction in large outdoor scenes, overcoming the omnipresent visibility challenge that invalidated previous partitioning strategies.

Abstract: Scaling 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) to large outdoor scenes is costly in both data acquisition and computation. Adopting panoramic images with equirectangular projection (ERP) can reduce capture effort via their full $360^{\circ}$ field of view, yet the resulting omnipresent visibility invalidates existing partitioning strategies that rely on local camera frustums, causing block-wise optimization to degenerate into global training. Thus, we propose PanoLOG, a two-stage coarse-to-fine framework equipped with a Geometry and Gradient-based Partitioning Strategy tailored for large-scale panoramic 3DGS reconstruction. In the global coarse stage, PanoLOG leverages sky-sphere modeling and panoramic monocular depth supervision for reliable geometry, while in the refinement stage, G$^2$PS builds adaptive bounding volumes via parallax-driven uncertainty and assigns cameras via gradient-based importance scoring. Furthermore, we construct Pano360, the first benchmark on large-scale panoramic dataset for outdoor scene reconstruction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that G$^2$PS achieves state-of-the-art rendering quality while maintaining scalable, block-parallel training. Our models, training code, and dataset are publicly available.

</details>


### [70] [LongE2V: Long-Horizon Event-based Video Reconstruction, Prediction, and Frame Interpolation with Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08770)
*Cheng-De Fan,Chun-Wei Tuan Mu,Chen-Wei Chang,Chin-Yang Lin,Kun-Ru Wu,Yu-Chee Tseng,Yu-Lun Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LongE2V leverages pre-trained video diffusion priors with autoregressive unrolling, adaptive context switching, and reencoding alignment to achieve high-quality event-based video reconstruction, prediction, and frame interpolation with superior temporal coherence and zero-shot generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering high-quality video from sparse event streams is challenging; regression methods blur textures and existing generative models lack long-term stability.

Method: Fine-tunes a foundational video diffusion model with Autoregressive Unrolling and Adaptive Context Switching to mitigate temporal drift, Reencoding Alignment with Cross Residual Correction for bidirectional consistency, and Event Voxel Density Augmentation for robustness across sensor resolutions.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art methods on real-world benchmarks across video reconstruction, prediction, and frame interpolation, with exceptional temporal coherence and zero-shot generalization.

Conclusion: LongE2V demonstrates that leveraging pre-trained video diffusion priors with tailored temporal stabilization techniques yields high-quality, temporally coherent event-based video processing.

Abstract: Recovering high-quality video from sparse event streams is a challenging task. Regression methods often blur textures, while existing generative models struggle with long-term stability. We propose LongE2V, a novel approach that leverages pre-trained video diffusion priors to jointly handle event-based video reconstruction, prediction, and frame interpolation. By fine-tuning a foundational video model, our approach achieves high data efficiency and superior perceptual quality. We introduce Autoregressive Unrolling and Adaptive Context Switching to mitigate temporal drift in extremely long sequences. We also propose Reencoding Alignment with Cross Residual Correction to ensure precise bidirectional consistency during frame interpolation. Furthermore, Event Voxel Density Augmentation ensures robustness across varying sensor resolutions. Extensive experiments on real-world benchmarks demonstrate that LongE2V outperforms state-of-the-art methods across all three tasks, exhibiting exceptional temporal coherence and zero-shot generalization. Project page: https://cdfan0627.github.io/LongE2V-page/

</details>


### [71] [ZipDepth: Bringing Lightweight Zero-Shot Monocular Depth Anywhere, on Any Device](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08771)
*Fabio Tosi,Luca Bartolomei,Matteo Poggi,Stefano Mattoccia*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ZipDepth is a 6.1M-parameter monocular depth network combining reparameterizable encoder-decoder with knowledge distillation from a foundation model, achieving real-time performance and competitive zero-shot accuracy across diverse benchmarks, bridging the gap between lightweight efficiency and foundation-model-level generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing monocular depth foundation models offer strong zero-shot generalization but are computationally prohibitive for embedded/mobile platforms, while lightweight alternatives are limited to single-domain self-supervised paradigms and fail under domain shift.

Method: ZipDepth uses an efficient reparameterizable encoder-decoder architecture trained via large-scale knowledge distillation from a foundation model over a multi-domain dataset.

Result: With only 6.1M parameters, ZipDepth achieves real-time inference across diverse hardware and delivers the best accuracy-efficiency trade-off among lightweight models on five benchmarks, approaching the accuracy of foundation models with 50x more parameters.

Conclusion: ZipDepth demonstrates that compact models can significantly close the gap to foundation model accuracy through efficient architecture design and multi-domain distillation, enabling robust zero-shot depth estimation on resource-constrained platforms.

Abstract: Monocular depth estimation has seen remarkable progress through foundation models achieving robust zero-shot generalization, yet their computational demands place them far beyond the reach of embedded and mobile platforms. Lightweight alternatives exist, but have been developed almost exclusively within single-domain, self-supervised paradigms, failing silently under domain shift. We present ZipDepth, a compact monocular depth network that bridges this gap by combining an efficient reparameterizable encoder-decoder with large-scale knowledge distillation from a foundation model over a large multi-domain training set. Comprising just 6.1M parameters, ZipDepth runs at real-time rates from server GPUs to power-constrained devices, achieving the best trade-off between zero-shot accuracy and deployment efficiency among lightweight models across five benchmarks, taking a significant step towards the accuracy of foundation models with 50x more parameters.

</details>


### [72] [Wat3R: Underwater 3D Geometry Learning without Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08772)
*Jiangwei Ren,Xingyu Jiang,Zijie Song,Wei Xu,Hongkai Lin,Dingkang Liang,Xiang Bai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Wat3R is a cross-domain semi-supervised learning framework that adapts feed-forward 3D reconstruction models from air to underwater scenes without requiring any annotated underwater data, using a teacher-student architecture on unlabeled real underwater video and a cross-view consistency loss, outperforming SOTA methods on a new Water3D benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater 3D geometry estimation is challenging due to light attenuation, scattering, and lack of large-scale high-quality 3D annotations; existing methods require massive dense annotations impractical in underwater settings.

Method: Proposes Wat3R, a cross-domain semi-supervised learning framework with a teacher-student architecture that adapts air-trained feed-forward 3D reconstruction models to underwater scenes using only unlabeled real underwater video footage, plus a cross-view consistency loss to handle information degradation from water attenuation and scattering.

Result: Wat3R outperforms current state-of-the-art methods in underwater multi-view depth estimation and point cloud reconstruction, validated on the constructed Water3D dataset.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively bridges the air-to-water domain gap for 3D reconstruction without underwater annotations, and the Water3D dataset provides a comprehensive benchmark for future underwater geometric tasks.

Abstract: Estimating 3D geometry in underwater environments presents unique challenges due to light attenuation, scattering, and the absence of large-scale, high-quality 3D annotations. Pioneering methods rely on massive dense annotations that are impractical in underwater settings. In this paper, we propose Wat3R, a cross-domain semi-supervised learning framework designed to adapt feed-forward 3D reconstruction models from air to underwater scenes. Uniquely, our method eliminates the need for any annotated underwater data following a teacher-student architecture, that learns robust geometry representations merely on abundant unlabeled real underwater video footage. We also design a cross-view consistency loss that leverages geometric cues from other views to compensate for the information degradation in the current view caused by water attenuation and scattering. Furthermore, considering the lack of comprehensive evaluation benchmarks, we construct Water3D, a diverse dataset covering various water bodies and underwater scenarios, designed for geometric task evaluation. Experimental results demonstrate that Wat3R outperforms current state-of-the-art methods in underwater multi-view depth estimation and point cloud reconstruction. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/LSXI7/Wat3R .

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [73] [REFORGE: A Method for Benchmarking LLMs' Reverse Engineering Capabilities in Decompiled Binary Function Naming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07738)
*Nicolas Koller,Andreas u. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Reforge is a provenance-tracked pipeline for constructing reliable function-level ground truth in LLM-assisted binary analysis. It reveals that compiler optimization causes significant alignment uncertainty (high-confidence yield drops from 87.2% to 65.9%), and that unpaired comparisons overstate performance decay via survivorship bias. Evaluation of seven LLMs on function naming demonstrates the need for uncertainty-aware benchmarking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary analysis treat function-level ground truth construction as a solved pre-processing step and fail to account for alignment reliability under compiler optimization. Claims about LLM capability in reverse-engineering outpace the ability to measure it fairly.

Method: Reforge constructs function-level ground truth via a provenance-tracked pipeline: C source compilation, DWARF/syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation. It operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification.

Result: High-confidence yield drops from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels. Unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay due to survivorship bias.

Conclusion: The principal obstacle to fair evaluation is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. Uncertainty-aware benchmarking is necessary for fair assessment of LLMs in reverse-engineering tasks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to reverse-engineering tasks, and recent threat-intelligence reporting shows them operating inside live offensive-security workflows. Claims about their capability, however, outpace our ability to measure it. Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary analysis treat the construction of function-level ground truth as a solved pre-processing step and report accuracy without disclosing how many functions were reliably evaluable. We argue that the principal obstacle to fair evaluation is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. This paper presents Reforge, a provenance-tracked pipeline that constructs function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation, and that operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification. On a controlled micro-benchmark, high-confidence yield falls from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels, and unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay through survivorship bias. A proof-of-concept evaluation of seven contemporary LLMs on function naming demonstrates the substrate and motivates uncertainty-aware benchmarking practice

</details>


### [74] [PERFOPT-Bench: Evaluating Coding Agents on Software Performance Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07744)
*Yingyun Cui,Yi Xie,Piaohong Wang,Jiawei Ma,Bo Liu,Liangliang Cao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PERFOPT-Bench is a new benchmark for evaluating the full performance-engineering loop of coding agents, covering profiling, diagnosing, editing, and verifying performance optimizations. Experiments on 7 agent stacks and 7 tasks show optimization performance is workload-dependent and framework matters as much as model identity; raw speedup alone is unsafe as a metric due to shortcut exploitation, and a relay restart strategy can recover additional gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding-agent benchmarks focus on functional correctness, but production software requires measurable speedups on real execution targets. Performance optimization is a distinct agentic task involving profiling, cross-layer diagnosis, correctness-preserving edits, and verification of reproducible gains—none of which are captured by current benchmarks.

Method: The authors introduce PERFOPT-Bench, where each task provides a correct but suboptimal codebase and asks the agent to improve a target metric. Scoring combines hidden correctness tests, verified-speedup measurement, and trajectory-level audit. They evaluate 7 agent stacks (varying LLMs and frameworks) on 7 long-horizon tasks, and also test a relay pilot where agents restart from an externalized optimization summary.

Result: No single agent stack dominates; performance is workload-dependent. Changing the agent framework materially changes the same LLM's speedup profile. Raw speedup is unsafe as a metric because large gains sometimes come from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation. The relay restart strategy can recover additional optimization headroom after an initial session stops.

Conclusion: PERFOPT-Bench fills a gap in evaluating the full performance-engineering loop. The findings highlight that optimization is workload-dependent, framework choice matters, raw speedup metrics are unreliable without audit, and multi-session relay strategies may improve outcomes.

Abstract: Coding-agent benchmarks have largely measured whether agents can produce functionally correct patches, but production software also demands measurable speedups on real execution targets. Performance optimization is a distinct agentic task: agents must profile executions, diagnose cross-layer bottlenecks, edit code without breaking correctness, and verify that gains are reproducible rather than measurement artifacts. We introduce PERFOPT-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this full performance-engineering loop. Each task provides a correct but deliberately suboptimal codebase and asks the agent to improve a target performance metric; scoring requires hidden correctness tests, verified-speedup measurement, and trajectory-level audit. We evaluate 7 agent stacks with different LLMs and agent frameworks on 7 long-horizon optimization tasks. The results show that optimization performance is workload-dependent rather than determined by model identity alone: no single stack dominates, and changing the agent framework can materially change the same LLM's per-task speedup profile. We further find that raw speedup is unsafe as a benchmark score, since some large gains arise from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation; an exploratory relay pilot suggests that restarting from an externalized optimization summary can recover additional headroom after an initial session stops. The benchmark and our evaluation are available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Dataset-D3CC.

</details>


### [75] [Functional and Secure Code Generation with Task Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07881)
*Felix Wang,Anudeep Das,Mei Nagappan,N. Asokan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SecVecCoder uses task-vector arithmetic on LLM weights to improve secure and functional code generation without post-generation fixes, achieving 2.1-36.0 percentage point improvements in trustworthy code completions across six coding LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle to generate code that is both functional AND secure; prior work evaluates these separately or only detects vulnerabilities post-generation, lacking an integrated, efficient solution.

Method: Apply task-vector arithmetic (linear operations on LLM weights) to simultaneously enhance both functionality and security alignment, requiring no method-specific decoding or post-generation adjustments.

Result: Across six coding LLMs from three families on CodeGuard+, SecVecCoder improves trustworthy code completions by 2.1-36.0 percentage points over base models, with gains on unseen CWE types up to 39.1 percentage points; decoding latency is within 0.6% of the base model.

Conclusion: Task-vector-based weight modification is an efficient and effective approach for producing code that is both functional and secure, eliminating the need for post-generation vulnerability fixes.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for code generation, but they struggle to generate functional code free of security vulnerabilities. Prior work to improve the secure code generation abilities of such coding LLMs has largely focused on evaluating code functionality and security separately using different datasets, or focused on finding vulnerabilities post-generation. At the same time, the text-generation domain has seen significant work on alignment techniques, where models are tuned such that their outputs exhibit certain qualities (e.g., helpfulness, harmlessness). Of particular interest is task-vector arithmetic, where linear operations on LLM weights can be used to arbitrarily enhance alignment while incurring only minimal computational overhead. We develop a novel method, SecVecCoder, leveraging task vectors to produce trustworthy code that is simultaneously functional and secure without the need for post-generation adjustment. Across six coding LLMs from three families on the CodeGuard+ benchmark, SecVecCoder improves the rate of trustworthy code completions by 2.1-36.0 percentage points over the base model, with improvements on unseen CWE types reaching up to 39.1 percentage points. Since the effectiveness of the coding LLM relies only on changing the model weights, SecVecCoder requires no method-specific decoding and hence achieves a decoding latency within 0.6% of the base model's, on average.

</details>


### [76] [Bug Report Specification Refinement with Trajectory Guidance for Automated Program Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07882)
*S M Farah Al Fahim,Md Nakhla Rafi,Md Ahasanuzzaman,Zeyang Ma,Dong Jae Kim,Shaowei Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TrajSpec is a trajectory-guided approach for refining bug reports in repository-level automated program repair. It uses a trajectory-collection agent to gather unverified execution traces, organizes them into a three-level hierarchical evidence representation (high-level interpretation, diagnostic findings, concrete observations), applies repository-based review to produce refined reports, and consistently improves repair performance across multiple APR agents (GPT-5-mini: 41.00%→59.67%, MiniMax M2.5: 54.67%→64.33%, Agentless: 41.00%→71.00%, AutoCodeRover: 47.00%→72.00% on SWE-Bench Lite).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bug reports used for repository-level APR often omit repair-relevant information (failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirement, implementation scope), causing repair agents to inspect irrelevant code, infer incorrect requirements, or generate patches that only address symptoms without restoring intended repository behavior.

Method: TrajSpec runs a trajectory-collection agent on the original bug report and pre-fix repository to obtain an unverified execution trajectory. It extracts three-level evidence (high-level issue interpretation, diagnostic findings, concrete repository observations), generates a draft refined report, and applies repository-based review to remove unsupported claims, revise uncertain ones, and add repository-supported details.

Result: On all 300 SWE-Bench Lite instances: GPT-5-mini Pass@1 improves from 41.00% to 59.67%; MiniMax M2.5 from 54.67% to 64.33%. On a stratified 100-instance sample: Agentless from 41.00% to 71.00%; AutoCodeRover from 47.00% to 72.00%. Ablation removes repository-based review or hierarchical evidence representation drops Pass@1 from 59.67% to 48.00% and 47.67%.

Conclusion: TrajSpec provides actionable repository-supported context that consistently improves repair performance across different APR agents, with both repository-based review and hierarchical evidence representation being critical components.

Abstract: Bug reports serve as task specifications for repository-level automated program repair (APR) agents, but they often describe only the observed failure and omit repair-relevant information such as the failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirement, and implementation scope. As a result, a repair agent may inspect irrelevant code, infer an incorrect requirement, or generate a patch that addresses the reported symptom without restoring the intended repository behavior. We present TrajSpec, a trajectory-guided approach for repository-supported bug report specification refinement. Given an original report and a pre-fix repository, TrajSpec runs a trajectory-collection agent and uses the resulting unverified trajectory as a source of trajectory-derived specification evidence. It organizes this evidence into a three-level representation consisting of a high-level interpretation of the issue, diagnostic findings supporting that interpretation, and concrete repository observations. TrajSpec then generates a draft refined report and applies repository-based review to remove unsupported claims, revise uncertain claims, and add repository-supported details. We evaluate TrajSpec on all 300 SWE-Bench Lite instances using Mini-SWE-Agent V2. TrajSpec's refined reports improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 59.67% with GPT-5-mini and from 54.67% to 64.33% with MiniMax M2.5. On a stratified sample of 100 instances, TrajSpec's refined reports also improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 71.00% with Agentless and from 47.00% to 72.00% with AutoCodeRover. Ablation results show that removing repository-based review or the hierarchical evidence representation reduces Pass@1 from 59.67% to 48.00% and 47.67%, respectively. Overall, TrajSpec provides actionable repository-supported context that consistently improves repair performance.

</details>


### [77] [The Memory Wall of Green Software: Empirical Energy Evaluation of Memento Design Pattern](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07944)
*Imane Jriri,Tarik Houichime,Younes El Amrani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper empirically investigates the energy efficiency of the Memento design pattern by comparing Classic full-snapshot and Differential delta-encoding strategies against a direct baseline. Using RAPL hardware telemetry across state volumes from 10 MB to 200 MB, the study reveals that the Differential strategy achieves up to 65.8% energy reduction for mid-scale states but hits a "memory wall" at 200 MB where GC thrashing and power spikes neutralize all optimizations, leading to evidence-based heuristics for Green Software Engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As energy efficiency becomes a mission-critical requirement in Green Software Engineering, the implicit "metabolic cost" of abstraction layers in software design patterns remains poorly understood. This study aims to empirically reveal the hidden energy dynamics of the Memento pattern to bridge the gap between structural design quality and sustainable computing.

Method: The authors conducted an empirical measurement study comparing three implementations: a direct unabstracted baseline, Classic full-snapshot Memento, and Differential delta-encoding Memento. Energy dissipation was quantified using Intel RAPL (Running Average Power Limit) interface for high-fidelity hardware telemetry, with state volumes ranging from 10 MB to 200 MB.

Result: The Differential strategy achieves up to 65.8% maximum energy reduction for mid-scale states by minimizing memory traffic. However, at the 200 MB saturation point, algorithmic optimizations are completely neutralized by severe garbage collection (GC) thrashing and non-linear power spikes, creating a "memory wall" where the energy benefits collapse.

Conclusion: The findings reveal a critical architectural trade-off: differential strategies are effective for mid-scale states but catastrophically fail at saturation points. The authors synthesize evidence-based heuristics to help architects reconcile structural design quality with sustainable Green IT imperatives.

Abstract: As Green Software Engineering matures, energy efficiency has transitioned into a mission-critical non-functional requirement. While software design patterns ensure structural integrity, their inherent abstraction layers impose an implicit "metabolic cost" that often remains obscured during the design phase. This paper empirically investigates the energy dynamics of the Memento design pattern, contrasting a direct, unabstracted baseline against Classic full-snapshot and Differential delta-encoding strategies. Leveraging the RAPL interface for high-fidelity hardware telemetry, we quantify energy dissipation across state volumes scaling from 10 MB to 200 MB. Our empirical results expose a critical architectural trade-off: the Differential strategy minimizes memory traffic, yielding a maximum energy reduction of 65.8% for mid-scale states, but collides with a catastrophic "memory wall" at 200 MB. At this saturation point, algorithmic optimizations are completely neutralized by severe GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes. We synthesize these findings into evidence-based heuristics, providing architects with a robust framework to reconcile structural design quality with sustainable Green IT imperatives.

</details>


### [78] [DeepSWE: Measuring Frontier Coding Agents on Original, Long-Horizon Engineering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07946)
*Wenqi Huang,Charley Lee,Leonard Tng,Serena Ge*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DeepSWE is a new benchmark of 113 original software engineering tasks designed to address two key flaws in existing agentic coding benchmarks (like SWE-bench): test contamination from public GitHub data and unreliable grading via merged-fix tests. Tasks are written from scratch across 91 open-source repos and 5 languages, kept out of public training data, and graded by hand-written verifiers that accept any valid implementation. The benchmark shows lower disagreement rates with LLM judges (1.4% vs 32.4%), touches 5.5x more code per reference solution despite shorter prompts, and better differentiates frontier agent performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks like SWE-bench suffer from two critical issues: (1) test contamination — tasks mined from merged GitHub PRs may have been seen during model pretraining, causing scores to reflect recall rather than true problem-solving; (2) unreliable grading — tests shipped with merged fixes are tailored to one specific solution, so they can falsely reject correct alternative implementations or accept incomplete ones.

Method: DeepSWE constructs 113 original, long-horizon software engineering tasks across 91 active open-source repositories and 5 languages. Tasks are written from scratch and never contributed back upstream, keeping reference solutions out of public training data. Each task is graded by a hand-written verifier that checks requested functionality and accepts any valid implementation. Evaluation includes independent LLM judge re-review of graded runs.

Result: DeepSWE's hand-written verifiers disagree with an independent LLM judge only 1.4% of the time, versus 32.4% for SWE-Bench Pro's inherited tests. Despite prompts being about half the length, reference solutions touch 5.5x more code. The benchmark better differentiates frontier agents across a wider score band compared to existing leaderboards where they cluster.

Conclusion: DeepSWE provides a more reliable and contamination-resistant benchmark for evaluating coding agents, with more accurate grading and better ability to distinguish between different agent capabilities. The benchmark, verifiers, and evaluation trajectories are released publicly.

Abstract: DeepSWE is a benchmark of 113 original, long-horizon software engineering tasks for evaluating coding agents. Most public agentic coding benchmarks follow SWE-bench in mining merged fixes from public GitHub repositories, which creates two problems: the fixes and their discussion were likely seen during pretraining, so a high score can reflect recall rather than problem-solving; and each task is graded by the tests that shipped with its merged fix, which were written to confirm one specific fix rather than grade an arbitrary solution, so they can fail a correct alternative or pass an incomplete one. DeepSWE avoids both.
