Its tasks are written from scratch across 91 active open-source repositories and five languages and are never contributed back upstream, so their reference solutions stay out of the public record that model training scrapes; and each task is graded by a hand-written verifier that checks the requested functionality and accepts any implementation that provides it. When an independent LLM judge re-reviews graded runs, it disagrees with DeepSWE's verifier about an order of magnitude less often than with SWE-Bench Pro's inherited tests (1.4% versus 32.4%). Despite being about half the length of SWE-Bench Pro's prompts, DeepSWE's prompts describe tasks whose reference solutions touch 5.5x more code, and the benchmark separates frontier agents across a wider score band than the leaderboards on which they otherwise cluster. We release the benchmark, its verifiers, and the full record of evaluation trajectories.

</details>


### [79] [3100 Opinions on Code Review in an AI World: Building Causal Theory from Practitioner Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07980)
*Shyam Agarwal,Courtney Miller,Christian Kästner,Bogdan Vasilescu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Agent-authored pull requests are reviewed less, merged faster, and discussed less than human-authored ones, but these trends reverse under different analysis choices. Through large-scale grey-literature analysis (38,709 documents), the authors build a causal model showing that code review is a control point where team expertise and process structure determine AI's effect on software quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners sharply disagree about whether coding agents make code review the bottleneck, whether human review remains necessary, and whether it erodes team understanding. Existing repository-mining studies measure surface trends without explaining mechanisms, and the trends themselves are unstable.

Method: Observational analysis of public GitHub activity; large-scale grey-literature collection (38,709 documents from engineering blogs and Reddit); stratified random sampling (3,100 documents) with LLM-assisted coding pipeline; causal model construction with 26 constructs and 67 relationships.

Result: Agent-authored PRs are reviewed less, merged several times faster, and discussed less, but these trends flip under different analysis choices. The causal theory identifies review as the control point where team expertise and process structure determine AI's effect. The model includes 26 constructs, 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested).

Conclusion: AI does not fix the sign of its effect on code review; the team sets it through human expertise and review process structure. The theory turns competing positions into falsifiable propositions. The LLM-assisted grey-literature method is offered as a scalable template for SE research.

Abstract: Coding agents now author entire pull requests, and practitioners sharply disagree about what this does to code review: whether it becomes the bottleneck, whether human review is still necessary, and whether it quietly erodes the understanding that it once built. Repository-mining studies measure surface trends but seldom explain the mechanisms beneath them, and the trends themselves prove unstable. A motivating observational analysis of public GITHUB activity finds that agent-authored pull requests are reviewed less often, merged several times faster, and discussed less than human-authored ones, yet the direction of these trends flips under different but equally defensible analysis choices, so the traces establish what is changing without explaining why. To recover the mechanisms, we synthesize practitioner discourse at scale into an explanatory theory: we collect 38,709 grey-literature documents (engineering blogs and Reddit threads), filter to those substantively about code review, and code a stratified random sample of 3,100 with an LLM-assisted pipeline, from which we build a causal model of 26 constructs and 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested). Its organizing claim is that review is the control point through which a coding agent's effect on software is decided, and that AI does not fix the sign of that effect: the team sets it, through the expertise its humans bring and how it structures the review process. The theory makes the competing positions explicit and turns "AI is changing code review" into falsifiable propositions with named constructs and moderators. As a secondary contribution, we offer the underlying LLM-assisted, grey-literature theory-building method as a scalable template for software-engineering research, with a public implementation.

</details>


### [80] [On the Correctness of Software Merge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07987)
*Akira Mori,Masatomo Hashimoto*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a new structural merge tool that ensures merge results are both parsable (syntactically valid) and universal (incorporating all and only the edit operations from each branch without duplication), defined via category theory pushouts. Experiments on 43,774 Java merge scenarios show the tool produces no incorrect results, outperforming existing tools like Git's merge tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Three-way merge tools are critical in modern development workflows (e.g., pull requests), yet their results are often accepted without scrutiny. The paper aims to establish clear correctness criteria and develop a principled merge tool that avoids errors in existing tools.

Method: The authors propose two syntactic criteria: parsability (syntactically valid according to the programming language grammar) and universality (incorporates all and only edits from each branch, with common edits applied once). This is formalized using pushouts from category theory. A large-scale experiment was conducted on 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, comparing their structural merge tool with existing tools like Git's merge tool, and further evaluating against 2,582 developer-resolved merges and 2,459 merge scenarios involving 21 refactoring types.

Result: Existing tools (e.g., Git companion merge tool) produced a number of incorrect results, whereas the proposed structural merge tool reported none. Additional comparisons with developer-resolved merges and refactoring scenarios revealed both strengths and limitations of structural merge.

Conclusion: The proposed correctness criteria (parsable and universal) provide a foundation for developing more reliable and principled merge tools. The results underscore the importance of clear correctness criteria in merge tool evaluation.

Abstract: Three-way merge tools play crucial roles in modern software development, where a developer forks a branch to make local modifications and requests it to be merged into the main branch via a "pull request." Despite its importance, the task has traditionally been defined in an intuitive manner, and the results of merge tools are often accepted without scrutiny. In this paper, we present a new structural merge tool in comparison with existing tools based on the syntactic criteria we propose for evaluating the merge results. We require the merge result to be both parsable and universal. Being parsable means that the result is syntactically valid according to the grammar of the programming language. Being universal means that the result incorporates all and only the edit operations occurring in each branch while ensuring that edits common to both branches are applied only once. This requirement can be precisely defined using the notion of pushouts in category theory. In a large-scale experiment involving 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, we found a number of incorrect results reported by existing tools such as the Git companion merge tool, whereas our tool reports none. We further compared d3j's results with 2,582 developer-resolved merges and with 2,459 merge scenarios involving 21 refactoring types. These experiments revealed both the strengths and current limitations of structural merge, and underscore the importance of clear correctness criteria. We expect that the proposed criterion will provide a foundation for developing more reliable and principled merge tools.

</details>


### [81] [Aleena: Alignment Agent for Research Software Engineering Collaborations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08043)
*Kshitij Dani,Cordero Core,Landung Setiawan,Carlos Garcia Jurado Suarez,Anshul Tambay,Vani Mandava,Anant Mittal*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Aleena is an open-source agentic AI tool that uses GitHub as a shared surface to capture, structure, and preserve decision rationale from multi-modal stakeholder interactions (Slack, meetings, PRs) to maintain alignment and project-state continuity in research software engineering collaborations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research software collaborations involve fragmented communications across multiple modalities (chats, meetings, issues, PRs), causing loss of decision rationale and divergent mental models between domain researchers and research software engineers regarding project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions.

Method: The authors built Aleena, an open-source lifecycle alignment agent that ingests multi-modal stakeholder interactions from GitHub-linked surfaces, transforms them into structured project records, and surfaces risks, tracks open questions, and preserves decision continuity.

Result: The paper presents the system design, prototype implementation, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios demonstrating Aleena's capability to maintain alignment without replacing human decision-making.

Conclusion: Alignment in research software engineering is a continuous lifecycle problem, and agentic AI like Aleena can support stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking without replacing human judgment.

Abstract: Research software collaborations span meetings, informal chats, pull requests, and GitHub issues. A decision surfaced in a Slack thread, refined in a meeting, and implemented in a pull request can lose its original rationale across these artifacts, leaving domain researchers and research software engineers with divergent mental models of project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions. We argue that alignment in research software engineering is a continuous lifecycle problem, and that agentic AI can support stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking without replacing human decision-making. We present Aleena, an open-source lifecycle alignment agent that uses GitHub as a shared collaboration surface, transforming multi-modal stakeholder interactions into structured project records that surface risks, track open questions, and preserve decision continuity. Grounded in university-based research software engineering center experiences, this paper presents the motivating problem, system design, prototype, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios for Aleena.

</details>


### [82] [TTHE: Test-Time Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08124)
*Jun Nie,Yonggang Zhang,Jun Song,Qianshu Cai,Dahai Yu,Yike Guo,Xinmei Tian,Bo Han*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TTHE optimizes LLM agent harnesses (the executable control program) at test time through evolution over execution traces, without model weight updates, gold labels, or separate adaptation models, improving performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches freeze agent harnesses after pre-deployment search, limiting adaptation when test distributions, failure modes, or tool interactions differ from development-time scenarios.

Method: TTHE maintains a population of candidate harnesses during evaluation, uses an agentic proposer to reason over unlabeled execution traces, and a judge commits improved harnesses based on execution-derived proxy signals—all using the same frozen LLM in different roles.

Result: TTHE improves fixed ReAct-style baselines across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks.

Conclusion: Test-time adaptation for LLM agents can be recast as evolution over executable control programs, with execution-derived proxy reliability as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.

Abstract: The behavior of an LLM agent is determined not only by the underlying model, but also by its harness: the executable program that constructs context, invokes tools, verifies intermediate results, and recovers from failures. Existing approaches optimize such harnesses before deployment, searching training or development data for a fixed agent workflow that is then frozen at test time. This limits adaptation when the test distribution, failure modes, or tool interactions differ from those seen during development. We ask whether the harness can instead be optimized during evaluation itself, using only the unlabeled execution traces the agent produces on the test inputs. We introduce Test-Time Harness Evolution (TTHE), which treats the executable harness as the state of test-time adaptation. During evaluation, TTHE maintains a population of candidate harnesses and refines them through an agentic proposer that reasons over their execution traces, without gold labels or task-specific supervision; a judge then commits an improved harness from execution-derived proxy signals, and the selected program persists to govern subsequent inputs. Crucially, TTHE does not update model weights, require gold labels, or train a separate adaptation model: solver, proposers, and judge are different roles and harnesses around the same frozen LLM, so all adaptation occurs through changes to the surrounding program. Across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks, TTHE improves fixed ReAct-style baseline harnesses, yielding persistent, inspectable improvements rather than a pre-searched workflow or per-query retries. These results recast test-time adaptation for LLM agents as evolution over executable control programs and identify execution-derived proxy reliability as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.

</details>


### [83] [ProjAgent: Procedural Similarity Retrieval for Repository-Level Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08691)
*QiHong Chen,Aaron Imani,Iftekhar Ahmed*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ProjAgent introduces procedural similarity as a new retrieval signal for repository-level code generation, decomposing target functions into reasoning steps and using an agentic workflow to retrieve similar procedural code, achieving 41.14% Pass@1 on REPOCOD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval methods rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity, missing functions with similar procedural logic but different identifiers or domains.

Method: ProjAgent decomposes target functions into intermediate reasoning steps, uses an agentic workflow to retrieve procedurally similar functions, integrates with conventional semantic retrieval, and applies a conservative static-analysis feedback loop for iterative repair.

Result: 41.14% Pass@1 on REPOCOD, outperforming existing retrieval-based baselines.

Conclusion: Procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation.

Abstract: Repository-level code generation requires implementing target functions while accounting for complex cross-file dependencies and project-specific conventions. Existing retrieval methods predominantly rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity, often overlooking repository functions that implement similar procedural logic despite differing in identifiers or application domains. We propose ProjAgent, a repository-level code generation system that introduces procedural similarity as an explicit retrieval signal. ProjAgent decomposes the target function into intermediate reasoning steps and employs an agentic workflow to retrieve repository functions that exhibit similar procedural behavior at each step. The retrieved procedural context is integrated with conventional semantic retrieval to construct a richer repository context for code generation. ProjAgent further incorporates a conservative static-analysis feedback loop that iteratively repairs generated code using compiler and static-analysis feedback. Evaluated on REPOCOD, ProjAgent achieves 41.14% Pass@1, outperforming existing retrieval-based baselines. These results demonstrate that procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [84] [A Self-Supervised Approach for Minimal-Annotation Hydroacoustic Data Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07733)
*Pierre-Yves Raumer,Axel Marmoret,Dorian Cazau,Anatole Gros-Martial,Richard Dreo,Maelle Torterotot,Sara Bazin,Flore Samaran,Jean-Yves Royer*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A self-supervised pipeline using Masked AutoEncoder and clustering (MAE + UMAP + HDBSCAN) enables efficient exploration and classification of hydroacoustic signals from large unlabeled datasets, achieving comparable performance to supervised detectors and revealing known and novel acoustic patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive hydroacoustic monitoring generates massive continuous recordings that are underutilized due to high manual annotation costs. Supervised methods require large labeled datasets, which are often unavailable for rare signals or understudied environments.

Method: A Masked AutoEncoder (MAE) is pre-trained on a reconstruction pretext task to extract patch-level spectrogram representations. Adjacent informative patches are aggregated into event-level embeddings to disentangle overlapping events. These embeddings are clustered at dataset scale using UMAP (dimension reduction) and HDBSCAN (clustering).

Result: Applied to a multi-year hydroacoustic dataset near Mayotte Island, the pipeline produced 317 clusters manually mapped to 15 classes or noise in <1 hour. Quantitatively, it matched two existing detectors' performance. Qualitatively, it recovered known seasonal marine mammal patterns and identified previously unstudied signals.

Conclusion: The self-supervised pipeline is a practical, scalable tool for exploring large unlabeled hydroacoustic datasets, reducing annotation effort while matching supervised detection performance and enabling discovery of novel acoustic events.

Abstract: Passive hydroacoustic monitoring often generates large volumes of continuous recordings that are only partially exploited due to the cost of manual annotation. Supervised detection methods perform well but require large labeled datasets, seldom available for rare signals or understudied environments. This work proposes a self-supervised exploration pipeline to address this limitation in low-frequency settings. A Masked AutoEncoder (MAE) is pre-trained on a reconstruction pretext task, then used to extract patch-level representations from spectrograms. Within each spectrogram, adjacent informative patches are aggregated into event-level embeddings, enabling the disentanglement of overlapping events. These embeddings are then clustered at the dataset scale using the dimension reduction algorithm UMAP and the clustering algorithm HDBSCAN to identify hydroacoustic patterns. The pipeline was applied to a multi-year hydroacoustic dataset collected near Mayotte Island, Indian Ocean, containing marine mammal vocalizations, seismo-volcanic signals, and anthropogenic noise. The 317 clusters were manually mapped to 15 hydroacoustic classes or noise in less than one hour. The method was evaluated in two ways. Quantitatively, when used as a classifier, it achieved performance comparable to two existing detectors. Qualitatively, it recovered known seasonal patterns of marine mammal acoustic activity. It also identified patterns of previously unstudied signals, thereby demonstrating its practical value.

</details>


### [85] [PS4: Proxy-Supervised Joint Training for Real Target Speaker Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08111)
*Wanyi Ning,Wei Zhou,Yingpeng Li,Yinshang Guo,Haitao Qian,Yiming Cheng*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: PS4 is a proxy-supervised training framework for target speaker extraction in real conversational mixtures, using a large-scale corpus of 71,771 samples and four complementary differentiable objectives to fine-tune a BSRNN-based TSE model, achieving 2nd place on the REAL-T challenge leaderboard with best speaker similarity and timing F1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training TSE models for real conversational mixtures is difficult due to lack of large-scale training corpora and clean target speech for supervision.

Method: (1) Constructed a large-scale corpus of 71,771 samples from four public datasets covering Chinese and English; (2) Proposed proxy-supervised joint training with four differentiable objectives: ASR cross-entropy, speaker similarity, frame-level VAD, and perceptual audio quality; (3) Fine-tuned only the BSRNN separator from a public pre-trained checkpoint.

Result: Ranks 2nd overall on REAL-T challenge leaderboard; achieves best speaker similarity and timing F1 among all submitted systems.

Conclusion: Proxy-supervised learning with multi-objective joint training is effective for TSE in real conversational mixtures without requiring clean target speech for supervision.

Abstract: Training target speaker extraction (TSE) models for real conversational mixtures remains challenging because large-scale training corpora and clean target speech for supervision are unavailable. We present PS4, a proxy-supervised training framework for TSE in real conversational mixtures, with two main contributions. First, we construct a large-scale corpus of 71,771 training samples derived from four public datasets, covering both Chinese and English scenarios. Each sample contains an overlapping speech mixture, per-speaker enrollment audio, a ground-truth transcript, and frame-level voice activity labels. Second, we propose a proxy-supervised joint training strategy that fine-tunes a BSRNN-based TSE model using four complementary differentiable objectives: ASR cross-entropy, speaker similarity, frame-level voice activity detection, and perceptual audio quality. Starting from a publicly available pre-trained checkpoint, only the BSRNN separator is updated during fine-tuning. On the REAL-T challenge leaderboard, PS4 ranks 2nd overall, achieving the best speaker similarity and timing F1 among all submitted systems.

</details>


### [86] [MuScriptor: An Open Model for Multi-Instrument Music Transcription](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08168)
*Simon Rouard,Michael Krause,Axel Roebel,Carl-Johann Simon-Gabriel,Alexandre Défossez*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MuScriptor is an open-weight multi-instrument transcription model that uses synthetic pre-training, real audio fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning post-training to achieve robust performance on diverse real-world music.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing transcription methods fail on complex, real multi-instrument music mixes; synthetic data pre-training alone leads to poor generalization in realistic settings.

Method: Combines synthetic data pre-training with fine-tuning on real music audio and post-training via reinforcement learning; introduces instrument presence conditioning for customizable transcriptions.

Result: MuScriptor produces usable transcriptions on real-world multi-instrument recordings across diverse musical genres.

Conclusion: The proposed three-stage training paradigm (synthetic pre-training + real fine-tuning + RL post-training) with instrument conditioning enables effective multi-instrument transcription in realistic settings.

Abstract: Existing methods for automatic music transcription are often limited to single-instrument recordings or fail on complex, real music mixes. Although previous work utilizes synthetic training data, the resulting models generalize poorly, leading to largely unusable transcription output in realistic, multi-instrument settings. In this work, we analyze the effectiveness of synthetic data for pre-training while combining it with fine-tuning on real music audio and post-training using reinforcement learning. We further introduce conditioning on instrument presence to customize transcriptions. Finally, we release MuScriptor, an open-weight multi-instrument music transcription model that works on real-world music recordings from across a diverse range of musical genres.

</details>


### [87] [A Quantized Native Runtime for On-Device Semantic Audio Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08526)
*Matteo Spanio,Antonio Rodà*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: aria is a dependency-free native runtime that runs Stable Audio 3's text-to-music pipeline on commodity hardware (GPUs, CPUs, Raspberry Pi 5) without Python or deep-learning frameworks. By using quantization (8-bit with no quality loss, 4-bit enabling 1.2B-parameter models on 8GB Pi), in-place memory savings, and activation steering, it matches or exceeds official implementation speed, starts ~7x faster, and enables on-device semantic audio for IoT applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic audio applications increasingly require controllable generation on commodity and embedded hardware rather than through framework-heavy datacenter stacks, motivating a lightweight, dependency-free native runtime for text-to-music synthesis.

Method: Developed a dependency-free native runtime (aria) that runs Stable Audio 3's text-to-music pipeline on ordinary GPUs, CPU-only machines, and Raspberry Pi 5 without Python or deep-learning frameworks. The key technique is quantization (running the model at lower numerical precision) to fit tight memory budgets, saving memory in place rather than adding to it. The runtime also exposes activation steering for low-cost control over generation.

Result: 8-bit quantization shows no measurable quality loss on any measure (prompt adherence, overall audio quality, taste preservation) while sharply cutting memory, and is the fastest mode on GPU; 4-bit adds a small bounded cost but enables running the 1.2-billion-parameter model on an 8GB Raspberry Pi 5. Against the official implementation, aria matches or exceeds generation speed and starts about seven times faster. The steering interface demonstrates genuine but bounded control for a subset of attributes.

Conclusion: These results make a compact, quantized runtime with built-in control a practical basis for on-device semantic audio in Internet-of-Sounds settings.

Abstract: Semantic audio applications increasingly require controllable generation on commodity and embedded hardware rather than through framework-heavy datacenter stacks. We present \textit{aria}, a dependency-free native runtime that runs the complete text-to-music pipeline of Stable Audio~3 (SA3) on ordinary GPUs, CPU-only machines, and a Raspberry~Pi~5, with no Python or deep-learning framework underneath. Our main contribution is a study of quantization: running the model at lower numerical precision to fit tight memory budgets, saving memory in place rather than adding to it. Because the runtime owns every internal tensor, it also exposes activation steering, a low-cost way to steer what the model generates. We judge the quality cost with three independent measures of the output (prompt adherence, overall audio quality, taste preservation), each compared against the ordinary variation between random seeds. Eight-bit precision shows no measurable quality loss on any measure while sharply cutting memory, and it is the fastest mode on the GPU; four-bit adds a small, bounded cost but shrinks the footprint enough to run the $1.2$-billion-parameter model on an $8$\,GB Pi. Against the official implementation, aria matches or exceeds generation speed and starts about seven times faster. A case study of the steering interface generates music carrying taste associations (\emph{sonic seasoning}), with genuine but bounded control for a subset of attributes. These results make a compact, quantized runtime with built-in control a practical basis for on-device semantic audio in Internet-of-Sounds settings. The \textit{aria} runtime is released at https://github.com/matteospanio/aria.

</details>


### [88] [Structural Bottlenecks on Frequency Representation in End-to-End Audio Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08545)
*Nicole Cosme-Clifford*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: State-of-the-art strided convolutional audio encoders impose two predictable bottlenecks (alias equivalence collapse and limited frequency resolution) that degrade access to time-frequency-localized primitives. The authors propose Gabor Latent Refactorization (GLRF), a lightweight post-hoc intervention that recovers frequency-localized access, improving interpretability and control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While end-to-end neural audio models achieve high-fidelity compression, it is unclear whether their encoders preserve access to interpretable frequency-localized primitives (e.g., pitch, timbre) or entangle them in unreachable bases.

Method: The authors combine theoretical analysis (on strided convolutions, aliasing, and filter bandwidth) with controlled experiments to identify two structural bottlenecks. They then introduce GLRF, a post-hoc intervention that re-expresses encoder latents in a Gabor (frequency-localized) basis without retraining.

Result: Collapse rates of 31-35% and filter bandwidths 10-35x above the theoretical resolution bound are observed. GLRF reduces bandwidths to 1.5-3x the bound while preserving reconstruction fidelity and improving pitch control.

Conclusion: Strided convolutional encoders predictably degrade access to frequency-localized primitives, but a lightweight, retraining-free intervention can recover much of that access, improving steerability and interpretability of learned representations.

Abstract: End-to-end neural audio models achieve high-fidelity compression and generation. We might read that performance as evidence they directly represent interpretable features such as pitch and timbre, but a model can produce plausible outputs without doing so. A model may encode these features in any reachable basis, but regardless of which, the features are well described as compositions of time-frequency-localized primitives. Whether state-of-the-art encoders preserve access to these primitives, and thus to compositions of them, remains unclear. Through theoretical analysis and controlled experiments, we show that several state-of-the-art strided convolutional encoders impose two structural bottlenecks, both predictable from architecture and signal structure, on access to these primitives: (1) they collapse primitives into alias equivalence classes, establishing a bound on representational capacity, and (2) they limit the frequency resolution available to learned filters, restricting separability. For well structured data, we find collapse rates of 31-35% and filter bandwidths 10-35x above the theoretical resolution bound, confirming that both bottlenecks arise under realistic signal conditions. We then introduce Gabor Latent Refactorization (GLRF), a lightweight post-hoc intervention that re-expresses encoder latents in a frequency-localized basis, reducing filter bandwidths from 10-35x to 1.5-3x of the theoretical resolution bound while preserving reconstruction fidelity and improving control over attributes like pitch. These results show that the encoders in question predictably degrade access to frequency-localized primitives, entangling the features that depend on them, and that a lightweight, retraining-free intervention can recover much of that access, improving steerability and interpretability.

</details>


### [89] [It Takes Few to TANGO: A Quantized Distributed Model for Binaural Speech Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08645)
*Zahra Benslimane,Pierre Chouteau,Martyna Poreba,Fabrice Auzanneau,Michal Szczepanski,Fabian Chersi,Romain Serizel*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Investigates low-precision inference for TANGO, a hybrid distributed binaural speech enhancement system, showing that quantization errors in mask estimators are compensated by downstream spatial filtering. The resulting MN-TANGO achieves 4.65 MMAC/s and 0.177 MB via INT8 quantization, ERB compression, and grouped recurrent layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural network-based multichannel speech enhancement systems have high computational and memory requirements that limit deployment on resource-constrained devices.

Method: Evaluates post-training quantization and quantization-aware training for TANGO's neural components; analyzes how quantization errors propagate through the spatial filtering stage; introduces MN-TANGO combining INT8 weight-and-activation quantization with ERB compression and grouped recurrent layers.

Result: The spatial filtering stage compensates for most quantization-induced errors; the most compact MN-TANGO reaches 4.65 MMAC/s and 0.177 MB while maintaining comparable performance.

Conclusion: Low-precision inference is feasible for hybrid distributed binaural speech enhancement systems, with spatial filtering providing robustness against quantization errors, enabling significant model size and complexity reduction.

Abstract: Neural network-based multichannel speech enhancement systems achieve strong enhancement performance, but their computational and memory requirements limit deployment on resource-constrained devices. This paper investigates low-precision inference for TANGO, a hybrid distributed binaural speech enhancement system combining neural mask estimation with spatial filtering. We evaluate post-training quantization and quantization-aware training for the neural components, and analyze how quantization errors in the mask estimators propagate through the downstream spatial filtering stage. Our analysis shows that, although quantization degrades intermediate mask estimates, the spatial filtering stage compensates for most quantization-induced errors. Leveraging this robustness, we simplify TANGO into MN-TANGO, reducing both model size and computational complexity while maintaining comparable final performance. By combining INT8 weight-and-activation quantization with ERB compression and grouped recurrent layers, the most compact MN-TANGO reaches 4.65 MMAC/s and 0.177 MB.

</details>


### [90] [MulTTiPop: A Multitrack Transcription Dataset for Pop Music](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08756)
*Nathan Pruyne,Benjamin Stoler,William Chen,Chien-yu Huang,Shinji Watanabe,Chris Donahue*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MulTTiPop is a 3.5-hour dataset of 572 pop music segments with synchronized multitrack MIDI recordings, collected via metadata matching and beat-aligned warping, used to evaluate transcription models (best model achieves 38% Onset F1).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a benchmark dataset of pop music with aligned multitrack MIDI for evaluating automatic music transcription models, addressing the lack of such resources for polyphonic popular music.

Method: Metadata-based matching on song segments from Lakh MIDI and TheoryTab datasets; manual identification of an anchor beat between audio and MIDI; beat tracking on audio and warping the MIDI to match tempo and timing.

Result: State-of-the-art models evaluated on MulTTiPop achieve at most 38% Onset F1, indicating substantial room for improvement.

Conclusion: MulTTiPop serves as a challenging benchmark revealing significant gaps in current automatic music transcription performance on diverse pop music.

Abstract: We present MulTTiPop, a dataset of pop music segments and their associated multitrack MIDI recordings for the evaluation of automatic music transcription models. MulTTiPop contains 572 segments of popular music totaling 3.5 hours of audio, and contains songs from diverse genres and decades from the 1930s to 2000s. To collect this dataset, we perform metadata-based matching on song segments from the Lakh MIDI and TheoryTab datasets, manually identify an anchor beat between the audio and MIDI, then use beat tracking on the audio and warp the MIDI to match its tempo and timing. We evaluate state-of-the-art automatic music transcription models on MulTTiPop and find substantial room for improvement, with the best model achieving 38% Onset F1. More details and sound examples of MulTTiPop are available at https://gclef-cmu.org/multtipop.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [91] [HoloTetSphere: Unified TetSphere Mesh Reconstruction for Physical Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08398)
*YaQiao Dai,Renjiao Yi,Zhirui Gao,Wei Chen,Kai Xu,Chenyang Zhu*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: A topology-adaptive framework that reconstructs a unified, coherent tetrahedral mesh directly from multi-view images via end-to-end optimization, bypassing the error-prone decoupled two-stage pipeline and producing meshes ready for physical simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pipelines rely on a decoupled two-stage paradigm (surface extraction + tetrahedralization) which is error-prone. Recent Lagrangian methods (e.g., TetSphere Splatting) optimize volumetric primitives directly but are constrained by homeomorphic restrictions, producing disjoint tetrahedra unsuitable for physical simulation.

Method: (1) Coupling Gaussian spheres to tetrahedral elements with edge connections to estimate a continuous opacity field for differentiable element pruning. (2) Jointly minimizing mesh smoothing energy and multi-view Gaussian rendering error to drive alternating geometric refinement while preserving topological adaptivity.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art techniques in geometric accuracy and produces coherent, single-connected tetrahedral meshes, effectively bypassing the error-prone tetrahedralization step.

Conclusion: The proposed topology-adaptive framework successfully streamlines downstream physical simulation by directly producing unified, topologically coherent tetrahedral meshes from multi-view input, eliminating the need for decoupled processing.

Abstract: Standard pipelines for physics-ready 3D reconstruction rely on a decoupled two-stage paradigm: extracting surface geometry followed by an error-prone tetrahedralization process. While recent Lagrangian methods like TetSphere Splatting attempt to bypass this by directly optimizing volumetric primitives, their homeomorphic constraints prevent topology-adaptive optimization. Consequently, they produce disjoint tetrahedra rather than a single connected mesh, rendering the structures unsuitable for further physical simulations. To address this, we propose a topology-adaptive framework for holistic tetrahedral mesh reconstruction through end-to-end topological and geometric optimization. First, by coupling Gaussian spheres to tetrahedral elements and leveraging edge connections, we estimate a continuous opacity field for differentiable element pruning. Next, jointly minimizing mesh smoothing energy and multi-view Gaussian rendering error drives alternating geometric refinement while preserving topological adaptivity. Consequently, our approach effectively constructs a unified and topologically coherent tetrahedral mesh. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms state-of-the-art techniques by achieving superior geometric accuracy and producing coherent, single-connected tetrahedral meshes, thereby effectively bypassing the error-prone conventional tetrahedralization step for reconstructed surface meshes and streamlining downstream physical simulation.

</details>


### [92] [ARDY: Autoregressive Diffusion with Hybrid Representation for Interactive Human Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08741)
*Kaifeng Zhao,Mathis Petrovich,Haotian Zhang,Tingwu Wang,Siyu Tang,Davis Rempe*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: ARDY is a streaming motion generation framework that enables real-time, controllable 3D human motion synthesis using online text prompts and kinematic constraints, combining explicit root features with latent body embeddings via a two-stage autoregressive transformer denoiser with variable history context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing offline motion generation methods offer precise control but lack real-time inference speed; online methods enable real-time synthesis but sacrifice controllability and struggle with complex text semantics and long-horizon goals due to limited context windows. ARDY bridges this gap.

Method: ARDY uses a hybrid representation combining explicit root features with latent body embedding for balanced trajectory control and generative learning. It employs a two-stage autoregressive transformer denoiser with variable history context, conditioned on flexible long-horizon kinematic constraints and text labels, trained on large-scale motion capture data with constraints sampled from ground truth poses.

Result: Evaluations on HumanML3D and the Bones Rigplay dataset demonstrate high motion quality and constraint adherence, validating architectural decisions. An interactive demo shows dynamic text control, keyframe pose constraints, path following, and interactive locomotion control.

Conclusion: ARDY achieves high-fidelity, controllable, real-time motion generation through its hybrid representation and two-stage autoregressive denoiser, successfully enabling online text prompting and flexible long-horizon kinematic goals for interactive applications.

Abstract: Generating realistic 3D human motions in real-time within interactive applications is key for animation, simulation, and humanoid robotics. While recent offline motion generation approaches offer precise control via text and kinematic constraints, they lack the inference speed required for interactive settings. Conversely, existing online methods enable real-time synthesis but often sacrifice controllability or struggle with complex text semantics and long-horizon goals due to limited context windows. In this work, we introduce ARDY, a streaming generation framework that bridges this gap by enabling high-fidelity motion generation controllable via online text prompts and flexible kinematic constraints. ARDY employs a hybrid representation that combines explicit root features with a latent body embedding, balancing precise trajectory control with efficient generative learning. We propose a two-stage autoregressive transformer denoiser that features variable history context and supports conditioning on flexible, long-horizon kinematic constraints. By training on a large-scale motion capture dataset and being directly conditioned on text labels and kinematic constraints sampled from ground truth poses, ARDY natively learns controllable generation that supports online prompting and flexible long-horizon goals. Extensive evaluations on the HumanML3D benchmark and the large-scale, high-fidelity Bones Rigplay dataset demonstrate ARDY's high motion quality and constraint adherence, validating the efficacy of our key architectural decisions. Finally, we demonstrate the method's practical versatility through an interactive demo featuring dynamic text control, diverse keyframe pose constraints, path following, and interactive locomotion control via mouse and keyboard. Supplementary video results, code, and model releases can be found at https://research.nvidia.com/labs/sil/projects/ardy/.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [93] [Context Graphs for Proactive Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07721)
*Avinash Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Context Graph, a proactive AI system that monitors enterprise entities and relationships to surface actionable insights before human queries, achieving 0.83 Precision@5 and reducing mean time to surface from 47 minutes to under 30 seconds across three enterprise case studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG and agentic frameworks are fundamentally reactive—agents wait for human queries before acting. This limits enterprise productivity gains. The paper argues that proactive agents that surface relevant information to workers before they ask are needed for genuine productivity improvements.

Method: The system consists of: (1) Context Graph—a live relational data structure modeling enterprise entities, relationships, and state transitions over time; (2) Delta Detection Engine—continuously monitors state changes; (3) Proactivity Scorer—ranks candidate insights by urgency, relevance, and persona-fit using a unified Proactivity Score function; (4) Surfacing Layer—LLM-powered component delivering ranked notifications with grounded explanations. Implemented end-to-end in Python using NetworkX and Anthropic Claude API.

Result: Evaluation across three generic enterprise case studies (contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, sales pipeline hygiene) demonstrates Precision@5 of 0.83, false positive rate of 0.11, and reduction in mean time to surface from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 seconds.

Conclusion: Context-graph-driven proactivity significantly outperforms reactive baselines in enterprise settings, enabling timely, relevant, and personalized information delivery without waiting for human queries.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic frameworks have advanced enterprise AI considerably, yet agents remain fundamentally reactive: they wait for a human query before acting. This paper argues that genuine enterprise productivity gains require proactive agents: systems that surface relevant, actionable information to workers before they ask. We propose the Context Graph, a live relational data structure that models enterprise entities, their relationships, and state transitions over time. Built on this graph, we define a Delta Detection Engine that continuously monitors state changes, a Proactivity Scorer that ranks candidate insights by urgency, relevance, and persona-fit, and a Surfacing Layer powered by an LLM that delivers ranked notifications with grounded explanations. We formalize each component, derive a unified Proactivity Score function, and provide a complete end-to-end Python implementation using NetworkX and the Anthropic Claude API. Evaluation across three generic enterprise case studies (contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, and sales pipeline hygiene) demonstrates that context-graph-driven proactivity achieves Precision@5 of 0.83, a false positive rate of 0.11, and reduces mean time to surface from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 second.

</details>


### [94] [AI-integrated models for assessing agricultural resilience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07759)
*Joshua R. Waite,Dana Golden,Brett Indelicato,Kevin Camp,Mojdeh Saadati,Shannon Regan,Patrick Schnable,Baskar Ganapathysubramanian,Carlos Messina,Suzanne Thornsbury,Soumik Sarkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI tool integrating economic (GTAP) and biophysical (APSIM) models analyzes agricultural supply chain disruptions via natural language queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agricultural supply chains are vulnerable to disruptions through linked biophysical and economic systems, motivating the need for cross-disciplinary impact assessment.

Method: The paper develops an AI-powered tool that integrates the GTAP economic model with the APSIM biophysical model to analyze supply chain shocks, enabling natural language querying and response.

Result: The tool allows policymakers and market participants to assess cross-disciplinary impacts of supply chain disruptions through natural language interactions.

Conclusion: Integrating economic and biophysical models with an AI interface can help stakeholders better understand and respond to agricultural supply chain vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Agricultural supply chains are vulnerable to disruptions through linked biophysical and economic systems. We develop an AI-powered tool that integrates economic models (GTAP) with biophysical models (APSIM) to analyze supply chain shocks, enabling policymakers and market participants to assess cross-disciplinary impacts through queries and responses written in natural language.

</details>


### [95] [Adversarial Social Epistemology for Assemblies of Humans and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07760)
*Mihnea C. Moldoveanu,Joel A. C. Baum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an adversarial social epistemology (ASE) framework for analyzing how agents exploit trust mechanisms in densely interconnected communicative landscapes, offering analytical language, mechanisms of trust subversion, and auditing tools based on inferentialist semantics and epistemic networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing concepts like epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, and misinformation diffusion inadequately capture how agents strategically exploit the commitments and entitlements that normally make scaffolded public assertions trustworthy in densely interactive communicative landscapes.

Method: The paper develops an adversarial social epistemology framework with an inferentialist semantics for interpreting assertions, combined with epistemic network analysis to model how agents distort, color, omit, fabricate, or strategically under-specify information for private gain.

Result: Provides new analytical language for describing trust subversion in scaffolded communications, outlines specific mechanisms for subverting auditability of inferential chains, and proposes machinery for auditing and redressing trust breaches.

Conclusion: Understanding adversarial communication requires moving beyond simple misinformation models to account for how agents exploit the very scaffolding that normally enables trustworthy public assertion, with proposed inferentialist auditing tools offering a path toward redress.

Abstract: We outline an adversarial social epistemology (ASE) for densely interactive communicative landscapes in which public assertions are scaffolded by chains of testimony, inference, institutional certification, and tacit trust. In such landscapes, agents have incentives and affordances to distort, color, omit, fabricate, or strategically under-specify information for private, reputational, rhetorical, or material gains. We argue that these phenomena are not adequately captured by familiar descriptions of epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, or misinformation diffusion. What requires explanation is how communicative agents exploit the commitments and entitlements that normally make scaffolded assertions trustworthy. We provide language that delivers the requisite analysis, outline mechanisms that subvert trust in scaffolded public communications, and outline machinery for auditing and redressing trust breaches arising from subverting the auditability of inferential chains, drawing on epistemic networks, enriched with an inferentialist semantics for interpreting assertions.

</details>


### [96] [Aligning Clinical Needs and AI Capabilities: A Survey on LLMs for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07761)
*Qi Peng,Jiatong Li,Sirui Huang,Yiyang Jiang,Kaisong Gong,Ronger Ding,Shijie Ye,Changmeng Zheng,Yi Cai,Xiaobo Yang,Jin Huang,Xiao-Yong Wei,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey examines progress in medical LLMs through a dual clinical-computational lens, proposing a five-level competency scheme (Miller's Pyramid), linking reasoning patterns to medical tasks, benchmarking 18 state-of-the-art models, and discussing open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge clinical practice and computational methods by systematically analyzing medical LLMs' reasoning capabilities, addressing the need for structured evaluation frameworks in healthcare AI.

Method: Dual-view approach: (1) Clinical side: five-level competency scheme based on Miller's Pyramid (knowledge recall to dynamic case management). (2) Computational side: mapping deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning to medical goals/tasks. Benchmarking on 18 models across the proposed five-level dataset.

Result: Medical specialist models excel in diagnosis-centric tasks; general models lead in decision support and dialogue. The benchmark reveals distinct capability profiles across the five reasoning levels.

Conclusion: Current progress shows promise but faces challenges (data limitations, hallucination, grounding issues). Future directions point toward safer, more reliable, workflow-integrated medical LLM systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as important tools in healthcare, showing growing potential for clinical reasoning and patient care. This survey examines recent progress in medical LLMs, focusing on reasoning applications and requirements. We present a dual-view approach that connects clinical practice with computational methods. On the clinical side, we establish a five-level competency scheme following Miller's Pyramid, progressing from knowledge recall to dynamic case management. On the computational side, we link deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning patterns to common medical goals and tasks. We also introduce a benchmark dataset spanning five levels of medical reasoning capability and report results on 18 state-of-the-art models, revealing that medical specialist models excel in diagnosis-centric tasks while general models lead in decision support and dialogue. We conclude by discussing current progress and open challenges, including data limitations, hallucination, and grounding issues, and outline directions toward safer, more reliable, and workflow-ready systems.

</details>


### [97] [Alignment Plausibility: A New Standard for Assuring AI in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07766)
*Gwydion Williams,Sara Zannone,Bilal A Mateen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs used for mental health support are products of an attention economy that prioritizes engagement over therapeutic friction. Safety efforts are reactive, overlooking subtle risks like dependency and boundary erosion. The authors propose "alignment plausibility" — a three-tier alignment framework (value specification, embedded training, deployment oversight) analogous to clinical supervision — to argue for trust that AI systems are safe and beneficial in healthcare.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM safety measures in mental health are reactive, addressing only visible acute harms while ignoring subtler long-term risks (dependency, boundary erosion, distorted belief amplification) stemming from the attention economy's engagement-driven design.

Method: The paper proposes a three-level alignment framework inspired by clinical practice safeguards: 1) explicit value specification from codified clinical norms; 2) training that embeds those values; 3) deployment oversight to detect drift and long-term harm. This yields "alignment plausibility," a regulatory construct analogous to biological plausibility for arguing trust in AI health systems.

Result: A structured construct called alignment plausibility is introduced, providing a principled way to demonstrate that an AI system's values, training regime, and oversight mechanisms are collectively consistent with safe, positive health outcomes, no-harm guarantees, and patient benefit.

Conclusion: Organizing LLM alignment into three tiers mirroring clinical practice oversight yields alignment plausibility as a regulatory standard for trustworthy AI in mental health, addressing the structural safety gap left by reactive, engagement-driven approaches.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become significant providers of mental health support, yet they remain products of an attention economy whose operational and commercial targets favour sustained engagement over the friction that effective psychological support often requires. Developers' safety responses have been largely reactive, addressing the most visible and acute harms while subtler, longer-term patterns of risk (e.g., dependency, boundary erosion, the amplification of distorted beliefs) receive less attention. We contend that making LLMs structurally safe requires alignment organised at three levels that mirror how society assures the safety of human clinical practice: 1) explicit value specification grounded in the codified normative commitments of clinical practice; 2) training that embeds those values in the model; and 3) oversight that detects drift and longer-term harm during deployment, much as clinical supervision does for human practice. Organising alignment in this way yields a construct we call alignment plausibility - a structured demonstration that a system's values, training regime, and oversight mechanisms are together consistent with safe and positive outcomes. We propose alignment plausibility as a regulatory construct (by drawing analogy to the established construct of biological plausibility) for AI in health: a principled way to argue for, or against, trust that systems are aligned to positive health outcomes, will cause no harm even where capable of doing so, and will ultimately lead to patient benefit.

</details>


### [98] [Idiobionics: The Unification of Privacy and Intelligent Robotic Prostheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07775)
*Kwesi Afari Darfoor,Patrick M. Pilarski,Bailey Kacsmar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "idiobionics," a new research field investigating privacy risks in intelligent bionic limbs (robotic prostheses), defines the concept, demonstrates potential adversarial attacks, and outlines open research questions at the intersection of privacy and bionic devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advanced sensing and AI control in robotic prostheses introduce privacy threat vectors that could be exploited by malicious entities. To fully realize the benefits of next-generation bionic limbs, it is important to directly understand and address these privacy risks and the barriers they might present to user adoption.

Method: The paper defines the term "idiobionics," grounds it in related literature, and provides preliminary evidence showing and discussing potential adversarial attacks that could exploit intelligent bionic limb designs.

Result: A curated list of open research questions within idiobionics relevant to researchers in wearable robotics and other human-facing autonomous systems.

Conclusion: Idiobionics research will help unlock the full potential of robotic prostheses and related bionic devices by holistically addressing privacy risks.

Abstract: The human body is at the center of a growing family of technologies designed to tightly and persistently couple biological and digital systems. Robotic prostheses are a representative example of this tight coupling. Also referred to as bionic limbs, robotic prostheses are devices that support people who have lost limbs in pursuing daily life activities such as walking and grasping objects. Bionic limbs are now perceptive and responsive owing to their integration with advanced sensors and artificial intelligence-based control approaches. Consequently, such robotic prostheses can now be viewed as semiautonomous wearable robotic systems that can co-adapt with their users. However, the same sensing and control advancements that increase the capability of robotic prostheses also introduce threat vectors that could be exploited by malicious entities to violate the privacy of users. To fully realize the benefits of next-generation bionic limbs, we maintain it is important to directly understand and address these privacy risks and the barriers they might present to user adoption. This paper therefore introduces a new line of inquiry we term idiobionics to holistically investigate issues at the intersection of privacy and intelligent bionic limbs. As the main contribution of this paper, we define idiobionics, ground it in related literature, and provide preliminary evidence showing and discussing potential adversarial attacks that could exploit intelligent bionic limb designs. We then contribute a curated list of open research questions within idiobionics that are relevant to researchers in wearable robotics and other human-facing autonomous systems. We expect that idiobionics research will help unlock the full potential of robotic prostheses and related bionic devices.

</details>


### [99] [Infinity-Parser2 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07836)
*Zuming Huang,Jun Huang,Kexuan Ren,Baode Wang,Weizhen Li,Jianming Feng,Yu Wang,Yichen Yao,Shijun Lin,Yige Tang,Cheng Peng,Weidi Xu,Wei Chu,Yinghui Xu,Yuan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Infinity-Parser2 is a large multimodal model for end-to-end document parsing, featuring a controllable data-synthesis pipeline and multi-task reinforcement learning. It introduces Infinity-Doc2-5M (5M bilingual corpus), a verifiable reward system for Joint RL across 8 tasks, and two variants (Flash for speed, Pro for accuracy). Pro achieves SOTA on olmOCR-Bench (87.6%) and ParseBench (74.3%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the persistent scarcity of faithfully annotated parsing corpora for end-to-end document parsing by building a controllable data-synthesis pipeline and leveraging multi-task reinforcement learning.

Method: (1) A scalable synthesis engine with controllable rendering and iterative refinement, producing Infinity-Doc2-5M (5M bilingual samples). (2) A verifiable multi-task reward system enabling Joint Reinforcement Learning across eight co-trained objectives. (3) Two model variants (Flash and Pro) under a shared architecture.

Result: Infinity-Parser2-Pro achieves SOTA: 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, surpassing DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, and MinerU2.5. Flash variant achieves 3.68x throughput gain over Infinity-Parser-7B.

Conclusion: The proposed data synthesis pipeline and multi-task RL approach effectively solve data scarcity and unify perception, structure, and reasoning for document parsing, with strong generalization to charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA.

Abstract: We present Infinity-Parser2, a large multimodal model that couples a controllable data-synthesis pipeline with multi-task reinforcement learning for end-to-end document parsing, addressing the persistent scarcity of faithfully annotated parsing corpora. Our contributions are threefold. First, we build a scalable synthesis engine, pairing a controllable rendering framework with an iterative refinement loop, and use it to construct and open-source Infinity-Doc2-5M: a 5-million-sample bilingual (Chinese/English) corpus spanning diverse document types, annotated with element bounding boxes, canonical content forms (Markdown, HTML, LaTeX, SMILES, structured charts), and full-page reading order. Second, we introduce a verifiable, multi-task reward system that enables Joint Reinforcement Learning across eight co-trained objectives (document parsing, layout analysis, table parsing, math formula parsing, chart parsing, chemical formula parsing, document VQA, and general multimodal understanding), unifying perception, structure, and reasoning in a single optimization signal. Third, we release two variants under a shared architecture: Infinity-Parser2-Flash, optimized for low-latency inference with a $3.68\times$ throughput gain over Infinity-Parser-7B, and Infinity-Parser2-Pro, engineered for precision-critical settings. Infinity-Parser2-Pro reaches state-of-the-art 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, surpassing DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, and MinerU2.5, with strong generalization to charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA.

</details>


### [100] [VectorizationLLM: Smart Vectorization Based AI Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07846)
*Ryan Duke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VectorizationLLM is a specialized Large Language Model fine-tuned on Google's open-weight LLMs, designed as an instructive assistant for students in CTEC 247 (Applied Computational Analysis II) at NYIT. It teaches MATLAB concepts including vectorization, Fourier analysis, and differential equations using a RAG knowledge base and system prompt architecture, providing explanations with examples without giving direct answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a specialized instructional tool that helps students learn computational analysis concepts in MATLAB without providing direct answers, thereby promoting genuine understanding and preventing academic dishonesty.

Method: The model is built on Google's open-weight LLMs, enhanced with a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) knowledge base and a system prompt architecture. It is designed to provide detailed explanations with code, text, and image examples sourced from in-class notes.

Result: The LLM successfully functions as an instructive assistant that offers concept explanations and examples across vectorization, time/wave vector analysis, piecewise functions, Fourier analysis, and differential equations in MATLAB, while withholding direct answers to questions.

Conclusion: VectorizationLLM demonstrates a promising approach to domain-specific educational AI by combining LLM technology with RAG and pedagogical constraints to support active learning in engineering computational analysis courses.

Abstract: VectorizationLLM is a specialized Large Language Model based on Google open-weight LLMs. The model is designed to assist students to learn smart vectorization, time/wave vector analysis, piecewise functions, Fourier analysis, and differential equations in MATLAB. The course application is CTEC 247: Applied Computational Analysis II by the Department of Electrical & Computer Engineering Technology at New York Institute of Technology Old Westbury. The LLM model is designed to be an instructive assistant, providing detailed explanations of concepts with examples from in-class notes without providing direct answers to questions. The model is designed with a RAG (Retrieval Augmented Generation) knowledge base and system prompt architecture. Examples in both code, text, and images are provided in the LLM responses.

</details>


### [101] [A Graph Neural Network Model for Real-Time Gesture Recognition Based on sEMG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07850)
*Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan,Kamal Premaratne,Manohar Murthi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a novel graph-based representation of sEMG signals for real-time hand gesture recognition, achieving 99% accuracy with 48ms processing time using a graph neural network.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable seamless control of advanced hand prostheses and augmented reality through accurate and immediate hand gesture recognition using sEMG signals.

Method: A graph network representation of muscle activation patterns from forearm sEMG signals (8 electrodes, Myoband), combined with a graph neural network (GNN) for real-time hand gesture classification.

Result: 99% average classification accuracy across 8 healthy subjects, outperforming state-of-the-art methods; average graph construction and prediction time of 48ms on an M1 Pro CPU.

Conclusion: The proposed graph-based sEMG representation and GNN approach is highly accurate and suitable for real-time applications in prosthetics and augmented reality.

Abstract: For seemless control of advanced hand prostheses and augmented reality, accurate and immediate hand gestures recognition is essential. Surface electromyography (sEMG) signals obtained from the forearm are commonly employed for this purpose. In this paper, we present a novel approach for sEMG representation that utilizes graph networks which contain information about muscle activation patterns in the forearm. Based on these graph networks, we have developed a machine learning algorithm capable of real-time hand gesture recognition using a graph neural network. The algorithm's performance was evaluated using sEMG signals acquired from myoband, which has 8 electrodes placed around the forearm, involving 8 healthy subjects. The proposed method demonstrated an average classification accuracy of 99\%, surpassing the performance of state-of-the-art techniques. The average time for both graph construction and prediction stood at 48ms utilizing a M1 pro CPU, rendering the approach well-suited for real-time applications.

</details>


### [102] [Agentic AI and Retrieval-Augmented Models in Straight-Through Underwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07858)
*Robert Richardson,Josh Meyers,Brian Hartman,David Sandberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores how agentic AI architectures (LLMs, RAG, multi-agent systems) can support actuarial underwriting, demonstrating through a synthetic BOP underwriting experiment that a multi-agent "Agentic RAG" pipeline outperforms single-LLM and naive RAG baselines, especially in multi-step and missing-information scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is reshaping actuarial practice, but actuaries face a complex design space from rule-based automation to LLM-based systems. The paper aims to examine how emerging AI architectures can support actuarial priorities like transparency, auditability, and human-in-the-loop governance in regulated decision workflows.

Method: The authors develop a synthetic experimental environment for straight-through underwriting of small commercial Business Owner Policies (BOPs) and compare three pipelines: (i) single-LLM baseline, (ii) naive RAG system, and (iii) multi-agent "Agentic RAG" pipeline combining targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation.

Result: The agentic system performs best overall, with the largest gains observed in multi-step and missing-information scenarios, where structured retrieval and reflection prevent unsupported straight-through decisions.

Conclusion: Multi-agent agentic architectures offer superior performance for actuarial underwriting tasks compared to simpler single-LLM or naive RAG approaches, particularly in complex scenarios requiring multi-step reasoning and handling of incomplete information.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is beginning to reshape actuarial practice, particularly in domains that require reasoning over unstructured documents, heterogeneous data sources, and regulated decision workflows. Actuaries now face a design space that ranges from traditional rule-based automation to large language models (LLMs), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and multi-agent ``agentic'' systems that plan, retrieve, call tools, and reflect. This paper examines how these emerging architectures can support actuarial priorities such as transparency, auditability, and human-in-the-loop governance, with a focus on straight-through decision processes. To make these ideas concrete, we develop and analyze an agentic AI framework for straight-through underwriting of small commercial Business Owner Policies (BOPs). We construct a synthetic but realistic experimental environment and compare three underwriting pipelines: (i) a single-LLM baseline, (ii) a naive RAG system, and (iii) a multi-agent ``Agentic RAG'' pipeline that combines targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation. The agentic system performs best overall, with the largest gains in multi-step and missing-information scenarios, where structured retrieval and reflection help the model avoid unsupported straight-through decisions.

</details>


### [103] [Feedback Manipulation Regularization: Enabling Offline Agent Alignment for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07859)
*Benjamin Poole,Minwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FMR is an algorithm-agnostic method that uses evaluative feedback as a corrective signal to improve alignment of imitation learning policies in sequential decision-making, achieving up to 98% reduction in misalignment across Safety Gymnasium environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for combining human demonstrations and feedback use multi-stage pipelines designed for contextual bandit framing of language generation, with little work exploring how these complementary inputs can serve as interconnected signals for single-stage offline training in fully sequential decision-making environments.

Method: Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR) — an algorithm-agnostic method that harnesses evaluative feedback as a corrective signal to improve alignment of imitation learning policies, tested on adapted Safety Gymnasium environments.

Result: Demonstrated improved aptitude and up to 98% reduction in misalignment across various imitation learning algorithms; remains robust in limited data regimes even with scarce aligned and uninformative noisy demonstrations.

Conclusion: FMR effectively leverages evaluative feedback as a corrective signal for single-stage offline alignment training in sequential decision-making, proving robust even under data scarcity.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) research has increasingly shifted focus towards alignment, ensuring agents learn behaviors adhering to human values. While human demonstrations and feedback have proven crucial for alignment, existing approaches predominantly combine these signals using multi-stage pipelines designed for the contextual bandit framing of language generation. Yet little work explores how these complementary inputs can serve as a richer, interconnected signal for single-stage offline training in fully sequential decision-making environments. We propose Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR), an algorithm-agnostic method that harnesses evaluative feedback as a corrective signal to improve the alignment of imitation learning policies. We adapt Safety Gymnasium environments to be a principled testbed for alignment evaluation, demonstrating improved aptitude and up to a 98\% reduction in misalignment across a range of imitation learning algorithms. FMR remains robust in limited data regimes, even when learning from scarce aligned and uninformative noisy demonstrations.

</details>


### [104] [From Prompts to Contracts: Harness Engineering for Auditable Enterprise LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08028)
*Joongho Ahn,Moonsoo Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a harness-engineering approach that transforms LLM application prototypes into traceable, auditable agent architectures by moving deterministic behavior into code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts around a replaceable composition boundary. Evaluated on 25 Korean listed companies across 270 runs, the harness preserves source-grounding, entity-routing, trace, output-hygiene, and recommendation-language contracts under model substitution, while ablation experiments show that prompt-only enforcement lets violations through (caught by harness), and bolt-on guardrails over-refuse (dropping utility to 88/120 vs. harness's 120/120).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise LLM applications often start as prototype prompts and retrieval context, but productization requires source boundaries, entity routing, answer contracts, and reproducible traces—requirements unmet by purely prompt-driven approaches.

Method: A harness-engineering architecture where deterministic behavior is encoded in code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts around a replaceable composition boundary; source-backed claims serve as the authority for runtime answers.

Result: (1) Harness preserves all contract types across fixed validation scenarios; fault-injection confirms validators detect broken contracts. (2) Checks held under model substitution (270 runs, 3 models); failures confined to model-composed side. (3) Prompt-only enforcement allows violations; bolt-on guardrails over-refuse (88/120 utility), while harness preserves full utility (120/120) and safety.

Conclusion: The result is a reusable engineering pattern for converting exploratory LLM prototypes into auditable applications with versioned source, control, and validation artifacts.

Abstract: Enterprise large language model (LLM) applications often begin as prototypes whose behavior is carried by prompts and retrieval context. Productization adds requirements for source boundaries, entity routing, answer contracts, and reproducible traces. We present a harness-engineering approach that reconstructs this pattern into a traceable, auditable LLM-agent architecture: deterministic behavior moves into code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts around a replaceable composition boundary, while source-backed claims remain the authority for runtime answers. We instantiate it on a public-data slice of five Korean corporate groups (25 listed companies) and evaluate three research questions. (1) The harness preserves its source-grounding, entity-routing, trace, output-hygiene, and recommendation-language contracts across the fixed validation scenarios; a fault-injection control confirms the validators flag deliberately broken contracts. (2) The checks the harness enforces held under model substitution: across three hosted models, they passed on all 270 composition-boundary runs; failures were confined to the model-composed side and were caught and recorded. (3) The code-owned guarantees are load-bearing, not reproducible by prompting alone: holding the model fixed and varying only the enforcement layer, prompt instructions alone let recommendation-language and internal-trace-leakage violations reach the reader, which the harness blocks entirely. A bolt-on external guardrail prevents such violations too but over-refuses, dropping utility to 88/120 where the harness preserves full utility (120/120); in this ablation, only code-owned enforcement preserves both safety and utility. The result is a reusable engineering pattern for turning exploratory prototypes into auditable applications with versioned source, control, and validation artifacts.

</details>


### [105] [Nigeria Machinery: A Low-Resource Industrial Dataset with a Domain-Grounded Reasoning Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07883)
*Gospel Bassey,Vincent Fakiyesi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper releases a small Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset (89 records, 28 indicators, 2006-2025) and a method for building chain-of-thought reasoning examples from sparse numeric data, achieving 94 domain-grounded prompt-completion-reasoning rows with 100% retrieval accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of public, model-ready industrial machinery data for African economies, hindering quantitative analysis and numeric task training for language models in this domain.

Method: The authors compile 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators from public sources, create a codebook, and develop a method to construct chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning examples from sparse numeric values, resulting in 94 prompt-completion-reasoning trace rows with provenance tracking.
