Result: Domain-grounded prompts increased from 1 out of 78 in an earlier release to 94 out of 94, and retrieval answer accuracy reached 84 out of 84 (100%). The dataset is released under CC-BY-4.0 with a per-row provenance file.

Conclusion: The dataset is a reference and seed dataset (not a large training set) with clear limitations: 89 records, 17 indicators with only one observation, and most reasoning rows are retrieval rather than multi-step computation.

Abstract: There is relatively little, public, and model-ready data on industrial machinery for African economies. This makes it hard to do quantitative analysis or to train language models on numeric tasks grounded in that setting. We release two things to help with part of this problem. The first is the Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset: 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators, covering Nigeria's manufacturing and oil and gas sectors from 2006 to 2025. Every record names a public source and is decoded by a codebook. The second is a method for building chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning examples from these sparse numeric values. The result is 94 prompt, completion, and reasoning-trace rows. In every row, the prompt names the real indicator, subsector, year, and source of the record it comes from. The data adaptation work was carried out by Adaption Labs. Along the way we describe a problem that is common when language models are used to build datasets. The prompts can match the real numbers while saying nothing about the real domain. We show that fixing this raises the share of domain-grounded prompts from 1 out of 78 in an earlier release to 94 out of 94, and that every retrieval answer now matches its source value (84 out of 84). We release the data, the reasoning layer, and a per-row provenance file under CC-BY-4.0. We are clear about the limits. With 89 records and 17 indicators that have only one observation, this is a reference and seed dataset, not a large training set. Most reasoning rows are retrieval rather than multi-step computation.

</details>


### [106] [Workflow as Knowledge: Semantic Persistence for LLM-Mediated Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08740)
*Emanuele Quinto,Carlo Andrea Rozzi,Francesco Zanitti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Lisp-inspired, language-independent conceptual model where LLM workflow definitions, instances, and records are treated as persistent knowledge objects, with a core semantic distinction between deterministic computation (derive) and LLM-mediated judgment (infer).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM applications increasingly use explicit workflows for tool use, retrieval, branching, checkpointing, and human approval, but lack a unified conceptual model for representing workflows as inspectable, resumable, and reviewable knowledge objects.

Method: A Lisp-inspired but language-independent conceptual model using symbolic forms, object identity, and live-image thinking as explanatory lenses. Workflow definitions, instances, inference records, context snapshots, and dependency relations are represented as persistent knowledge objects in a shared knowledge substrate. A central semantic distinction is introduced: derive (deterministic computation over available state) vs. infer (mediated LLM judgment under declared context and capability policy).

Result: A preliminary conceptual account of semantic persistence where workflows are representable as inspectable, resumable, and reviewable knowledge objects.

Conclusion: The proposed model enables semantic persistence for LLM workflows, though formal transition semantics remain future work.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) applications increasingly use explicit workflows for tool use, retrieval, branching, checkpointing, and human approval. Existing workflow systems already address many execution concerns. This paper proposes a Lisp-inspired but language-independent conceptual model: symbolic forms, object identity, and live-image thinking are used as explanatory lenses, not implementation commitments. In this model, workflow definitions, workflow instances, inference records, context snapshots, and dependency relations are represented as persistent knowledge objects in a shared knowledge substrate. Its central semantic distinction is between derive and infer: derive is deterministic computation over available state; infer is mediated LLM judgment under declared context and executor-controlled capability policy.
  The result is a preliminary conceptual account of semantic persistence: workflows do not merely produce knowledge and leave traces, but can themselves be represented as inspectable, resumable, and reviewable knowledge objects, while formal transition semantics remain future work.

</details>


### [107] [Persona Cartography: Charting Language Model Personality Traits in Weight Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916)
*Luke Baines,Anton Gonzalvez Hawthorne,Mariia Koroliuk,Irakli Shalibashvili,Clément Dumas,Konstantinos Voudouris,David Demitri Africa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes treating LLM personas as positions in the OCEAN personality trait space, trains low-rank adapters to control individual traits, and demonstrates monotonic scaling, additive composition, preserved capabilities, and effects on safety-relevant behaviors like frustration and sycophancy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs exhibit recurring behavioral patterns (personas) that shape generalization and safety, but existing tools for decomposing, measuring, and controlling these personas are unreliable or lacking.

Method: (1) Map LLM personas to OCEAN (Big Five) personality framework; (2) Train low-rank adapters to amplify or suppress individual OCEAN traits; (3) Evaluate effects via an LLM-judge calibrated against human-validated panels, trait-specific MC benchmarks, and standard capability evaluations; (4) Introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline to recover interpretable behavioral factors from model rollouts.

Result: Across 6 models (4B-32B) from 3 families: each adapter moves its target trait monotonically with scale; adapters combine approximately additively for mixed personas; capability benchmarks are preserved at moderate scales; moving along neuroticism and agreeableness axes affects frustration and sycophancy respectively; the unsupervised pipeline recovers four interpretable factors (tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution).

Conclusion: Persona control can be understood as learning, scaling, and composing traits in weight space, bridging personality measurement, model editing, and safety.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit recurring behavioural patterns -- personas -- that shape generalisation and safety, but we lack reliable tools for decomposing, measuring, and controlling them. Our central insight is to treat personas as positions in a space of behavioural traits, using the OCEAN framework to describe model personas in terms of Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, and Neuroticism. We train low-rank adapters to amplify or suppress individual traits, and evaluate their effects using an LLM-judge calibrated against a human-validated panel, trait-specific multiple-choice benchmarks, and standard capability evaluations. Across six models from three families (4B-32B), we find that each adapter moves its target trait largely monotonically with scale, combines approximately additively with other adapters to construct mixed personas, and preserves performance on capability benchmarks at moderate scales. We further show that the induced trait axes affect safety-relevant behaviour in downstream evaluations: for example, moving along neuroticism and agreeableness axes affects frustration and sycophancy respectively. We also introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline that recovers four interpretable behavioural factors (tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution) from model rollouts. Persona control can then be considered in terms of learning, scaling, and composing traits in weight space, providing a bridge between personality measurement, model editing, and safety.

</details>


### [108] [Evaluating the Effect of Frame Rate in Sequence-Based Classification of Autism-Related Self-Stimulatory Hand Idiosyncrasies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07957)
*Raunak Mondal,Peter Washington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study systematically evaluates sequence-based neural architectures (LSTM, GRU) and data augmentation strategies for detecting autism-related self-stimulatory behaviors from video, finding GRU achieves 98.75% accuracy at 15-frame sampling intervals and identifying upsampling as the most critical augmentation technique.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ASD affects over 75 million individuals globally, yet scalable computational methods for remote behavioral screening remain limited. The study addresses the need for automated detection of self-stimulatory behaviors from video to enable more accessible screening.

Method: LSTM and GRU models were trained on pose-derived features from the SSBD dataset across six frame sampling intervals (1, 5, 15, 30, 45, 90). Ten data augmentation strategies were evaluated in an I3D transfer learning pipeline with ablation study. Personalized per-subject models with temporal split testing were also implemented.

Result: LSTM achieved 97.5% and GRU achieved 98.75% peak accuracy at 15-frame sampling, both exceeding prior CNN baselines (62-76%). Horizontal flip achieved highest standalone augmentation accuracy (48.78%). Removing upsampling caused the largest performance drop. Personalized models yielded mean loss 1.84 (SD 0.79).

Conclusion: The study provides actionable guidance on optimal architecture (GRU), sampling rate (15 frames), and augmentation strategy (upsampling critical) for video-based behavioral classification in data-scarce clinical settings.

Abstract: Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) affects over 75 million individuals worldwide, yet scalable computational methods for remote behavioral screening remain limited. This study addresses two complementary challenges in automated detection of autism-related self-stimulatory behaviors from video: (1) identifying the optimal sequence-based neural network architecture and temporal sampling rate, and (2) characterizing data augmentation strategies for training on small behavioral datasets. For the first objective, long short-term memory (LSTM) and gated recurrent unit (GRU) models were trained on pose-derived features from the Self-Stimulatory Behavior Diagnosis (SSBD) dataset at frame sampling intervals of 1, 5, 15, 30, 45, and 90 frames. Both architectures exceeded prior convolutional neural network (CNN) baselines (62-76% accuracy), with peak accuracies of 97.5% (LSTM) and 98.75% (GRU) at a sampling interval of every 15 frames. For the second objective, ten data augmentation strategies were applied to an I3D transfer learning pipeline, with an ablation study quantifying the marginal contribution of each technique. Horizontal flip achieved the highest standalone accuracy (48.78%), while exclusion of upsampling from the augmentation pipeline produced the largest performance degradation, indicating its necessity for complex behavioral video augmentation. A personalized machine learning approach, in which per-subject models were trained and tested on temporally split segments of each video, produced consistent predictions (mean loss 1.84, SD 0.79). These results provide practitioners with concrete guidance on architecture selection, sampling rate, and augmentation strategy for video-based behavioral classification in data-scarce clinical domains.

</details>


### [109] [Agentic Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07984)
*Seokhoon Jeong,Mijung Kim,Taehwan Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentNAS bridges LLM-driven architecture design and conventional NAS by having an LLM generate a seed architecture and decompose it into a "slotted architecture" with interchangeable modules, defining a bounded search space for NAS to explore. It achieves SOTA on 11 of 17 diverse tasks, with LLM seeds and NAS combinatorial search proving broadly complementary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NAS methods remain limited by manually engineered search spaces requiring domain expertise, while LLMs can generate architectures in open-ended spaces but lack efficient search. The optimal division of labor between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search is unexplored.

Method: An LLM generates a high-quality seed architecture and decomposes it into a "slotted architecture" — a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that automatically defines a bounded, task-specific search space for conventional NAS, without manual engineering. This is instantiated as AgentNAS, a modular three-phase pipeline.

Result: On 17 tasks across classification, dense regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging (NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS), AgentNAS achieves SOTA on 11 tasks, outperforming published baselines including task-specific expert designs. Ablations show LLM seeds surpass published baselines on most tasks, and NAS provides additional gains via combinatorial recombination across slots that independent LLM samples cannot replicate. Patterns hold across three LLMs of different capability levels.

Conclusion: The LLM-NAS division of labor is robust and complementary: LLMs provide high-quality seeds, while NAS explores combinatorial recombinations across slots. This framework eliminates manual search space engineering while benefiting from both paradigms.

Abstract: Neural architecture search (NAS) methods have grown increasingly efficient, yet they remain bounded by manually engineered search spaces that require substantial domain expertise and must be rebuilt for every new task. Large language models (LLMs) can generate architectures in an open-ended space, but how to optimally divide the labor between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search remains unexplored. We propose a mechanism that bridges these two paradigms: an LLM produces a high-quality seed architecture, then decomposes it into a "slotted architecture", a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that automatically defines a bounded, task-specific search space for conventional NAS to explore, without manual engineering. We instantiate this mechanism in AgentNAS, a modular three-phase pipeline in which each component's contribution can be measured independently. On 17 tasks spanning classification, dense regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging across diverse modalities (NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS), AgentNAS establishes a new state of the art on 11 tasks, outperforming published baselines including task-specific expert designs. Ablation studies show that the two search mechanisms are broadly complementary: the LLM-generated seed already surpasses published baselines on the majority of tasks, and NAS delivers additional gains in most cases through combinatorial recombination across slots, a mode of search that independent LLM samples cannot replicate. These patterns hold across three LLMs of different capability levels, confirming that the division of labor is robust. Our code is available at https://github.com/alroimfebruary/AgentNAS.

</details>


### [110] [Concretized Proposition Prompting Resolves Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08018)
*Changhun Lee,Minguk Jeon,Jongkyung Shin,Chiehyeon Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs face a Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy; the proposed Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP) resolves this by concretizing propositions, significantly improving reasoning on knowledge-heavy (medical) and composition-heavy (math) benchmarks, and scales across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle to balance compositional reasoning with factual knowledge retrieval, creating a dichotomy that hinders robust reasoning.

Method: Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP) explicitly concretizes propositions relevant to the question before reasoning, bridging composition and knowledge.

Result: CPP significantly enhances reasoning performance, especially on medical benchmarks (knowledge-priority), and remains competitive on math benchmarks (reasoning-priority). It scales across foundation models and parameter sizes.

Conclusion: CPP resolves the composition-knowledge dichotomy by providing a paradigm for logically organized and factually grounded reasoning.

Abstract: LLMs often struggle to balance compositionality with knowledgeability, a challenge we define as Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy. To address this, we propose Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP), a framework that explicitly concretizes propositions relevant to questions. The results demonstrate that CPP significantly enhances reasoning performance, particularly in medical benchmarks where precise knowledge is paramount, while being competitive on math benchmarks where deductive reasoning is prioritized. Additional experiments reveal that CPP is scalable to various foundation models and parameter sizes, being a fundamental paradigm that bridges the gap between composition- and knowledge-based approaches. Consequently, CPP resolves the composition-knowledge dichotomy by providing a solid foundation for logically organized and factually grounded reasoning.

</details>


### [111] [A safety-oriented hypothetico-deductive framework for AI-assisted differential diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08038)
*Fan Ma,Mauro Giuffrè,Donald Wright,Kent McCann,Mark Iscoe,Lingfei Qian,Mingyang Jiang,Chi Wing Ng,Na Hong,Huan He,Cathy Shyr,Qingyu Chen,Lee Schwamm,Lucila Ohno-Machado,Hua Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AegisDx is a safety-oriented framework using role-specific LLM components, structured verification gates, and evidence retrieval to improve differential diagnosis and must-not-miss condition detection. It outperforms standalone LLMs on multiple benchmarks (Top-3 accuracy: 59.9% vs 52.1% on JAMA cases; 85.7% vs 68.6% on Annals of Emergency Medicine cases; must-not-miss capture: 78.0% vs 52.0%) and achieves higher physician-rated safety scores (4.55 vs 4.31 on a 5-point scale) in real-world ED note evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnostic error remains a major patient safety threat, and current LLM-based systems treat diagnosis as one-shot prediction without safeguards against missing high-risk conditions or verifying reasoning.

Method: AegisDx coordinates specialized LLM components via role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates to generate broad differential diagnoses, enforce must-not-miss screening, verify reasoning against medical evidence, and structure actionable next steps.

Result: Top-3 accuracy improvements: 59.9% vs 52.1% (JAMA), 62.7% vs 51.4% (NEJM), 85.7% vs 68.6% (Annals of Emergency Medicine). Must-not-miss capture: 78.0% vs 52.0%. Blinded physician evaluation on 43 real-world ED notes: composite safety score 4.55 vs 4.31 (adjusted p=2.1×10⁻⁴).

Conclusion: Engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework, rather than optimizing raw predictive accuracy alone, can provide safer, more transparent, and clinically meaningful decision support for acute care workflows.

Abstract: Diagnostic error is a major threat to patient safety, yet current large language model (LLM) systems often treat diagnosis as a one-shot prediction task, lacking safeguards against missed high-risk alternatives or rigorous verification of their reasoning. Here, we present AegisDx, a safety-oriented framework for hypothetico-deductive clinical reasoning. AegisDx coordinates specialized LLM components through role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates to generate broad differential diagnoses, enforce explicit screening for dangerous "must-not-miss" conditions, verify reasoning against grounded medical evidence, and structure actionable next steps. We evaluated AegisDx across three layers. On literature-derived case reports from NEJM and JAMA, with GPT-oss-120B as the shared backbone, Top-3 diagnostic accuracy was 59.9% versus 52.1% for the standalone LLM on JAMA cases and 62.7% versus 51.4% on NEJM cases. On cases from Annals of Emergency Medicine, Top-3 accuracy was 85.7% versus 68.6%; against physician-consensus must-not-miss diagnosis sets, AegisDx captured at least one such condition among its top three diagnoses in 78.0% of cases versus 52.0%. In a blinded physician evaluation of 43 real-world emergency department notes from the Yale New Haven Health System compared against GPT-5, AegisDx improved the physician-rated composite safety score from 4.31 to 4.55 on a 5-point scale (adjusted p = 2.1x10^-4), with qualitative gains in must-not-miss identification and reasoning safety. Our findings suggest that engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework, rather than optimizing raw predictive accuracy alone, can provide a safer, more transparent, and clinically meaningful layer of bedside decision support for acute care workflows.

</details>


### [112] [When LLMs Agree, Are They Right? Auditing Self-Consistency and Cross-Model Agreement as Confidence Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08065)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-as-judge evaluations assume that agreement among judges indicates correctness, but this paper demonstrates that agreement is unreliable—it can arise from shared bias, heuristics, or priors rather than truth. Across 53 runners, 265,000 samples on GPQA Diamond and AIME, agreement is a weak predictor (rho 0.20-0.59) and performs worst for consistent frontier models, which show high confidence but frequent errors (77% agreement on GPQA, 48% wrong). Self-consistency is a conditional proxy, not a standalone confidence metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The default in enterprise AI pipelines is to use LLM-as-judge evaluations, often with ensembles or panels, assuming that agreement among judges or self-consistency implies correctness. The authors challenge this assumption, noting that agreement can stem from shared biases, memorized heuristics, or position priors rather than truth.

Method: A large-scale cross-runner study with 53 runners, each drawing K=50 samples for overlapping cases across model tier, prompting, and scale on GPQA Diamond and AIME datasets, totaling 265,000 samples. Majority-correctness is used as the deployment label, and a hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap is applied for analysis. An exploratory cross-family check on three Claude tiers is also conducted.

Result: Agreement is a positive but weak predictor of correctness (rho 0.20-0.59, all positive under item-clustered resampling). Its usefulness is regime-dependent: best for unsaturated mid-tier models and compute allocation; worst for the most consistent frontier model, where agreement ≥0.8 on 77% of GPQA entries but 48% of those are wrong. The cross-family check confirms frontier over-confidence with recurring errors across providers.

Conclusion: Self-consistency is a conditional proxy for correctness, not a standalone confidence score. The assumption that agreement indicates reliability is flawed, especially for high-consistency frontier models. The authors publicly release de-identified per-run rows and answer distributions.

Abstract: LLM-as-judge (Zheng et al., 2023) is increasingly the default for evaluating AI systems in enterprise pipelines, often scaled to ensembles (Verga et al., 2024) or "mixture-of-experts" (Shazeer et al., 2017) panels of judges. These systems share a key assumption: that consistency -- agreement among judges, or among a model's own samples -- indicates correctness. We show this assumption is unreliable. Agreement is not accuracy: a model can agree with itself, and different models can agree with each other, out of shared bias, a memorized heuristic, or an option-position prior rather than truth. We ask when agreement is nonetheless a usable proxy, in a large-scale cross-runner study: 53 runners drew K=50 samples for assigned overlapping cases across comparisons of model tier, prompting, and scale on GPQA Diamond and AIME -- 265,000 samples. Using majority-correctness as the deployment label and a hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap, agreement is a positive but weak predictor (rho 0.20-0.59, all positive under item-clustered resampling) whose usefulness is regime-dependent: best for unsaturated mid-tier models and for allocating compute, and worst -- over-confident yet no more accurate -- for the most consistent frontier model (agreement >=0.8 on 77% of GPQA case-result entries, 48% of those wrong). An exploratory cross-family check on three Claude tiers shows the same frontier over-confidence, with confident errors recurring across providers above a marginal-preserving null. Self-consistency is thus a conditional proxy for correctness, not a standalone confidence score. We publicly release the de-identified per-run rows and answer distributions.

</details>


### [113] [Persuasion Attacks Can Decrease Effectiveness of CoT Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08066)
*Jennifer Za,Julija Bainiaksina,Nikita Ostrovsky,Tanush Chopra,Victoria Krakovna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoT monitoring can backfire against adversarial persuasion, increasing harmful action approval by 9.5%; model-diverse fact-checking (e.g., Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor + GPT-4.1 fact-checker) reduces approval by up to 45%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CoT monitoring is proposed as a safety mechanism for AI agents, but its robustness against persuasion-based jailbreaks—where natural-language arguments override model constraints—is underexplored.

Method: Designed an evaluation framework with 40 tasks and analyzed thousands of agent-monitor interactions under adversarial settings where agents argue for policy-violating proposals. Proposed a fact-checking monitoring framework pairing different model families.

Result: Monitor access to CoT reasoning increases harmful action approval by 9.5% on average. Model-diverse fact-checker/monitor pairing reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45% (vs. 6% with same model for both roles).

Conclusion: CoT monitoring alone is insufficient against adversarial persuasion; model-diverse fact-checking provides robust mitigation.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising safety mechanism for AI agents, based on the premise that visible reasoning traces can surface misaligned or deceptive behavior. While effective in standard scenarios, recent work highlights that LLMs remain vulnerable to persuasion-based jailbreaks, where natural-language arguments override model constraints. We stress-test whether this vulnerability extends to monitoring LLMs: can an adversarial agent persuade its CoT monitor to approve proposed actions that violate the monitor's policy? We design an evaluation framework with 40 tasks and analyze thousands of agent-monitor interactions, where agents are instructed to argue for policy-violating proposals. We find that in such adversarial settings, monitor access to the agent's CoT reasoning increases rather than decreases approval of harmful actions on average by 9.5%, as the scratchpad provides an additional persuasion channel. To address this, we introduce a fact-checking monitoring framework. We find that a fact-checker and monitor pairing from different model families, for example a Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor paired with a GPT-4.1 fact-checker, reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45%, compared to only 6%, when using the same model for both fact-checking and monitoring roles. Our results demonstrate that CoT monitoring alone may be insufficient against adversarial persuasion, and that model-diverse fact-checking provides a robust mitigation.

</details>


### [114] [PARA-PV: Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented PV Prediction Based on Frozen Foundation Model and Distribution Shift Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08079)
*Hang Fan,Weican Liu,Ying Lu,Dunnan Liu,Long Cheng,Wei Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PARA-PV is a Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented framework for PV power forecasting that integrates physical knowledge through historical patch retrieval, foundation model calibration, distribution shift correction, and physics-constrained loss, achieving accurate predictions across diverse operating regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PV power forecasting is challenging because generation is jointly shaped by weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and strict physical constraints; existing methods struggle to embed physical knowledge throughout the process.

Method: The framework consists of four components: (1) a physics-aware retrieval-augmented learner that retrieves historical patches matching current temporal shape, power level, PV state, and intra-day period; (2) a residual adapter that calibrates the base forecast against a frozen Chronos foundation model prior; (3) a physics-aware distribution shift correction module that adjusts for weather/diurnal regime changes via gated mean-shift and scale corrections; and (4) a physics-constrained loss that partitions samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes with adaptive reweighting.

Result: The proposed method achieves accurate PV power forecasting by embedding physical knowledge at every stage—retrieval, calibration, correction, and loss optimization—while the code is publicly available.

Conclusion: Integrating physical constraints throughout the forecasting pipeline (retrieval, calibration, distribution correction, and loss design) effectively handles regime-dependent PV dynamics and prevents dominant regular regimes from suppressing learning of operationally critical states.

Abstract: Accurate photovoltaic (PV) power forecasting is essential for reliable grid dispatch and renewable energy integration, yet it remains challenging because PV generation is jointly shaped by weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and strict physical constraints. We propose PARA-PV, a Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented framework that embeds physical knowledge throughout the forecasting process. The framework first encodes multivariate PV observations into patch-level representations and, through a physics-aware retrieval-augmented learner, retrieves historical patches and analog trajectories that are consistent with the current window in temporal shape, power level, PV operating state, and intra-day period; this yields a physically grounded base forecast. To supplement local memory with broader temporal knowledge, the base forecast is then calibrated against a frozen Chronos time-series foundation-model prior through a lightweight residual adapter, so that general temporal regularities are adapted to PV-specific dynamics without overriding the physically grounded prediction. Because residual conditional distribution shifts persist when weather and diurnal regimes change, a physics-aware distribution shift correction module subsequently adjusts the preliminary forecast using power, weather, timestamp, and day/night conditions, applying gated mean-shift and scale corrections selectively. Finally, a physics-constrained loss function partitions the samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes and adaptively reweights their error contributions, preventing the dominant regular regime from suppressing learning of operationally critical states. Our code is available at https://github.com/weican1103/PARA-PV.

</details>


### [115] [CausalDS: Benchmarking Causal Reasoning in Data-Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08093)
*Andrej Leban,Yuekai Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CausalDS is a benchmark evaluating LLMs on causal reasoning in data-science workflows, combining structural causal models, synthetic data, and natural-language stories across all three Pearl's causal rungs, with coding, tool use, abstention, and uncertainty quantification components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks either test symbolic causal reasoning without realistic data analysis, or test data analysis without principled causal structure. Current causal evaluation datasets are limited to curated examples with templatized variations rather than systematic generation of novel synthetic causal structures.

Method: Each benchmark instance is a scene with: (1) a sampled structural causal model (SCM) with generated observational data, (2) a synthetic natural-language story grounded in a realistic domain, optionally grounded in empirical distributions from real-world datasets. Tasks span all three Pearl's causal rungs, include data-science coding components requiring tool use, imperfect observations via an observation model, and first-class abstention scoring.

Result: The benchmark jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and tool use/coding in an integrated manner.

Conclusion: CausalDS provides a more comprehensive evaluation of LLM capabilities in causal reasoning within realistic data-science agentic workflows, addressing limitations of existing benchmarks that separate causal reasoning from practical data analysis.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as integrated data-science agents, combining abstract reasoning with advanced tool use. Yet the relevant benchmark landscape largely divides into symbolic causal reasoning benchmarks without realistic data analysis or data analysis benchmarks without a principled causal data-generating structure. Furthermore, existing causal evaluation datasets are often restricted to curated examples from existing sources, with diversity coming from limited templatized variations rather than from systematic generation of novel synthetic causal structures. We introduce CausalDS, a benchmark for evaluating causal reasoning in agentic data-science workflows. Each benchmark instance is a scene consisting of a sampled structural causal model (SCM) with generated observational data and an accompanying synthetic natural-language story grounded in a realistic domain. We optionally ground the composition of the benchmark components in empirical distributions obtained from real-world datasets, thus retaining empirical structure while reducing the "causal parrot" risk through completely synthetic generation. From each scene, we then derive tasks spanning all three of Pearl's rungs, with typical data-science prediction tasks appearing as Rung 1. Most tasks include a data science coding component, where the model typically needs to use several tools to arrive at the final answer due to the frequent presence of imperfect observations, which are generated by an observation model. Additionally, recognizing when a question admits no warranted answer and abstaining is treated as a first-class scored outcome. The benchmark thus jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and tool use/coding.

</details>


### [116] [Answer Set Programming Energised! End-to-End Neurosymbolic Reasoning and Learning with ASP and Energy Based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08136)
*Jakob Suchan,Julius Monsen,Salim Baloch,Mehul Bhatt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A modular integration of answer set programming with energy-based models enabling end-to-end neurosymbolic learning and reasoning with joint optimization in continuous latent space, supporting non-monotonic inference and background knowledge, demonstrated on MNIST, CLEVR, and MOT benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge neural perception and symbolic reasoning by enabling robust end-to-end training that incorporates declarative background knowledge, constraints, and non-monotonic inference within a continuous latent space optimization framework.

Method: Modular neurosymbolic integration of Answer Set Programming (ASP) with Energy-Based Models (EBM), providing joint optimization in continuous latent space with explicit ASP-based declarative semantics; builds upon answer set modulo theories and probabilistic logic frameworks.

Result: Demonstrated on MNIST for basic use, and evaluated on CLEVR (visual question answering) and MOT (multi-object tracking) benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed ASP-centric neurosymbolic framework enables robust, end-to-end training for dynamic domain applications involving perception and interaction, with practical implementation available.

Abstract: We present a general neurosymbolic reasoning and learning methodology based on a modular integration of answer set programming with an energy based model substrate. Key contributions are: (1) supporting joint optimisation in the continuous latent space through explicit ASP-based declarative semantics fully incorporating background knowledge, constraints, non-monotonic inference; and (2) advancing recent works at the interface of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories by providing a generalised model and practical platform for ASP-centric robust, end-to-end training for applications in dynamic domains (e.g., involving perception and interaction). We provide a practical implementation, and demonstrate basic use and application (with MNIST), and evaluate with the visual question-answering benchmark Clevr and the multi-object tracking benchmark MOT.

</details>


### [117] [Overthinking: Amplifying Reasoning Weights to Extract Learned Secrets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08173)
*Jack Hopkins,Dipika Khullar,Fabien Roger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "overthinking" - a method that amplifies reasoning in language models by extrapolating beyond reasoning-distilled model parameters. This technique surfaces hidden information (secrets, misalignment) up to 10x more frequently than standard reasoning models across various model sizes and settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black box auditing of language models may miss subtle forms of misalignment and hidden information, motivating the need for better methods to elicit such information during auditing.

Method: The overthinking model is defined as θ_Oα = θ_M + α(θ_R - θ_M) where α>1 amplifies reasoning beyond the pure reasoning model R. Layer-wise attenuation strategies selectively amplify reasoning without losing output quality/coherence.

Result: Overthinking models reveal hidden information across four experimental settings across 2B-32B model sizes. Reasoning amplification surfaces secrets or unintended behaviors up to 10x more frequently than the original reasoning model. The mechanism depends on secret type - some require perturbation along reasoning direction, others respond to any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

Conclusion: Reasoning amplification via overthinking can surface secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training, providing a more effective auditing tool for detecting hidden information in language models.

Abstract: Black box auditing of language models is an essential pre-deployment tool, but it may miss subtle forms of misalignment and hidden information. To better elicit hidden information during an auditing process, we introduce \emph{overthinking}: the process of using reasoning task vectors to amplify the propensity to think out loud of reasoning models. Given the parameters of a non-reasoning instruct model $M$ and reasoning-distilled model $R$, we define the \emph{overthinking model} as $\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{O}_α} = \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}} + α(\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{R}} - \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}})$, where $α> 1$ amplifies reasoning beyond the pure reasoning model $R$. Additionally, we introduce new layer-wise attenuation strategies that selectively amplify reasoning without losing quality and coherence of model outputs. We demonstrate that overthinking models are more likely to reveal hidden information across four experimental settings, across 2B-32B models. Our findings suggest that reasoning amplification may surface secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training up to $10\times$ more frequently than the original reasoning model. How secrets surface depends on the secret type: some require perturbation along the reasoning direction, while others yield to any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

</details>


### [118] [ASMR: Agentic Schema Generation for Ship Maintenance Report Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08177)
*Sohrab Namazi Nia,Amogh Dalal,Ning Sa,Peter Ly,Marti Zentmaier,Tomek Strzalkowski,Jay Miller,Rishi Singh,Senjuti Basu Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASMR is a modular agentic framework with two specialized agents (Field Generation Agent and Structural Optimizer Agent) that automatically discovers compact and informative schemas from historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, using adaptive multi-granularity clustering and reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the automatic schema generation problem for historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, aiming to capture essential information requirements of each report type.

Method: A modular agentic framework (ASMR) with two agents: (1) Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts via adaptive multi-granularity clustering; (2) Structural Optimizer Agent uses reinforcement learning to identify compact, informative, and non-redundant schemas.

Result: Preliminary results demonstrate promise of the proposed approach, with identified open research challenges at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

Conclusion: The generated schemas can guide report authors toward more complete, consistent, and actionable reports, though further research is needed.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the automatic schema generation problem: given a collection of historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, automatically discover compact and informative schemas that capture the essential information requirements of each report type. To address this challenge, we propose ASMR, a modular agentic framework consisting of two specialized agents. A Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts from historical narratives and generates candidate schema fields through adaptive multi-granularity clustering, while a Structural Optimizer Agent employs reinforcement learning to identify compact, informative, and non-redundant schema representations. The resulting schemas can guide report authors toward producing more complete, consistent, and actionable reports. Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the proposed approach and highlight several open research challenges at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

</details>


### [119] [A First-Principles Theory of Slow Thinking and Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08196)
*Hongkang Yang,Zhi-Qin John Xu,Feiyu Xiong,Weinan E*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents "active lifting," a mathematical theory that formally derives slow thinking (active perception) from first principles, encompassing the design, training, and inference of slow thinking LLMs through lifting and projecting probability distributions on observable and latent spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous mathematical formulation of thinking and perception (specifically slow thinking/active perception) from first principles, and to unify the design, training, and inference of slow thinking large language models within a formal theoretical framework.

Method: Proposes "active lifting" theory based on sampling latent sequences with an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty at maximum rate, using lifting and projection of probability distributions between observable and latent spaces, with simple function families (e.g., neural networks) to represent complex data distributions.

Result: Derives a large design space containing slow thinking models (static theory subspace), a representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy for upgrading models, an inference process with internal time axis, and a training objective resembling minimum-length coding and language invention. Technical by-products include a three-stage improvement pathway, unified encoder/generative model construction, a priori human-like visual representations, and a potential solution to policy collapse.

Conclusion: Active lifting characterizes the agency of perception and emergence of slow thinking formats, offering a principled foundation for building and improving slow thinking models across modalities.

Abstract: As part of a series on first-principles modeling of cognitive functions, this paper attempts to provide a mathematical formulation of thinking and perception. It formally derives slow thinking or more generally, active perception, and encompasses the design, training and inference of slow thinking large language models. Our starting point is the lifting and projection of probability distributions on the observable and latent spaces, with the objective of representing complex data distributions by simple function families such as neural networks. A theory called "active lifting" is proposed, based on the sampling of latent sequences and an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty with maximum rate. It derives a large design space, containing the slow thinking models in a subspace that we call the static theory. These models are positioned on the representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy induced by the static theory, and can be upgraded by climbing the two hierarchies. Active lifting further derives an inference process with an internal time axis, and a training objective that resembles minimum-length coding as well as the invention of languages. Thus, it characterizes the agency of perception, including the emergence of the slow thinking formats. Technical by-products of this theory include a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models, a unified approach to constructing encoders and generative models for all data modalities, a priori formation of human-like visual representations, and a possible solution to policy collapse.

</details>


### [120] [Playing ZendoWorld: Challenging AI Agents on Active Visual Concept Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08233)
*Sophia Koehler,Antonia Wüst,Inga Ibs,Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Wolfgang Stammer,Constantin Rothkopf,Kevin Ellis,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZendoWorld is a controlled interactive environment for evaluating agents' ability to infer logical rules from visual observations, propose informative experiments, and refine hypotheses. Evaluations of VLM, Bayesian, concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic agents reveal that label prediction accuracy doesn't imply rule recovery, perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks, and VLM agents fail at active information gathering—highlighting gaps compared to human inductive reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study the challenge of enabling intelligent systems to jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments to test them—a critical capability for scientific discovery and general intelligence.

Method: The authors propose ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment where agents must infer a logical rule about visual game observations, acquire information by proposing new scenes, and refine hypotheses based on feedback. They evaluate agents spanning pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods, and collect human data for comparison.

Result: (1) High label prediction accuracy does not imply rule recovery; (2) perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks across agent classes; (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments and fail to reduce hypothesis uncertainty actively; (4) Humans outperform agents, with a notable gap in inductive reasoning for complex rules.

Conclusion: ZendoWorld serves as an important benchmark for evaluating intelligent agents in active hypothesis-driven reasoning, identifying concrete avenues for improvement particularly in domains like scientific discovery.

Abstract: A central challenge in building intelligent systems is enabling agents to jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments to test them. To study this problem, we propose ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment in which agents must infer a logical rule about visual game observations, acquire information by proposing new scenes, and refine their hypotheses based on feedback from the game environment. We evaluate several agents spanning pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods. Our main findings are: (1) high accuracy in predicting labels for observed examples does not imply recovery of the underlying rule; (2) perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks for different agent classes; and (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to actively reduce hypothesis uncertainty. To compare these results, we collect human data on the task, which reveals a gap in inductive reasoning, particularly for more complex rules. Overall, ZENDOWORLD takes an important step toward evaluating intelligent agents and identifies concrete avenues for improvement, particularly in domains like scientific discovery.

</details>


### [121] [AutoPersonas: A Multi-Timescale Loop Engine for Open-Ended Persona Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08252)
*Mengchen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoPersonas introduces a multi-timescale life-environment engine (OSO loop) that separates environment-side Occurrences, Observations, and persona State to combat self-locking—a runtime failure where persona agents become trapped in repetitive, high-probability behavioral loops. The system reduces macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubles cumulative theme count through context-slice masking and per-sample divergence targeting, supporting that controlled divergence plus evidence-governed absorption can reduce self-locking while preserving identity continuity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term persona agents suffer from "self-locking": a runtime failure where locally plausible events keep occurring but the generated life collapses toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages—caused by model-level convergence to high-probability channels and system-level context gravity from memory, history, and environment summaries.

Method: AutoPersonas is a multi-timescale engine with an OSO (Occurrences-State-Observations) loop that separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State, admitting divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State/reachability changes. Evaluated via a three-year compressed simulation and an 8-model 40-day stress test (1,600 events) plus a same-runtime A/B test using context-slice masking and per-sample divergence targeting.

Result: Mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition of 95.2%-97.6% across models, all crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic macro-theme repetition 79.0%-88.0% across direct-loop runs. A/B testing reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. A juvenile-goblin fictional run reproduced the anti-fixation regime without real-world intrusions.

Conclusion: Separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity, supporting a bounded claim for this approach.

Abstract: Long-term persona agents must remain identifiable while adapting to new events, relationships, evidence, and social conditions. We identify self-locking as a runtime failure mode in continuing persona-life loops: locally plausible events keep appearing while the generated life collapses toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages. We trace this failure to model-level convergence toward high-probability behavioral channels and system-level context gravity from State, memory, history, and environment summaries. We introduce AutoPersonas, a multi-timescale life-environment engine for bounded persona-level recursive self-evolution. It separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Its OSO loop admits divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State or reachability changes. A three-year compressed simulation exposed environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, slow-change accumulation failures, recursive indecision, and weak relationship persistence. An eight-model 40-day stress test generated 1,600 events and found mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition of 95.2%-97.6%, with all models crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping found 79.0%-88.0% macro-theme repetition across all direct-loop runs. In a same-runtime 40-day A/B, context-slice masking plus per-sample divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. A juvenile-goblin fictional-world run reproduced the anti-fixation regime without hard real-world intrusions. These results support a bounded claim: separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity.

</details>


### [122] [Compete Then Collaborate: Frontier AI Teachers Build a Verifiable Curriculum to Improve a Coding Student Beyond Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08255)
*Miseong Shawn Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A compete-then-collaborate framework using four frontier AI teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) ranked by execution-based judge, where collaborative curriculum used as RLVR environment improves student coding performance (+49% relative gain on competition problems), while SFT on verified solutions degrades performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior multi-teacher knowledge distillation methods merge outputs without determining which frontier model teaches best, and often rely on an LLM judge biased toward its own outputs. The authors aim to address teacher ranking bias and explore effective knowledge transfer from multiple teachers to a smaller student model.

Method: Four frontier AI teachers are ranked head-to-head via execution-based judge (unit tests/stdio checks) with fairness controls; then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum. Student (Qwen2.5-Coder) is trained via SFT on verified solutions or RLVR using the collaborative curriculum. GRPO framework with reproducible on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10).

Result: (1) Under execution verification, all teachers solve standard problems near-perfectly (99-100%), but harder competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%), yet student-side results don't depend on teacher ranking. (2) SFT on verified solutions degrades performance (76.7%→72.7% on MBPP-test, 5.9%→2.9% on competition). (3) RLVR with collaborative curriculum improves competition performance (5.9%→8.8%, +49% relative gain).

Conclusion: The value of AI-teacher collaboration lies not in pooling answers to imitate, but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment where the student learns by doing. SFT on teacher solutions harms competent students while RLVR benefits from the collaborative curriculum.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly serve as teachers generating training data for smaller students. Prior multi-teacher knowledge distillation methods merge outputs without determining which frontier model teaches best, often relying on an LLM judge biased toward its own outputs. We introduce a compete-then-collaborate framework where four frontier AI teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked head-to-head by an execution-based judge (unit tests and stdin-stdout checks) with fairness controls, and then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for a student (Qwen2.5-Coder). We report three findings. (1) Under execution verification, all teachers solve standard problems near-perfectly after self-correction (99-100%) due to a saturation effect, but harder competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%); however, the robust student-side results do not depend on teacher ranking. (2) Imitation (SFT) on verified solutions does not improve, and can degrade, an already-competent student at 7B and 32B (e.g., from 76.7% to 72.7% on MBPP-test, and 5.9% to 2.9% on competition problems). (3) Using the same collaborative curriculum as a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) environment improves the student (from 5.9% to 8.8% peak on competition problems, a +49% relative gain), reversing SFT's direction. The value of AI-teacher collaboration lies not in pooling answers to imitate, but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment where the student learns by doing. We release a reproducible on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10) with framework patches for running GRPO on a bleeding-edge stack.

</details>


### [123] [MentalHospital: A Virtual Environment for Evaluating Psychiatric Clinical Encounters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08257)
*Yuming Yang,Xiao Sun,Yuanwei Zou,Zhengxiao Wu,Yun Chen,Jiang Zhong,Haoyang Zeng,Jingwang Huang,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MentalHospital is a virtual evaluation environment for LLM-based psychiatric clinical encounters, simulating the full S.O.A.P. workflow using 1,193 de-identified EHR cases. It introduces MentalEval, five domain-specific evaluators for assessing clinical quality. Benchmarking reveals that even the best LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM benchmarks evaluate isolated psychiatric tasks (e.g., dialogue, diagnosis) but fail to simulate complete psychiatric clinical encounters, leaving a gap in holistic assessment of LLM clinical competence.

Method: MentalHospital instantiates the S.O.A.P. workflow using skill-augmented standardized patients from 1,193 de-identified psychiatric EHR cases (76 disorders across ICD-11 categories). A dual-track protocol combines objective comparison against EHR references with subjective clinical process quality assessment. MentalEval uses five rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO domain-specific evaluators covering communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, and treatment appropriateness.

Result: 22 clinicians rated MentalHospital's clinical fidelity at 3.88/5. MentalEval achieves strong expert alignment with average QWK of 0.944. Benchmarking shows the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.

Conclusion: MentalHospital provides a comprehensive virtual environment for evaluating LLM-based psychiatric encounters, revealing a substantial performance gap between LLMs and clinicians, particularly in mental status assessment.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on isolated psychiatric tasks, including dialogue, diagnosis, and treatment planning, yet existing benchmarks rarely simulate complete psychiatric clinical encounters. We introduce $\textbf{MentalHospital}$, a virtual evaluation environment for LLM-based psychiatric clinical encounters. MentalHospital instantiates the Subjective Interviewing, Objective Examination, Diagnostic Assessment, and Treatment Planning (S.O.A.P.) workflow, using skill-augmented standardized patients constructed from 1,193 de-identified psychiatric electronic health record (EHR) cases spanning all major ICD-11 categories and 76 disorders. Each encounter is assessed through a dual-track protocol that combines objective comparison against EHR-derived references with subjective assessment of clinical process quality. To scale specialist judgment, we develop $\textbf{MentalEval}$, five domain-specific evaluators covering communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, and treatment appropriateness, trained with rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO. Survey responses from 22 clinicians support MentalHospital's clinical fidelity (3.88/5), while MentalEval achieves strong expert alignment with an average QWK of 0.944. Benchmarking shows that even the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.

</details>


### [124] [Different Teachers, Different Capabilities: Sub-1B On-Device Distillation for Structured Text Enrichment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08268)
*Vinay Kumar Chaganti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Distilling a large reasoning model (8B) into a small on-device model (0.6B) achieves 0.8s vs 39s per article, recovering 58% of the quality gap, but capabilities split by teacher type—reasoning teachers transfer writing quality while managed pipelines transfer label diversity, with a trade-off in grounding on thin-source articles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-volume structured extraction using large models is slow and costly per item; distilling the task into a small on-device model aims to achieve comparable output quality with a fraction of the latency and cost.

Method: Distilled an 8B reasoning teacher (deepseek-r1:8b) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B) using QLoRA with three seeds; added two teacher controls (same-size non-reasoning teacher, larger managed pipeline); evaluated with a blinded three-judge panel against the full article, plus few-shot prompting and constrained decoding baselines.

Result: Student runs 0.8s/article vs teacher's 39s; recovers 58% of base-to-teacher gap on summary quality; beats constrained decoding by +16.8 points and few-shot by +4.9. Same-size non-reasoning teacher yields no improvement. Reasoning teacher transfers writing quality; managed pipeline transfers label diversity; instruction teacher's students are more grounded on thin-source articles (74 vs 55 faithful) but this is a non-significant trend.

Conclusion: No single engine dominates all fields; the deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment that accounts for trade-offs between latency, summary quality, label diversity, and factual grounding.

Abstract: High-volume structured extraction pays a large model's latency on every item, so distilling the task into a small on-device model is attractive: comparable output at a fraction of the time and cost. We measure what that distillation actually delivers, per sub-task. Each news article is mapped to one JSON object with a short summary and five categorical labels. We distill an 8B reasoning teacher (deepseek-r1:8b) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B; QLoRA, three seeds), and add two teacher controls: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. A blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel scores every arm against the full article, alongside two non-distillation baselines, few-shot prompting and constrained decoding. The student runs at about 0.8 s per article against the teacher's 39 s, and recovers 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality, beating its primary baseline (constrained decoding) by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by a secondary +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher trains a student no better than the untuned base, so the summary gain follows from the teacher's reasoning nature rather than its scale. Capabilities then split by teacher: the reasoning teacher transfers writing quality and the managed pipeline transfers label diversity, while a same-size instruction teacher's students stay more grounded on the 22 short, thin-source articles in the 93-item test set (74 versus 55 faithful), where the reasoning-lineage student fabricates. That grounding difference is a consistent ordering rather than a significant aggregate effect, and the subgroup is small, so we report it as a direction. Because no single engine wins every field, the deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment.

</details>


### [125] [PolyUQuest: Verifiable Structure-Aware Web RAG over Heterogeneous Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08269)
*Ying Liu,Yi Ye,Quanyu Feng,Mingxi Ye,Mingtao Zhang,Haoyang Li,Chen Jason Zhang,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PolyUQuest is a structure-aware web RAG framework that uses a heterogeneous graph (unifying hyperlinks, DOM hierarchy, and entity-relations) with a two-tier router for three retrieval modes, achieving verifiable answers with higher correctness, coverage, and faithfulness while using fewer LLM tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems treat web pages as flat text, losing the structural and semantic signals encoded in HTML, which limits answer verifiability and retrieval quality.

Method: A heterogeneous graph unifying hyperlink topology, DOM hierarchy, and entity-relation knowledge; a two-tier router dispatches queries to direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, or multi-hop entity reasoning; each cited block carries source page, heading path, and entity links for full verifiability.

Result: Evaluated on PolyU's 4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, and 37,680 relations; outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query.

Conclusion: PolyUQuest enables verifiable, structure-aware web RAG and is being prepared for deployment as a student-facing QA service at PolyU.

Abstract: Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat web pages as flat text, losing the structural and semantic signals encoded in HTML. We present PolyUQuest, a verifiable, structure-aware web RAG framework built on a heterogeneous graph that unifies hyperlink topology between pages, DOM hierarchy within pages, and entity-relation knowledge across pages. A two-tier router dispatches each query to one of three retrieval modes matched to its structural need, including direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, and multi-hop entity reasoning. Every answer is fully verifiable, as each cited block carries its source page, heading path, and entity links so that users can trace any claim back to its structural evidence. We evaluate on the official websites of the Hong Kong Polytechnic University (PolyU), comprising 4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, and 37,680 relations, together with a multi-type evaluation benchmark. PolyUQuest outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness, while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query. The demonstration provides an interactive interface for inspecting cited answers, comparing retrieval traces across routing modes, and exploring evidence graph paths. PolyUQuest is being prepared for deployment as a student-facing QA service at PolyU.

</details>


### [126] [Understanding Axes of Difficulty For Long Context Tasks Via PredicateLongBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08284)
*Siddhartha Jain,Ameya Velingker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PredicateLongBench stress-tests LLM long-context reasoning by requiring models to find the longest contiguous subsequence satisfying given predicates, with systematic difficulty scaling across multiple axes, revealing frontier models' limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context benchmarks measure average-case performance but lack systematic ways to probe model behavior as task difficulty scales along various axes; many benchmarks are saturated or lack robustness.

Method: The benchmark defines tasks where models must identify the longest contiguous subsequence meeting predicates (e.g., lexicographic ordering) from a broader class. Two pipelines are provided: fully synthetic (random word-like strings) and real-world (words sampled from natural documents preserving distributional properties).

Result: Frontier models struggle as difficulty scales along multiple axes, demonstrating the benchmark's utility in revealing limitations of current long-context capabilities.

Conclusion: PredicateLongBench fills a gap in long-context evaluation by enabling systematic stress-testing across difficulty axes; tasks are conceptually simple and do not require LLM-based generation or judges.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated rapidly improving long-context capabilities, prompting a wave of benchmarks designed to evaluate them. However, existing long-context evaluations - from Needle-in-a-Haystack (NIAH) tests to more recent multi-hop reasoning and summarization tasks - predominantly measure average-case performance, and many are either saturated or lack robustness. Notably absent is a systematic way to probe how models perform as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along various axes. We address this gap by proposing PredicateLongBench, a benchmark that stress-tests long-context reasoning by asking models to identify the longest contiguous subsequence of words in a long input that satisfies given predicates/constraints (e.g., lexicographic ordering), drawn from a broader predicate class. The central innovation of our benchmark is the identification and systematic exploration of multiple different axes of difficulty which test multiple aspects of long context understanding. We provide two complementary generation pipelines - a fully synthetic setup using random word-like strings, and a real-world setup that samples words from natural documents while preserving their distributional properties. We find that frontier models struggle to perform well as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along our axes, demonstrating the utility of our benchmark in understanding the limitations of current long-context capabilities. Furthermore, the tasks in PredicateLongBench, though challenging, are conceptually simple and do not require LLM-based generations or judges.

</details>


### [127] [Psychological Competence as a Missing Dimension in AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08285)
*Marcos Economides,Paul M. Sacher,Samuel Salzer,Alexis Michelle Abellar,Fendi Tsim,Antoine Ferrère*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "psychological competence" as a missing dimension in AI evaluation for human-facing systems, defining it as the capacity to support user cognition, emotional interpretation, and behavioral decision-making through appropriate interaction properties like framing, tone, and uncertainty handling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation frameworks focus on technical performance (accuracy, robustness, reasoning, policy compliance), which is insufficient for systems that interact directly with users as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions. These interactions shape user reasoning, emotions, beliefs, trust, and decisions, yet psychological effects are rarely assessed directly.

Method: Drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research, the authors define the construct of psychological competence, clarify its boundaries, and outline core domains. They propose assessment via scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods rather than a specific benchmark.

Result: A conceptual framework for psychological competence is established, including interaction properties such as framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance as key components for supporting user cognition, emotional interpretation, and behavioral decision-making.

Conclusion: Psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators concerned with the real-world effects of human-facing AI systems.

Abstract: Current AI evaluation frameworks focus primarily on technical performance, including accuracy, robustness, reasoning ability, and policy compliance. These measures remain essential, but they are not sufficient for systems that interact directly with users through natural language. Human-facing AI systems are increasingly used as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions. In these roles, their responses can shape how users reason, interpret emotions, form beliefs, calibrate trust, and make decisions. The relevant unit of evaluation is therefore not only the model, but the human-AI interaction. This paper introduces psychological competence as a missing dimension in AI evaluation. We define psychological competence as the capacity of a human-facing AI system to support user cognition, emotional interpretation, and behavioral decision-making in ways that are appropriate to the user, context, and purpose of the interaction. This includes interaction properties such as framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance. Existing evaluation approaches capture parts of this problem but rarely assess these psychological effects directly. Drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research, we outline a conceptual framework for psychological competence and its core domains. Rather than proposing a specific benchmark, we define the construct, clarify its boundaries, and describe how it may be assessed through scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods. We argue that psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators concerned with the real-world effects of human-facing AI systems.

</details>


### [128] [INTENT: An LSTM Framework for Vehicle Intention Prediction in Intersection Scenarios with Comprehensive Ablation Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08316)
*Logine M. Zaki,Catherine M. Elias*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes INTENT, an LSTM-based framework that predicts vehicle intentions (straight, left turn, right turn) at intersections 2 seconds ahead, achieving 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vehicle intention prediction is critical for autonomous vehicle safety, especially in complex scenarios like intersections, roundabouts, and emergency situations where early prediction enables timely evasive actions to prevent catastrophes or minimize damage.

Method: The INTENT framework employs an LSTM model to predict vehicle intention at intersections, forecasting whether vehicles will go straight, turn left, or turn right, 2 seconds before the event occurs. The model is evaluated through various experiments and ablation studies on the InD dataset.

Result: The proposed INTENT framework achieves 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset.

Conclusion: The INTENT framework demonstrates highly accurate vehicle intention prediction at intersections, which can enhance autonomous vehicle safety and potentially improve trajectory prediction through intention-conditioned approaches.

Abstract: Vehicle intention prediction is a pivotal aspect in the agility and safety of autonomous vehicles in all driving scenarios; if genuine enhancement of autonomous vehicles are required, we need to make them adopt human interpretation of driver's intention especially in cases that require a lot of human interaction as well as complex driving behaviors like the ones at intersections, roundabouts and emergency cases such as sudden stops where vehicle intention prediction helps in taking the correct evasive action within a real time period where every second of action makes an impact and can prevent a catastrophe from taking place. In the worst case, it helps minimize the damage and make safety a priority. Intention prediction can also be used to enhance trajectory prediction (intention conditioned trajectory prediction). In this study, The INTENT framework is proposed using LSTM model to predict the vehicle's intention at intersections 2 seconds ahead of the event occurrence to predict whether the cars in intersections are going straight, turning left, or turning right. Various model experiments and ablation study are thoroughly tested on InD dataset achieving 99.71% accuracy.

</details>


### [129] [Blind-Spots-Bench: Evaluating Blind Spots in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08317)
*Matteo Santelmo,Xiuying Wei,Israa Fakih,Felix Bauer,Juan Garcia Giraldo,Chengkun Li,Etienne Bamas,Emmanuel Abbé*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces blind-spots-bench, a benchmark of 235 human-simple but AI-hard tasks, revealing that closed-source models outperform open-weight models by ~10% on these blind spots even when comparable on standard benchmarks, and no single model excels across all task types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks fail to capture persistent blind spots in modern AI models—tasks that humans find trivial (e.g., manipulating a string or drawing a dog with five legs) but current systems struggle with, leading to an overestimation of model capabilities.

Method: The authors collected raw questions from students in an AI course, cleaned and annotated them with structured reference solutions, proposed a task taxonomy, and developed an automated grading pipeline to evaluate language, vision-language, and image-generation models on the resulting 235-sample benchmark.

Result: Closed-source frontier models outperform open-weight models by approximately 10% on blind-spots-bench despite comparable performance on existing benchmarks; no single model dominates all task types; some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models.

Conclusion: blind-spots-bench serves as a valuable diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete weaknesses in modern AI models that are not captured by standard benchmarks.

Abstract: Modern AI models achieve strong performance on many established benchmarks, yet they still fail on tasks that humans find almost trivial, such as manipulating a string or drawing a dog with five legs. These examples suggest that existing benchmarks may under-measure persistent blind spots in current systems. We introduce $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$, a benchmark designed to expose such blind spots through tasks that appear simple for humans but remain challenging for modern AI. We collect raw questions from students in an AI course, clean and annotate them with structured reference solutions, and propose a task taxonomy tailored to the resulting dataset of 235 samples. We further develop an automated grading pipeline to evaluate a wide range of models, including open-weight and closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation models.
