Our analysis on $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ reveals that closed-source frontier models can substantially outperform open-weight models with even $\approx10\%$ gap, even when they attain comparable performance on existing benchmarks. A more fine-grained analysis shows that no single model dominates across all task types, and that some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models. These results highlight the value of $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ as a diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete weaknesses in current modern models.

</details>


### [130] [MobiDiff: Semantic-Aware Multi-Channel Discrete Diffusion for Human Mobility Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08357)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ximiao Li,Taichi Liu,Desheng Zhang,Yuan Tian,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MobiDiff is an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework for generating synthetic human mobility data, directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons (spatial, activity, temporal) without costly interpolation or latent trace pipelines. It uses structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to capture trajectory patterns and within-event dependencies, achieving 5.3× faster inference than GeoGen while preserving key mobility statistics on real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world mobility data are costly to collect and difficult to share due to privacy concerns. Existing diffusion-based methods rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal traces, limiting their ability to natively model discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures.

Method: MobiDiff is an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework that decomposes each human check-in event into spatial, activity, and temporal channels, and employs structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to jointly capture trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies.

Result: On three real-world datasets (Atlanta, Boston, Seattle), MobiDiff preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions while remaining competitive across broader mobility statistics. It is much faster than state-of-the-art methods, e.g., 5.3× faster than GeoGen on average during inference.

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion offers an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data generation, avoiding costly interpolation and coarse-to-fine pipelines used in prior work.

Abstract: Human mobility data are essential for transportation optimization, urban planning, and resource allocation, yet real-world mobility data are costly to collect and difficult to share due to privacy concerns. Recent diffusion-based methods have shown promise in synthesizing realistic mobility patterns, but they typically rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal traces, limiting their ability to natively model discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures. To address this issue, we introduce MobiDiff, an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework that efficiently generates mobility data by directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons, avoiding the costly interpolation, latent trace construction, and coarse-to-fine realization pipelines widely used in existing diffusion-based methods. Specifically, MobiDiff decomposes each human check-in event into spatial, activity, and temporal channels, and employs structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to jointly capture trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies. We evaluate generation fidelity, privacy-preserving, and efficiency on three large-scale real-world datasets from Atlanta, Boston, and Seattle. Results show that MobiDiff effectively preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions while remaining competitive across broader mobility statistics; it is also much faster than state-of-the-art methods, e.g., 5.3$\times$ faster than GeoGen on average during inference. These findings suggest that discrete diffusion offers an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data generation.

</details>


### [131] [FedOPAL: One-Shot Federated Learning via Analytic Visual Prompt Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08368)
*Lingyu Qiu,Daniela Annunziata,Stefano Izzo,Fabio Giampaolo,Francesco Piccialli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedOPAL uses visual prompts as feature rectifiers with local proximal constraints to fix feature manifold misalignment in analytical federated learning under non-IID data, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy with zero server-side training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Communication bandwidth is a core bottleneck in federated learning. While one-shot federated learning reduces communication rounds, existing methods suffer from high server-side computational costs and hyperparameter sensitivity. Analytical federated learning offers gradient-free aggregation but fails under non-IID data due to static feature assumptions causing feature manifold misalignment.

Method: FedOPAL adapts visual prompts as feature rectifiers with local proximal constraints to actively correct heterogeneous data feature distributions into a linearly separable space, satisfying the theoretical assumptions of analytical federated learning.

Result: FedOPAL significantly outperforms original analytical methods on several benchmarks and achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative methods while maintaining zero server-side training costs.

Conclusion: FedOPAL provides a new engineering paradigm for efficient large model collaboration on the edge by resolving the non-IID data contradiction in analytical federated learning without sacrificing computational efficiency.

Abstract: With the widespread deployment of basic models in edge intelligence, communication bandwidth has become a core bottleneck restricting the scalability of federated learning. Although one-shot federated learning alleviates this problem by minimizing communication rounds, existing iterative fine-tuning or knowledge distillation methods still face challenges such as high server-side computational costs and hyperparameter sensitivity. Analytical federated learning achieves efficient gradientfree aggregation using least-squares closed-form solutions, but in environments with non-independent and identically distributed data, its static feature assumptions fail, leading to feature manifold misalignment and severely impairing model performance. To address this contradiction, this paper proposes the FedOPAL framework. This framework adapts the visual prompts as feature rectifiers, actively correcting the feature distribution of heterogeneous data to a linearly separable space by applying local proximal constraints, thereby satisfying the theoretical assumptions of analytical federated learning. Experimental results show that FedOPAL not only significantly outperforms the original analytical methods on several benchmarks, but also achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative methods while maintaining zero server-side training costs, providing a new engineering paradigm for efficient collaboration of large models on the edge.

</details>


### [132] [Towards Mechanistically Understanding Why Memorized Knowledge Fails to Generalize in Large Language Model Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08393)
*Lu Dai,Ziyang Rao,Yili Wang,Hanqing Wang,Hao Liu,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit a Knowing-Using Gap where they memorize new facts but fail to use them for reasoning; this is caused by knowledge-circuit misalignment where memorized representations aren't routed to effective computation layers, and a simple heuristic can recover 58-75% of the generalization gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formalize and understand the critical failure mode where LLMs memorize new facts during fine-tuning but cannot use them for downstream reasoning tasks.

Method: Proposes self-patching, a novel intervention technique that identifies activation locations where relocating representations improves failed generalization; uses this to monitor spatial permeation dynamics of knowledge internally.

Result: Confirms the knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis; a simple heuristic strategy recovers 58-75% of the oracle headroom in generalization failure, with cross-domain robustness.

Conclusion: The Knowing-Using Gap stems from internal representations that are memorized but misrouted to computation-ineffective layers, and can be partially mitigated via targeted intervention.

Abstract: Fine-tuning LLMs to inject new knowledge faces a critical challenge: LLMs can quickly memorize new facts, yet fail to use them for downstream reasoning tasks. We formalize this failure as the \textit{\textbf{Knowing--Using Gap}}, characterized by an accuracy gap and a temporal lag between memorization and generalization. To understand this phenomenon, we fine-tune LLMs with unseen knowledge and monitor the spatial permeation dynamics of the knowledge internally using a novel intervention technique called self-patching. Self-patching identifies activation locations where relocating representations substantially improves failed generalization cases. These results are consistent with a knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis: memorized representations can exist internally but may not be routed to computation-effective layers. To demonstrate the practicality of this diagnostic finding, we design a simple heuristic strategy which recovers 58--75\% of the oracle headroom in generalization failure. Experiments are done cross-domain for the robustness of this finding.

</details>


### [133] [Game Theory Driven Multi-Agent Framework Mitigates Language Model Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08403)
*Runzhe Liu,Biquan Bie,Zihao Wang,Yuchao Ma,Yexin Liu,Xinghai Li,Harry Yang,Wenbo Yang,Jinzhe Cao,Shengyang Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework integrating Bayesian and game theory principles, synthesizes high-quality data to train OmniChem (7B model), which achieves GPT-4o mini parity with 79.46% fewer hallucinations than its base architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lightweight LLMs in rule-based scientific domains suffer from pattern mimicry and hallucinations because they fail to internalize axiomatic reasoning.

Method: G-Frame combines Bayesian and team game principles to create an automated closed-loop for synthesizing chain-of-thought data (363,045 CoTs, 199,589 QA pairs), then trains a 7B model (OmniChem) to internalize domain constraints through structured reasoning.

Result: OmniChem matches GPT-4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench, achieves a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations relative to its base architecture, and demonstrates advanced molecular design and synthesis planning capabilities.

Conclusion: Adaptive multi-agent frameworks can overcome inherent reasoning deficiencies in small LLMs, providing a scalable paradigm for accelerated knowledge discovery in specialized scientific domains.

Abstract: The application of lightweight Large Language Models in rule-based scientific domains remains severely limited by their tendency to mimic linguistic patterns rather than reproduce axiomatic reasoning, causing frequent hallucinations. Here, we show that G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework integrating Bayesian and team game principles, establishes an automated closed-loop for high-quality data synthesis and model training. By forcing the internalization of domain constraints through structured reasoning, we synthesized a specialized corpus of 363,045 chains-of-thought and 199,589 question-answer pairs. The resulting 7B model OmniChem achieves performance parity with GPT 4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench while exhibiting a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations relative to its base architecture. We further demonstrate the advanced capabilities of OmniChem in molecular design and synthesis planning. This work establishes a scalable paradigm utilizing adaptive multi-agents to overcome inherent reasoning deficiencies, offering a feasible pathway for accelerating knowledge discovery in specialized scientific fields.

</details>


### [134] [OmniFood-Bench: Evaluating VLMs for Nutrient Reasoning and Personalized Health Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08423)
*Qian Jiang,Zhecheng Shi,Jingpu Yang,Zirui Song,Miao Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniFood-Bench is a benchmark that evaluates VLMs on food understanding across basic perception, quantitative reasoning, and safety-critical advisory tasks, revealing a "Semantic-Physical Gap" where models excel at dish naming but fail at mass estimation and give dangerous advice for diabetic profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks only evaluate coarse-grained food classification, failing to assess the complex reasoning chain needed for real-world dietary management — from identifying hidden ingredients to estimating physical mass and synthesizing safety-critical medical advice.

Method: The benchmark is constructed from the MM-Food-100K dataset and evaluates VLMs across three progressive capabilities: Basic Perception (Ingredients & Cooking Methods), Quantitative Reasoning (Portion Size & Nutritional Profiling), and Safety-Critical Advisory (Disease-Specific Recommendations). Six state-of-the-art VLMs including gpt-5.1, gemini-3-flash, and qwen3-vl-8B were evaluated.

Result: Models achieve near-human accuracy in dish naming but exhibit catastrophic failure in mass estimation and frequently hallucinate benign advice for high-risk diabetic profiles, revealing a "Semantic-Physical Gap."

Conclusion: This work establishes a rigorous standard for trustworthiness in autonomous agents deployed for public health, highlighting the critical gap between semantic understanding and physical reasoning in VLMs.

Abstract: The rapid integration of Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) into critical infrastructure promises to revolutionize
  personalized healthcare and dietary management. However, in the domain of food systems, autonomous agents face a
  unique and persistent challenge: the "Systemic Information Asymmetry" between visual appearance and intrinsic
  nutritional composition. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on coarse-grained classification tasks, such as food
  category recognition, which fail to evaluate the intricate reasoning chain required for real-world dietary management
  -- specifically, the ability to traverse from identifying hidden ingredients to estimating physical mass, and finally
  synthesizing safety-critical medical advice. In this paper, we introduce OmniFood-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark
  constructed from the MM-Food-100K dataset. Unlike previous works, OmniFood-Bench evaluates VLMs across three
  progressive capabilities: Basic Perception (Ingredients & Cooking Methods), Quantitative Reasoning (Portion Size &
  Nutritional Profiling), and Safety-Critical Advisory (Disease-Specific Recommendations). We evaluate six
  state-of-the-art VLMs, including gpt-5.1, gemini-3-flash, and qwen3-vl-8B. Our extensive experiments reveal a
  startling "Semantic-Physical Gap": while models achieve near-human accuracy in naming dishes, they exhibit
  catastrophic failure in mass estimation and frequently hallucinate benign advice for high-risk diabetic profiles. This
  work establishes a rigorous standard for trustworthiness in autonomous agents deployed for public health. The code
  and datasets are available in: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OmniFood-Bench-7D0B

</details>


### [135] [Applying JEPA-Style Predictive Learning to JA4-Derived Network Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08465)
*Javier Izquierdo,Aygul Zagidullina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JA4-JEPA applies JEPA-style self-supervised learning to network fingerprints (JA4, JA4H, JA4S, JA4X) using latent prediction instead of input reconstruction. Trained on ~397K samples from JA4DB and CIC-IDS-2017, it achieves 0.9899 cosine similarity and 0.9220 kNN accuracy on protocol-family classification, demonstrating effective embeddings even with incomplete view overlap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether JEPA-style predictive learning (latent prediction without input reconstruction) can produce useful representations from compact network fingerprints (JA4-derived fields), especially under the practical constraint of incomplete view overlap across data sources.

Method: A Transformer-based model (JA4-JEPA) trained with JEPA objective on JA4, JA4H, JA4S, and JA4X subfields from JA4DB and CIC-IDS-2017 (~397K samples). Representations are evaluated using frozen kNN probe on protocol-family classification (TLS, DNS, SSH).

Result: On 39,416 heldout samples, the model achieves cosine similarity of 0.9899 and kNN accuracy of 0.9220 for protocol-family classification.

Conclusion: JEPA-style predictive learning is effective for JA4-derived network fingerprints, producing useful embeddings even when training data has incomplete view overlap across sources.

Abstract: I-JEPA and V-JEPA learn by matching latent predictions to target encoder outputs rather than regenerating the original input, and this has worked well for images and video. We explore whether the same objective works for compact network fingerprints. We built JA4-JEPA, a Transformer-based model trained on JA4, JA4H, JA4S, and JA4X subfields drawn from JA4DB and CIC-IDS- 2017. The training data combines roughly 397K samples from both sources, though no single sample contains all four view families. We evaluated the learned representations with a frozen kNN probe on protocol-family classification across TLS, DNS, and SSH. On 39,416 heldout samples the model achieved a cosine similarity of 0.9899 and a kNN accuracy of 0.9220. These results indicate that JEPA-style predictive learning can produce useful embeddings from JA4-derived fingerprints, even with incomplete view overlap across sources.
  Keywords: JA4, network fingerprinting, JEPA, predictive representation learning, self-supervised learning

</details>


### [136] [Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) for Adaptive Semantic Modeling in Biomedical Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08490)
*Bharathwaj Vijayakumar,Sahana K. Varadaraju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DATGR is a lightweight framework that dynamically rewires co-occurrence edges in biomedical concept graphs to capture semantic drift over time, achieving ~0.066 AUROC improvement over static baselines while maintaining computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical language evolves rapidly, causing static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs to lose semantic fidelity over time, which degrades performance in retrieval and knowledge discovery tasks.

Method: Proposes Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR), which dynamically updates co-occurrence edges using a logistic update rule applied to edge weights, avoiding full embedding retraining for each time slice.

Result: Achieved mean AUROC improvement of ~0.066 (0.699 vs. 0.633) over static baseline on BIOMRC; AUPRC remained comparable (0.738 vs. 0.744), indicating improved recall without precision loss.

Conclusion: Edge-level drift-aware adaptation effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text while remaining computationally efficient and interpretable.

Abstract: Biomedical language evolves rapidly as new discoveries emerge, causing traditional text models to lose semantic fidelity over time. Static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs cannot capture such evolution, leading to performance degradation in retrieval and knowledge discovery tasks. This paper introduces a Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) framework that models concept evolution by dynamically updating co-occurrence edges based on estimated semantic drift. Instead of retraining embeddings for each time slice, DATGR performs lightweight, feedback-driven rewiring using a logistic update rule applied to edge weights. Evaluated on the Biomedical Multi-Relation Corpus (BIOMRC), the method achieved a mean Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (AUROC) improvement of approximately 0.066 absolute difference (0.699 vs. 0.633) over a static baseline. Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC) remained comparable (0.738 vs. 0.744), showing that drift-aware adaptation enhances link-prediction recall without a loss in precision. These results demonstrate that edge-level adaptation effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text while remaining computationally efficient and interpretable.

</details>


### [137] [AI-guided stimuli discovery and generation to optimize facial emotion perception studies in autism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08533)
*Kushin Mukherjee,Na Yeon Kim,Maren Wehrheim,Ralph Adolphs,Kohitij Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autistic-neurotypical differences in facial emotion perception are concentrated in a small subset of expressions. Using AI models to select and transform face stimuli, researchers amplified or reduced group behavioral differences, demonstrating a framework for discovering population-specific perceptual patterns beyond traditional averaged assays.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies on facial emotion perception differences between autistic and neurotypical adults yield inconsistent results. The authors hypothesized that variability stems from image-level sparsity—differences are concentrated in specific diagnostic expressions rather than uniformly distributed—and sought a framework to systematically identify and manipulate such stimuli.

Method: Trained population-specific artificial neural network models to predict image-level emotion judgments for autistic vs. neurotypical participants. Used models to select novel faces predicted to maximize group separation (validated in an independent cohort). Then used models with a generative adversarial network (GAN) to transform diagnostic images toward greater predicted group agreement (validated in phenotype-matched subjects).

Result: Model-selected images produced larger behavioral differences than matched random images in an independent cohort. GAN-transformed images reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched originals in phenotype-matched validation.

Conclusion: The study establishes a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences, moving beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized assays that identify when neurodivergent perception diverges or converges.

Abstract: Understanding perceptual differences between autistic and neurotypical adults requires behavioral assays that are sensitive, reliable, and mechanistically informative. Facial emotion perception is a useful test case because group differences have been reported, but findings vary across studies. Here we show that this variability may reflect image-level sparsity: autistic-neurotypical differences in emotion judgments were concentrated in a small subset of diagnostic facial expressions rather than spread uniformly across stimuli. We trained population-specific artificial neural network models to predict image-level judgments for autistic and neurotypical participants, then used these models to select novel faces predicted to maximize group separation. In an independent cohort, model-selected images produced larger behavioral differences than matched random images. We then used the same models with a generative adversarial network to transform diagnostic images toward greater predicted group agreement. In phenotype-matched validation, synthesized images reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched originals. These results establish a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences. More broadly, they show how behavioral phenotyping can move beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized assays that identify the conditions under which neurodivergent perception diverges or converges.

</details>


### [138] [CommuniWave:A Machine Learning Model for Quantifying the Degree of Temporary Informal Behavior in Urban Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08554)
*Hongye Yang,Shien Liu,Zhihao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CommuniWave is a machine learning model that detects and quantifies informal behaviors in urban communities via street video analysis, generating DIB fluctuation charts to support dynamic monitoring and refined decision-making for enhancing territorial resilience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban planners need better tools to understand informal resident behaviors because top-down community planning often conflicts with actual usage patterns, and existing approaches lack quantitative metrics for these behaviors.

Method: CommuniWave integrates three components: (1) Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2 for behavior detection, (2) a self-developed YOLOv10 model (YLX) for object detection, and (3) Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest for evaluation. The system processes street videos to generate Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB) fluctuation charts.

Result: The model generates DIB fluctuation charts from street videos, enabling dynamic monitoring of informal behaviors in urban communities.

Conclusion: CommuniWave facilitates data-driven, refined decision-making for urban managers, helping enhance overall community resilience by quantifying previously unmeasured informal behaviors.

Abstract: For urban managers and designers, improving the functional attributes of urban communities to enhance territorial resilience in the face of complexity and uncertainty is crucial. Currently, community planning often follows a top-down approach and lacks effective metrics to quantify informal behaviors of residents, leading to frequent conflicts with original plans. This study introduces CommuniWave, a machine learning model designed to efficiently detect and quantify the Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB) in urban communities. The model integrates a Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2, a self-developed YOLOv10 model (YLX), and a Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest. Ultimately, by generating DIB fluctuation charts from street videos, the model facilitates dynamic monitoring, supporting urban managers in making refined decisions to enhance the overall resilience of communities.

</details>


### [139] [SHAP-Weighted Cross-Modal Expert Fusion for Emotion and Sentiment Recognition: Evidence and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08573)
*Adis Alihodzic,Selma Skopljakovic Hubljar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper revisits XAI-guided adaptive fusion (XGAF) for multimodal emotion/sentiment recognition, focusing on how SHAP attribution reduction methods (mean-abs, median-abs, sum-abs) interact with expert feature dimensionalities. Sum-abs reduction preserves cross-modal expert contributions and nearly matches or slightly exceeds early fusion performance on MELD (0.5983 vs 0.6018) and CMU-MOSEI (0.6519 vs 0.6485), while significantly outperforming late fusion. The main gain comes from cross-modal experts, particularly the trimodal expert, rather than per-sample routing complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the trade-off between early fusion (accurate but monolithic) and late fusion (modular but losing cross-modal interactions) by revisiting XAI-guided adaptive fusion and analyzing how SHAP attribution reduction strategies affect fusion when experts have unequal feature dimensionalities.

Method: Tree-based mixture of unimodal and cross-modal experts with sample-level weights derived from TreeSHAP attribution magnitudes. Three SHAP reduction methods (mean-abs, median-abs, sum-abs) are compared, with sum-abs preserving total attribution mass. Evaluated on MELD (7-class emotion) and CMU-MOSEI (3-class sentiment) with Transformer and other face-sequence aggregators.

Result: On MELD: sum-abs XGAF (0.5983 WF1) nearly matches early fusion (0.6018), significantly outperforming late fusion (0.4598, p<0.0001). McNemar test p=1.000 vs early fusion. On CMU-MOSEI: sum-abs XGAF (0.6519 WF1) slightly exceeds early fusion (0.6485) and late fusion (0.5696). Mean-abs/median-abs produce nearly uniform weights, while sum-abs concentrates on the trimodal expert.

Conclusion: The main contribution is a transparent empirical analysis showing that SHAP reduction method and cross-modal expert design critically affect modular multimodal fusion, with sum-abs reduction being most effective. The primary performance gain stems from adding cross-modal experts (especially trimodal) rather than complex per-sample routing.

Abstract: Multimodal emotion and sentiment recognition is commonly addressed by early fusion, which concatenates modalities before classification, or late fusion, which combines independently trained unimodal predictors. Early fusion can be accurate but monolithic, while late fusion is modular but may lose cross-modal interactions. This paper revisits XAI-guided adaptive fusion (\xgaf), a tree-based mixture of unimodal and cross-modal experts whose sample-level weights are derived from TreeSHAP attribution magnitudes. We focus on the effect of SHAP attribution reduction when experts have unequal feature dimensionalities. In this setting, mean-abs and median-abs reductions can suppress high-dimensional cross-modal experts, whereas sum-abs reduction preserves total attribution mass. On MELD 7-class emotion recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} nearly matches early fusion across three face-sequence aggregators; the Transformer variant reaches 0.5983 \wf{}, compared with 0.6018 for early fusion and 0.4598 for probability-average late fusion. McNemar testing shows no significant difference between sum-abs \xgaf{} and early fusion on MELD ($p=1.000$), while \xgaf{} remains significantly better than late fusion ($p<0.0001$). On CMU-MOSEI 3-class sentiment recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} reaches 0.6519 \wf{}, slightly exceeding early fusion (0.6485) and late fusion (0.5696). Ablation studies show that the main gain comes from adding cross-modal experts, especially the trimodal expert, rather than from complex per-sample routing. Diagnostics further show that mean-abs and median-abs weights are nearly uniform, while sum-abs weights concentrate on the trimodal expert. Thus, the main contribution is a transparent empirical analysis of how SHAP reduction, expert dimensionality, and cross-modal expert design affect modular multimodal fusion.

</details>


### [140] [Towards Precision Therapy in Hepatocellular Carcinoma: A Clinical-Reasoning LLM for Risk Stratification and Treatment Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08602)
*Peng Cui,Jitao Wang,Siyan Xue,Yao Huang,Haoming Xia,Dong Li,Dengxiang Liu,Weilin Wang,Liping Liu,Leida Zhang,Yunfu Cui,Tao Peng,Daolin Ji,Haitao Zhao,Wei Zhang,Xiaojuan Wang,Weijie Ma,Zongren Ding,Jinlong Li,Yuan Ding,Jiajing Zhao,Zhiyu Chen,Chengkun Yang,Ziyue Huang,Jiaqi Liu,Fusheng Liu,Yang Zhou,Xiaojuan Wang,Zhongquan Sun,Shiyun Bao,Xiaojun Wang,Ming Yang,Guangxin Li,Bin Shu,Yong Liao,Hongxuan Li,Yao Tang,Shizhong Yang,Yongyi Zeng,Yufeng Yuan,Yinpeng Dong,Jihui Hao,Jun Zhu,Jiahong Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HCC-STAR is a clinically aligned large language model that reads EMR narratives to jointly perform risk score-based staging, guideline-consistent treatment recommendation with evidence-based rationales, and individualized survival estimation for hepatocellular carcinoma, achieving state-of-the-art performance across multi-center cohorts and outperforming physicians in treatment accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current HCC staging systems (BCLC, CNLC) provide coarse categories that miss within-stage heterogeneity and clinical context embedded in EMRs, limiting precision in risk stratification and treatment decision-making.

Method: Curated ~30,000 HCC cases from SEER, expanded into EMR-style narratives via clinician-validated prompt-based augmentation; developed a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework with step-verifiable composite reward optimization, moving beyond text-level guideline memorization.

Result: In a multi-center cohort of 6,668 patients from 12 Chinese hospitals, HCC-STAR achieved SOTA performance in treatment recommendation and risk stratification vs. guidelines and models including GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5 Pro. Adherence to HCC-STAR recommendations yielded 51-month median survival vs. 29 (BCLC) and 32 (CNLC) months. Blinded specialists rated justifications as trustworthy; the model outperformed residents and attending physicians in treatment accuracy and improved physician decision-making when used as an assistant.

Conclusion: HCC-STAR is a reliable, verifiable decision-support system for risk stratification and precision therapy in HCC, with demonstrated clinical utility and superior performance over existing guidelines and state-of-the-art models.

Abstract: Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) is a common malignancy and a leading cause of cancer-related mortality. Current guidelines and staging systems provide coarse categories, but often miss within-stage heterogeneity and the clinical context in electronic medical records (EMRs). We present HCC-STAR (Hepatocellular Carcinoma Staging, Treatment And pRognosis), a clinically aligned large language model that reads routine EMR narratives and jointly outputs risk score-based staging, ranked guideline-consistent treatments with evidence-based rationales, and individualized survival estimates. We curated about 30,000 HCC cases from SEER and expanded them into EMR-style narrative training data using a clinician-validated, prompt-based augmentation workflow. On this corpus, we developed a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework optimized with a step-verifiable composite reward, moving beyond text-level memorization of clinical guidelines. In a multi-center cohort of 6,668 patients from 12 hospitals in China, HCC-STAR achieved state-of-the-art performance in treatment recommendation and risk stratification compared with clinical guidelines and competitive models, including GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5 Pro. Hypothetical overall-survival analysis showed a median survival of 51 months under adherence to HCC-STAR recommendations, compared with 29 and 32 months under BCLC and CNLC. In clinician-centric evaluations, blinded hepatobiliary specialists rated HCC-STAR's reasoning and evidence-based justifications as trustworthy. The model surpassed resident and attending physicians in treatment accuracy and helped physicians make more accurate decisions faster when used as an assistant. These findings support HCC-STAR as a reliable and verifiable decision-support system for risk stratification and precision therapy in HCC.

</details>


### [141] [The complexities of patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08625)
*João Matos,Olivia Buege,Donny Cheung,Gary S. Collins,Paula Dhiman,Nan Li,Bingyu Mao,Benjamin W. Nelson,Michail Ouroutzoglou,Paul Varghese,Jonathan Amar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations reveals wide variation in communication patterns and emotional expression. A patient simulator modeling clinical content, emotion, strategy, and style was developed. In a Turing test, simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones (55% human accuracy). Testing four LLMs across five personae (1,164 clinician-graded cases) showed communication style significantly alters triage outcomes, warning that idealized systems risk real-world underperformance and health disparities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Consumer-facing health chatbots powered by LLMs are increasingly used for symptom assessment, yet their development and evaluation rely on cooperative, articulate, simulated patients, failing to account for real-world communication diversity.

Method: Analyzed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations; developed a patient simulator modeling clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style; conducted a Turing-inspired evaluation with 15 human graders; used five patient personae across 1,164 clinician-graded cases to evaluate four LLMs on urgency assessment.

Result: Simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones (human graders achieved 55% accuracy). Communication style was found to significantly alter triage outcomes when testing LLMs across different patient personae.

Conclusion: Patient-centred conversational AI must accommodate communication diversity; systems designed for idealized rather than realistic interactions risk underperformance and amplifying health disparities in real-world deployment.

Abstract: Consumer-facing health chatbots powered by large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for symptom assessment. However, chatbot development and evaluation often rely on cooperative, articulate, simulated patients. We analysed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations and found that communication patterns and expression of emotions vary widely across users. We developed a patient simulator that separately models clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style. In a Turing-inspired evaluation of realism with 15 human graders, simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones, with human graders achieving an accuracy of 55%. We used five distinct patient personae, across 1,164 clinician-graded cases, to evaluate the performance of four LLMs in urgency assessment. We found that communication style can significantly alter triage outcomes. Patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence must accommodate communication diversity: systems designed for idealised, rather than realistic, interactions risk underperforming and amplifying health disparities when deployed in the real world.

</details>


### [142] [Formal Mechanisms for Market Stability in Self-Interested Agent Societies: A Marketplace Simulation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08652)
*Eugene Ng Yi Sheng,Bingquan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how formal mechanisms layered on unrestricted communication can maintain market stability among self-interested LLM agents in repeated social dilemmas. Using a multi-agent marketplace simulation with 18 DeepSeek-V3 agents, Mediation is identified as the best mechanism, remaining robust against iteratively optimized adversarial troll attacks, reducing honest-agent utility by only 13.3% without collapsing the market.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate what formal mechanisms can sustain cooperation and market stability among self-interested agents in repeated social dilemmas, and how resilient these mechanisms are to adversarial attacks.

Method: Multi-agent marketplace simulation with 18 DeepSeek-V3 LLM agents having complementary production specialties trading in a constrained social network. Phase 1: mechanism comparison across 8 conditions under progressive troll injection over 200 rounds. Phase 2: adversarial red-teaming of Mediation using iteratively prompt-optimized LLM-driven trolls.

Result: Mediation is the top-performing mechanism. Best attack (v6) reduces honest-agent utility by 13.3% but cannot collapse the market. Mediation enables recovery even under sustained adversarial pressure.

Conclusion: Mediation is adversarially robust: it can sustain positive honest-agent utility under optimized attack—it can be bent but not broken.

Abstract: Self-interested agents, left unconstrained, tend toward defection in repeated social dilemmas, causing cooperative gains from trade to collapse. This paper investigates what formal mechanisms, layered on top of unrestricted communication, are sufficient for a society of such agents to maintain market stability, and how resilient those mechanisms are to adversarial attack. We instantiate the research question as a multi-agent marketplace simulation where 18 LLM agents (DeepSeek-V3) with complementary production specialties must trade within a constrained social network to obtain utility. We conduct two experimental phases: (1) a mechanism comparison across eight conditions under progressive troll injection over 200 rounds, identifying Mediation as the top-performing mechanism; and (2) adversarial red-teaming of Mediation using iteratively prompt-optimised LLM-driven trolls, finding that the best attack (v6) reduces honest-agent utility by 13.3% but cannot collapse the market. Mediation enables recovery even under sustained adversarial pressure. We define adversarial robustness as a mechanism's ability to sustain positive honest-agent utility under optimised attack, and find that Mediation is robust: it can be bent but not broken.

</details>


### [143] [SolarChain-Eval: A Physics-Constrained Benchmark for Trustworthy Economic Agents in Decentralized Energy Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08681)
*Shilin Ou,Yifan Xu,Luyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SolarChain-Eval is a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy economic agents in decentralized energy markets. It reveals a utility-safety trade-off: RL agents improve market utility but can produce unsafe behaviors, and while an LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability, it cannot fully compensate for misspecified reward functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agentic AI systems are deployed in cyber-physical environments like decentralized energy markets, there is a need to evaluate both task performance and trustworthiness. Autonomous agents can improve market utility but may also exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and produce unstable governance decisions.

Method: SolarChain-Eval formulates market governance as a Gymnasium-compatible Markov Decision Process for hourly decisions. It evaluates policies across six dimensions: market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability. It incorporates an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer where the Planner defines episode-level bounds and audit rules, and the Auditor reviews/revises high-risk actions with structured logs.

Result: Experiments with static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies show a clear utility-safety trade-off. RL agents improve market utility but can produce unsafe behavior. Removing the physics penalty leads reward-maximizing agents to exploit invalid generation and increase artificial liquidity. The LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks but cannot fully compensate for a misspecified reward function.

Conclusion: Trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces. The results indicate that neither purely reward-based optimization nor LLM-based auditing alone is sufficient; both physical constraints and auditability mechanisms are needed for safe deployment in cyber-physical market systems.

Abstract: As agentic AI systems are increasingly applied to cyber-physical environments, their evaluation requires assessment of both task performance and trustworthiness. In decentralized energy markets, autonomous agents may improve market utility, but may also exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and produce unstable governance decisions. Therefore, we propose SolarChain-Eval, a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy economic agents. It formulates market governance as a Gymnasium-compatible Markov Decision Process, where agents make hourly decisions. SolarChain-Eval evaluates each policy across multiple dimensions, including market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability. To support agentic evaluation, SolarChain-Eval incorporates an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer. The Planner defines episode-level action bounds and audit rules, while the Auditor reviews and revises high-risk actions. All interventions are recorded through structured logs, including trigger signals, proposed actions, revised actions, and audit rationales. Experiments with static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies reveal a clear utility-safety trade-off. RL agents improve market utility but can still produce unsafe behavior. When the physics penalty is removed, reward-maximizing agents exploit invalid generation and increase artificial liquidity. The LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks, but it cannot fully compensate for a misspecified reward function. These results indicate that trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces. We release data and code as open access on GitHub for replicability.

</details>


### [144] [Remember When It Matters: Proactive Memory Agent for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08716)
*Yifan Wu,Lizhu Zhang,Yuhang Zhou,Mingyi Wang,Bo Peng,Serena Li,Xiangjun Fan,Zhuokai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "behavioral state decay" — a failure mode where decision-relevant information gets buried in long trajectories. To combat this, a memory agent actively updates a structured memory bank and selectively injects reminders into an unmodified action agent. Results show +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench and +6.8 pp on τ²-Bench, with selective intervention outperforming passive or always-on approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address "behavioral state decay," where relevant state (task requirements, environment facts, prior attempts, diagnoses, open subgoals) becomes inaccessible or fails to influence decisions as trajectories grow long, because it is buried in or pushed beyond the context window.

Method: A separate memory agent runs alongside an unmodified action agent. It updates a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory and decides whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. The module is plug-and-play with frontier action agents. Memory policies are trained using SFT and GRPO on SETA (with Qwen3.5-27B).

Result: Pass@1 improved by +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench 2.0 and +6.8 pp on τ²-Bench for both weaker and stronger action agents. Selective intervention outperformed passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. GRPO training improved validation reward and showed partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

Conclusion: Active, selective memory intervention is an effective plug-and-play solution for long-horizon tasks, and open-weight memory policies trained via SFT+GRPO show promise for generalization.

Abstract: In long-horizon tasks, decision-relevant state is often scattered across an expanding trajectory, while the action agent must surface it and act. As trajectories grow, task requirements, environment facts, prior attempts, diagnoses, and open subgoals can be buried in the context window or pushed beyond it, failing to influence decisions when needed. We call this failure mode "behavioral state decay". We study memory as an active intervention mechanism rather than passive retrieval. A separate memory agent runs alongside an unmodified action agent, updating a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory and deciding whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. The module is plug-and-play with frontier action agents and existing agent harnesses. Across Terminal-Bench 2.0 and $τ^2$-Bench, it improves pass@1 for both weaker and stronger action agents, with gains of +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench and +6.8 pp on $τ^2$-Bench. Ablations show that selective intervention outperforms passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. As an early step toward open-weight memory policies, we train Qwen3.5-27B on SETA using SFT and GRPO, improving validation reward and achieving partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

</details>


### [145] [The Illusion of Equivalency: Statistical Characterization of Quantization Effects in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08734)
*Baha Rababah,Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora,Carson K. Leung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Standard accuracy/perplexity metrics fail to capture behavioral changes from quantization; the authors propose correctness agreement (decision-level overlap) and show behavioral divergence emerges even when task performance seems preserved, with query/key projections being most sensitive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation of post-training quantization relies almost exclusively on accuracy and perplexity, which fail to capture behavioral changes in large language models induced by quantization.

Method: Introduces correctness agreement (a decision-level metric measuring overlap in correct predictions between base and quantized models), and analyzes quantization as a structural operator on attention weights using statistical and distributional measures of layer-wise distortion.

Result: Behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved; non-linear breakpoints exist at low bit-widths; query and key projections are consistently more sensitive than value and output projections.

Conclusion: There is an illusion of equivalence between base and quantized models; behavioral evaluation beyond conventional metrics is necessary for proper quantization assessment.

Abstract: Post-training quantization is widely used to deploy large language models in resource-constrained settings, yet its evaluation relies almost exclusively on accuracy and perplexity. We show that these metrics fail to capture behavioral changes induced by quantization. We introduce correctness agreement, a decision-level metric that measures overlap in correct predictions between a base model and its quantized variants, independent of absolute accuracy. Across multiple models and quantization schemes from 8-bit to 2-bit, we find that behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved. To explain this effect, we analyze quantization as a structural operator on attention weights and quantify layer-wise distortions using statistical and distributional measures. Our results reveal non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths and show that query and key projections are consistently more sensitive than value and output projections. These findings expose an illusion of equivalence between base and quantized models and motivate behavioral evaluation beyond conventional performance metrics.

</details>


### [146] [AUTOPILOT VQA: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Incident-Centric Dashcam Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08745)
*Siddharth Damodharan,Radhika Gupta,Ali Alshami,Ryan Rabinowitz,Jugal Kalita*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUTOPILOT-VQA is an incident-centric visual question answering benchmark for dashcam video understanding, designed to evaluate Vision-Language Models, Large Language Models, and Multimodal Large Language Models on safety-critical reasoning in autonomous driving. It covers diverse safety-relevant categories and will be part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Vision-Language and Multimodal Large Language Models show promise in autonomous driving tasks (scene understanding, decision making, trajectory prediction), but there is no standardized benchmark to evaluate whether these models can reliably reason about safety-critical incidents and near-incidents in real-world driving scenarios.

Method: The authors construct AUTOPILOT-VQA, an incident-centric VQA benchmark using dashcam videos of real-world driving incidents and near-incidents. It includes structured questions covering weather/lighting conditions, traffic environment, road layout, road surface state, signage, involved entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability-related reasoning. The dataset is released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition.

Result: The benchmark enables standardized evaluation of different systems on both contextual scene properties and event-level incident details, moving beyond object recognition toward temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning.

Conclusion: AUTOPILOT-VQA provides a standardized benchmark for assessing the reliability, interpretability, robustness, and safety consciousness of autonomous driving vision-language systems across diverse scenarios, supporting progress toward safer real-world deployment.

Abstract: Recent advances in Vision-Language Models, Large Language Models, and Multimodal Large Language Models have improved autonomous driving tasks such as scene understanding, decision making, trajectory prediction, and visual question answering. However, evaluating whether these models can reliably reason about safety-critical incidents remains challenging. To address this gap, we present AUTOPILOT-VQA, an incident-centric visual question answering benchmark for dashcam video understanding. The dataset evaluates different systems through structured questions designed around real-world driving incidents and near-incidents. The benchmark covers diverse safety-relevant categories, including weather and lighting conditions, traffic environment, road layout, road surface state, signage, involved entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability-related reasoning. By requiring models to answer grounded questions about both contextual scene properties and event-level incident details, AUTOPILOT-VQA moves beyond object recognition toward temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning. The dataset is released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition and provides a standardized benchmark for assessing the reliability of autonomous driving systems in different scenarios. Our benchmark support developments for more interpretable, robust, and safety-conscious vision-language systems for real-world autonomous driving.

</details>


### [147] [Using AI-based Learning Assistants in Higher Education: A Large-Scale Descriptive Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08748)
*Kristina Schaaff,Quintus Stierstorfer,Valerie Heckel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large-scale analysis of 77,543 students using Syntea AI learning assistant in higher education, examining usage patterns across demographics using objective log data rather than self-reported surveys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on educational chatbots relies on small samples and self-reported data; large-scale evidence on actual usage behavior is limited.

Method: Descriptive analysis of objective log data from 77,543 distance education students, examining usage patterns by gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode.

Result: Syntea is embedded in study routines, but usage differs across demographic and structural contexts.

Conclusion: Provides empirical basis for further development of AI-based learning support and contributes large-scale analysis of educational chatbot usage in higher education.

Abstract: In this study, we present a large-scale descriptive analysis of the use of an AI-based learning assistant (Syntea) in higher education. Based on objective log data from 77,543 students enrolled in distance studies, we examine usage patterns across gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode. To date, existing research on educational chatbots has largely relied on comparatively small samples and self-reported survey data, while large-scale evidence on actual usage behavior remains limited. Our findings show that Syntea is already embedded in the study routines of many learners, but that usage differs across demographic and structural contexts. By identifying these patterns, our study provides an empirical basis for the further development of AI-based learning support and contributes a large-scale analysis of educational chatbot usage in higher education.

</details>


### [148] [Ideas Have Genomes: Benchmarking Scientific Lineage Reasoning and Lineage-Grounded Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08758)
*Yifan Zhou,Qihao Yang,Yan Li,Donggang Li,Xiru Hu,Hokin Deng,Ziyang Gong,Xuanyi Zhou,Huacan Wang,Xiangchao Yan,Wanghan Xu,Wenlong Zhang,Shaofeng Zhang,Yue Zhou,Yifan Yang,Zhihang Zhong,Xue Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IdeaGene-Bench (IG-Bench) is a benchmark for evaluating AI systems on scientific lineage reasoning and generation, containing 1,961 lineage traces, 1,085 Idea Genome objects, and 920 GenomeDiff records across 10 domains. It supports two evaluations: IG-Exam (closed-form reasoning) and IG-Arena (generation with PES scoring). Experiments on 14 LLM-based scientists reveal a compositional bottleneck, with the best system achieving only 27.3% exact accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks fail to assess whether AI systems can follow the inheritance structure of scientific ideas—how they inherit mechanisms, repair limitations, and recombine prior work—much like biological genomes.

Method: The benchmark is built on the IdeaGene framework: papers are represented as minimal, typed, evidence-grounded Idea Genome objects with GenomeDiff aligning them under six evolutionary dynamics. It includes IG-Exam (42 task types, 1,029 instances for closed-form reasoning) and IG-Arena (generation evaluation using Population-Evolution Score).

Result: The strongest LLM system achieved only 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning. Structured lineage context reshuffles system rankings rather than uniformly helping all participants, revealing a compositional bottleneck.

Conclusion: AI systems still struggle with scientific idea lineage reasoning and generation, exposing a fundamental compositional limitation that persists even with structured lineage context.

Abstract: Scientific ideas rarely start from a blank page. They inherit mechanisms, repair known limitations, and recombine pieces of earlier work, much like biological genomes. Current benchmarks still say little about whether AI systems can follow this inheritance structure. We present IdeaGene-Bench (IG-Bench), a benchmark for scientific lineage reasoning and lineage-grounded idea generation. IG-Bench is organized around the IdeaGene framework: each paper or proposal is represented as a set of minimal, typed, evidence-grounded Idea Genome objects, and a GenomeDiff aligns these objects to record inheritance, mutation, loss, external import, and novel insertion under six operational evolutionary dynamics. The benchmark contains 1,961 golden lineage traces, 1,085 curated Idea Genome objects, and 920 pairwise GenomeDiff records across 10 scientific domains. It supports two evaluations. IG-Exam (42 task types, 1,029 instances) tests closed-form lineage reasoning across Idea Genome abstraction, inheritance tracing, evolutionary reasoning, and lineage verification. IG-Arena evaluates generation with a lineage-conditioned Population-Evolution Score(PES), asking whether a proposal can be inserted as a coherent descendant of a given lineage population: it should inherit the right Idea Genome objects, vary meaningfully from nearby work, and offer selection value for future research. Experiments on 14 LLM-based scientists expose a compositional bottleneck. The strongest system reaches only 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning, and structured lineage context reshuffles system rankings rather than helping every participant uniformly.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [149] [Towards the Explainability of Temporal Graph Networks via Memory Backtracking and Topological Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07716)
*Yazheng Liu,Xi Zhang,Sihong Xie,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes MemExplainer, a method for explaining Temporal Graph Network predictions by tracing influence through topology attribution trees and memory backtracking trees, using LRP to ensure faithful contributions, and optimizing event selection to overcome unfaithfulness in top-k selection. Experiments on nine datasets show superior performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanation methods for Temporal Graph Networks overlook the memory module, which is the core component that records and updates node histories, leaving the influence of past events on predictions unexplored.

Method: The method uses: (1) a topology attribution tree to capture influence of neighbors and their memory vectors, (2) a memory backtracking tree to quantify how historical events shape node memory vectors, (3) LRP (Layer-wise Relevance Propagation) applied to TGNs to ensure total event contribution equals model logits, and (4) optimization objectives to identify important events, addressing unfaithfulness of top-k selection due to nonlinear logit-to-probability mapping.

Result: Experiments on nine temporal graph datasets covering node property prediction, link prediction, and graph classification tasks show the method provides faithful explanations and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: MemExplainer effectively traces historical event influence through the memory module of TGNs, producing faithful explanations that outperform existing methods across diverse temporal graph tasks.

Abstract: Temporal graphs are ubiquitous in real-world applications and Temporal Graph Networks (TGNs) have achieved superior predictive accuracy. Understanding which historical events drive model predictions can enhance trustworthiness of TGNs. Existing explanation methods overlook the memory module, the core component that records and updates node histories, leaving the influence of past events unexplored. To address this, we attribute TGNs predictions through the topology attribution tree and memory backtracking tree. The topology attribution tree captures the influence of neighbors and their memory vectors, then the memory backtracking tree quantifies how historical events shape node memory vectors. We apply the LRP in TGNs, ensuring that the total contribution of events equals the logits of model. Finally, top-k selection may be unfaithful due to the nonlinear mapping from logits to probabilities, we design optimization objectives to identify the important events. Experiments on nine temporal graph datasets, spanning node property prediction, link prediction tasks and graph classification tasks, show that our method provides faithful explanations and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/yazhengliu/MemExplainer

</details>


### [150] [Who Gets Missed in the Tail? Thresholded Subgroup Underdiagnosis in Long-Tailed Chest X-ray Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07717)
*Ha-Hieu Pham,Hai-Dang Nguyen,Dang P. M. Cao,Thanh-Huy Nguyen,Min Xu,Trung-Nghia Le,Ulas Bagci,Huy-Hieu Pham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper audits fairness in multi-label chest X-ray classification by examining which patients are missed after converting ranking scores into binary decisions, finding that rare-positive patients below threshold (especially in subgroups) depend jointly on finding, subgroup, and operating threshold, not merely on label frequency or ranking metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Even with acceptable ranking performance in CXR classification, rare-positive patients can still fall below decision thresholds, particularly within demographic subgroups. The authors aim to study this pre-deployment fairness problem as an audit question—identifying who is missed after a long-tailed multi-label CXR model converts scores into decisions.

Method: The authors use a diagnostic ladder to separate class-level long-tail losses, subgroup-aware weighting, group robustness (GroupDRO), and threshold selection. Experiments are conducted on VinDr-CXR and MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT datasets, with paired bootstrap contrasts and GroupDRO reference runs for validation.

Result: On VinDr-CXR, group-tail weighting plus tail-aware thresholding reduces tail FNR from 0.665 to 0.269, sex worst-group FNR from 0.705 to 0.157, and age worst-group FNR from 0.822 to 0.133, while macro-mAP increases from 0.611 to 0.635. On MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, tail FNR reduces from 0.866 to 0.741 with similar worst-group improvements across sex, age, race, and insurance, though residual missed-positive rates remain high.

Conclusion: Rare-label fairness in chest X-ray classification depends jointly on the finding, subgroup, and operating threshold, not on label frequency or ranking metrics alone. Aggregate group robustness (e.g., GroupDRO) alone does not eliminate rare subgroup misses in this setting.

Abstract: In chest X-ray (CXR) classification, acceptable ranking performance can still leave rare-positive patients below threshold, especially within subgroups. We study this pre-deployment fairness problem as an audit question: after a long-tailed multi-label CXR model is converted from scores into decisions, who is missed? Across VinDr-CXR and MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, we use a diagnostic ladder to separate class-level long-tail losses, subgroup-aware weighting, group robustness, and threshold selection. On VinDr-CXR, group-tail weighting followed by tail-aware thresholding reduces tail FNR from 0.665 to 0.269, sex worst-group FNR from 0.705 to 0.157, and age worst-group FNR from 0.822 to 0.133, while macro-mAP increases from 0.611 to 0.635. On MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, the same score-to-threshold comparison reduces tail FNR from 0.866 to 0.741 and lowers worst-group FNR across sex, age, race, and insurance; residual missed-positive rates nevertheless remain high. Paired bootstrap contrasts on VinDr support the thresholded FNR reductions, and GroupDRO reference runs indicate that aggregate group robustness alone does not remove rare subgroup misses in this setting. The study supports a narrow audit claim: rare-label fairness in CXR depends jointly on the finding, subgroup, and operating threshold, not on label frequency or ranking metrics alone.

</details>


### [151] [LLT: Local Linear Transformer for PDE Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07718)
*Oded Ovadia,Eli Turkel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Local Linear Transformer (LLT) proposes a neural operator for PDE learning that combines linear global attention with local spatial mixing, achieving competitive accuracy and 1.8-2.5x faster training than Transolver across diverse PDE problems (elasticity, plasticity, airfoil flow, pipe flow, Darcy flow, 3D car aerodynamics) on both structured and unstructured meshes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention in Transformer-based neural operators scales quadratically with the number of computational nodes and lacks an explicit bias toward local interactions, limiting efficiency and effectiveness for PDE operator learning.

Method: LLT combines linear global attention (to reduce quadratic complexity) with local spatial mixing (to encode local interaction bias), and incorporates coordinate and geometry information into the architecture.

Result: LLT achieves competitive or lower relative L2 error compared to neural-operator and transformer baselines across elasticity, plasticity, airfoil flow, pipe flow, and Darcy flow. On matched structured discretizations, wall-clock time per training iteration is reduced by factors of 1.8 to 2.5 relative to Transolver. The method also scales to a 3D car aerodynamics dataset with 32,186 unstructured mesh points per sample.

Conclusion: LLT provides an accurate and computationally efficient neural operator for PDE problems across different discretizations, mesh types, and problem settings.

Abstract: Neural operators have become a common approach for learning PDE solution maps and accelerating numerical simulations. Transformer-based neural operators are of particular interest, since attention can learn long-range dependencies in the computational domain. However, standard attention has two major limitations when applied to PDEs: it scales quadratically with the number of computational nodes, and it lacks an explicit bias toward local interactions. To address these issues, we introduce Local Linear Transformer (LLT) for PDE operator learning. The architecture combines linear global attention with local spatial mixing, and incorporates coordinate and geometry information. We evaluate LLT on several PDE problems, including elasticity, plasticity, airfoil flow, pipe flow, and Darcy flow. The reference data for these problems span finite-element, finite-volume, and finite-difference discretizations on structured and unstructured meshes. Compared with other neural-operator and transformer baselines from prior studies, LLT achieves competitive or lower relative $L_2$ error across these problems. On matched structured discretizations, wall-clock time per training iteration is reduced by factors of 1.8 to 2.5 relative to Transolver. We also scale the approach and apply it to a three-dimensional car aerodynamics dataset with 32,186 unstructured mesh points per sample. Together, these results indicate that LLT provides an accurate and computationally efficient operator for PDE problems across discretizations, mesh types, and problem settings.

</details>


### [152] [ReCoLoRA: Spectrum-Aware Recursive Consolidation for Continual LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07719)
*Wentao Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReCoLoRA is a continual fine-tuning framework that recursively consolidates low-rank adapters via spectrum-aware decomposition, preventing task overwriting in sequential LLM adaptation and achieving strong results on continual GLUE benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LoRA-style methods suffer from catastrophic forgetting in continual learning because each new task's low-rank update stacks on the same frozen weight and overwrites previous tasks; a mechanism is needed to preserve prior knowledge while adapting to new tasks.

Method: ReCoLoRA initializes adapters from a randomized SVD of the pretrained weight, selects per-layer effective ranks via an elbow criterion, adapts the principal subspace before opening residual capacity, and before each new task recursively re-decomposes the current effective weight (not the original) into a frozen residual, a slowly updated principal component, and a fresh adapter.

Result: On six-task continual GLUE over four 7-8B backbones, ReCoLoRA achieves best final average score on three of four backbones against rank-swept LoRA, PiSSA, AdaLoRA, and DoRA, while training fewer parameters.

Conclusion: ReCoLoRA effectively mitigates catastrophic forgetting in continual fine-tuning by recursively consolidating knowledge, and its oracle-routed task-bank variant provides an upper bound under full task isolation.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adapts a large language model to one task cheaply, but across a task sequence LoRA-style methods keep stacking low-rank updates on the same frozen weight, so each new task tends to overwrite the previous ones. We present ReCoLoRA (Recursive Consolidation of Low-Rank Adapters), a spectrum-aware framework for continual fine-tuning: adapters are initialized from a randomized SVD of the pretrained weight, per-layer effective ranks are selected by an elbow criterion, and the principal subspace is adapted before residual capacity is opened. Before each new task, ReCoLoRA re-decomposes the current effective weight, rather than the original one, into a frozen residual, a slowly updated principal component, and a fresh adapter (recursive consolidation), so every task starts from the model that has already absorbed its predecessors. On a six-task continual GLUE sequence over four 7-8B backbones, ReCoLoRA attains the best final average score on three of the four backbones against rank-swept LoRA, PiSSA, AdaLoRA, and DoRA baselines while training fewer parameters; an oracle-routed task-bank variant serves as an upper bound under full task isolation. Code: https://github.com/bhqy666/ReCoLoRA.

</details>


### [153] [Omni-Sleep: A Sleep Foundation Model via Hierarchical Contrastive Learning of CNS--ANS Dynamic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07720)
*Zhoujie Hou,Song Wang,Kexin Lou,Mo Wang,Chen Wei,Quanying Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Omni-Sleep is a sleep foundation model that uses the CNS/ANS physiological partition as a structural prior for topology-constrained representation learning from multimodal PSG signals. With three objectives (intra-system consistency, inter-system synchronization, masked temporal modeling) and pre-training on 100,000+ hours of data, it outperforms baselines on sleep staging and multi-disease classification with better label efficiency, generalization, and robustness to missing modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sleep foundation models fuse heterogeneous biosignals (EEG, EOG, EMG, ECG, respiration) in a topology-agnostic manner, ignoring the physiological organization of the CNS and ANS. The authors aim to leverage this hierarchy as a prior for better representation learning.

Method: Omni-Sleep uses a CNS/ANS partition as a physiological prior for topology-constrained learning. It employs three objectives: (1) intra-system consistency (capturing shared subsystem-level factors within neural and cardio-respiratory signals), (2) inter-system synchronization (aligning subsystem trajectories for brain-body dynamics), and (3) latent-space masked temporal modeling (capturing long-horizon sleep dynamics). Pre-trained on 100,000+ hours of multi-center multimodal PSG data.

Result: Outperforms strong foundation-model baselines on sleep staging and multi-disease classification across datasets and modality-ablation settings, with improved label efficiency, cross-dataset generalization, and robustness to missing modalities.

Conclusion: Physiological hierarchy (CNS/ANS partition) is valuable for generalizable sleep representation learning, demonstrating that topology-constrained fusion of multimodal biosignals yields better performance than topology-agnostic approaches.

Abstract: Sleep physiology arises from the coordinated dynamics of the central nervous system (CNS) and autonomic nervous system (ANS), as reflected by multimodal polysomnography signals including EEG, EOG, EMG, ECG, and respiration. However, existing sleep foundation models often fuse heterogeneous biosignals in a topology-agnostic manner, overlooking their physiological organization. We introduce Omni-Sleep, a sleep foundation model that uses the CNS/ANS partition as a physiological prior for topology-constrained representation learning. Omni-Sleep learns structured representations through three objectives: intra-system consistency, which captures shared subsystem-level factors within neural and cardio-respiratory signals; inter-system synchronization, which aligns subsystem trajectories to model brain--body dynamics; and latent-space masked temporal modeling, which captures long-horizon sleep dynamics. Pre-trained on over 100,000 hours of multi-center multimodal PSG data, Omni-Sleep is evaluated on sleep staging and multi-disease classification. Across datasets and modality-ablation settings, Omni-Sleep outperforms strong foundation-model baselines, showing improved label efficiency, cross-dataset generalization, and robustness to missing modalities. These results highlight the value of physiological hierarchy for generalizable sleep representation learning. Code is available at https://github.com/AutoBrain-sleep/OmniSleep.

</details>


### [154] [Uncertainty-gated selection for block-sparse attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07724)
*Thomas Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a value-of-information router for block-sparse attention that identifies queries with ambiguous top-k cuts and doubles their kept blocks, improving recall on LongBench-v2 from 0.47 to 0.75 and preserving 0.81-0.89 of dense accuracy at 128K context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Block-sparse attention's per-query top-k selection is myopic: when the k-th and (k+1)-th blocks have nearly equal scores, the selector commits without extra budget, and a dropped block carrying answer evidence becomes unrecoverable downstream.

Method: A router measures for each query the decisiveness of the top-k cut based on the score gap between the k-th and (k+1)-th blocks, then doubles the kept set for queries where that gap is smallest. The rule is backbone-agnostic and stacks with existing block-scoring methods like Quest.

Result: On LongBench-v2 medium at n=215, router-on-Quest achieves paired recall 0.75 vs. top-k 0.47 (+28 pp, McNemar p<0.01). Within 2 pp of dense on RULER NIAH multikey. At 128K, preserves 0.81 and 0.89 of dense accuracy on Qwen2.5-7B-1M and Qwen3.6 (vs. SSA-style top-k at 0.09). Fused pipeline runs at 0.62x-0.80x dense wall time.

Conclusion: The value-of-information router effectively mitigates the myopic cutoff problem in block-sparse attention with minimal overhead, achieving near-dense accuracy while maintaining computational efficiency across multiple architectures.

Abstract: Block-sparse attention scales long-context language models by replacing the O(N^2) softmax with a per-query top-k selection over key blocks. This cutoff is myopic: when the k-th and (k+1)-th blocks are nearly tied in score, the selector commits without spending extra budget, and a dropped block carrying answer evidence is unrecoverable downstream. We propose a value-of-information router that measures, for each query, how decisively the top-k cut was made, and doubles the kept set for the queries where that gap is smallest; the rule is backbone-agnostic and stacks with existing block-scoring methods such as Quest. On LongBench-v2 medium at n=215 (the entire dataset subset), router-on-Quest reaches paired recall 0.75 vs. top-k 0.47 -- +28 pp over the SSA-style baseline (McNemar p<0.01) -- and lands within 2 pp of dense on RULER NIAH multikey at the same context. The lift reproduces on four models from three architectures (Qwen2.5, Mistral-Nemo, Qwen3.6). At 128K, the router preserves 0.81 and 0.89 of dense accuracy on Qwen2.5-7B-1M and Qwen3.6 (vs. SSA-style top-k at 0.09 on the former) while the fused selection-plus-kernel pipeline runs at 0.62x and 0.80x dense wall time.

</details>


### [155] [SHIFT: Survival Prediction from Incomplete and Heterogeneous Genomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07725)
*Muhammet Sami Yavuz,Ayhan Can Erdur,Sabri Mustafa Kahya,Benedikt Wiestler,Jana Lipkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SHIFT is a missingness-aware survival model using masked self-attention to predict directly from incomplete genomic inputs without imputation, showing strong generalization across cohorts with panel mismatch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Genomic prediction models fail to transfer across institutions due to structural feature missingness from different sequencing panels, and existing approaches (restricting to shared genes, excluding incomplete patients, test-time imputation) reduce robustness and limit multi-center data utility.
