Method: SHIFT represents each genomic feature separately, uses masked self-attention with a feature-availability mask to predict from observed inputs only, and applies variable-rate feature masking during training for robustness to heterogeneous missingness patterns.

Result: Evaluated on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma with external validation across multiple cohorts, SHIFT outperformed standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches. Incorporating patients from incomplete cohorts during development improved external performance.

Conclusion: Missingness-aware modeling is a practical strategy for multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology, enabling use of partially observed cohorts without exclusion or imputation.

Abstract: Genomic prediction models often fail to transfer across institutions because sequencing panels differ across sites, creating structural feature missingness at deployment. Existing approaches to this challenge typically restrict analysis to genes shared across cohorts, exclude patients with incomplete profiles, or rely on test-time imputation, all of which can reduce robustness and limit the use of multi-center data. We propose Survival prediction Handling Incomplete Features using Transformer (SHIFT), a missingness-aware survival model that directly predicts from incomplete genomic inputs without test-time imputation. SHIFT represents each genomic feature separately and uses masked self-attention, along with a feature-availability mask, so that predictions are based only on observed inputs. Further, we introduce variable-rate feature masking during training to improve robustness to heterogeneous missingness patterns. We evaluate the approach on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma with external validation across multiple cohorts, including a challenging setting with severe cross-cohort panel mismatch. Across these settings, SHIFT shows strong generalization and compares favorably with standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches, while using a single model across differing feature sets. We also find that incorporating patients from incomplete cohorts during development can improve performance on external data, suggesting that partially observed cohorts need not be excluded from model building. These results support missingness-aware modeling as a practical strategy for multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology.

</details>


### [156] [Jet-Long: Efficient Long-Context Extension with Dynamic Bifocal RoPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07740)
*Haozhan Tang,Zerui Wang,Yuxian Gu,Song Han,Han Cai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Jet-Long is a tuning-free zero-shot method for extending LLM context windows beyond the pretraining length by using a bifocal RoPE strategy: a local window keeps fidelity to the original RoPE, while a long-range window adapts its rescaling factor dynamically to the current sequence length. This recovers base-model accuracy on short inputs and extrapolates cleanly on long ones. The method uses an inclusion-exclusion attention merge and on-the-fly RoPE correction to keep inference overhead minimal (≤4% for generation, up to 1.39× FA2 throughput for prefill). Experiments on Qwen3 models up to 128K context show consistent gains on RULER, HELMET-RAG, and PG-19, and it generalizes to hybrid architectures like Jet-Nemotron without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zero-shot context extension methods fix a single rescaling factor upfront, which either sacrifices short-context fidelity (aggressive factor) or fails at long contexts (conservative factor). The authors aim to design a method that is faithful at short lengths and extrapolates effectively at long lengths, without tuning or retraining.

Method: Jet-Long uses a bifocal RoPE design with two windows: (1) a local window that stays faithful to the original RoPE to preserve short-context behavior, and (2) a long-range window whose rescaling factor dynamically adapts to the current sequence length. An inclusion-exclusion attention merge combines both windows, and an on-the-fly RoPE correction rotation ensures compatibility. The entire mechanism is fused into a single CuTe kernel for efficiency.

Result: On Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B up to 128K context, Jet-Long outperforms the strongest baselines on RULER by +4.79/+2.18/+2.03 pp, achieves best accuracy on HELMET-RAG, and attains lowest PG-19 perplexity. Long-context prefill reaches up to 1.39× FA2 throughput on H100, and single-batch generation incurs ≤4% overhead at any length.

Conclusion: Jet-Long provides a practical, tuning-free zero-shot solution for long-context deployment of open-weight LLMs. It handles arbitrary lengths beyond the training window, preserves short-input performance, generalizes to hybrid attention architectures (e.g., Jet-Nemotron) without retraining, and is robust to hyperparameter choices, making it easy to deploy.

Abstract: Modern LLMs are increasingly deployed in long-context applications such as retrieval-augmented generation, repository-level coding, and agentic workflows whose accumulated reasoning and tool traces routinely push the input an order of magnitude past the pretraining window, making zero-shot context extension the dominant deployment path for open-weight checkpoints. Most existing zero-shot methods fix a single rescaling factor up front, so an aggressive factor sacrifices short-context fidelity while a conservative one breaks down at long contexts. We propose Jet-Long, a tuning-free zero-shot method that pairs a local RoPE-faithful window with a long-range window whose rescaling factor adapts dynamically to the current sequence length, recovering the base model exactly at short inputs while extrapolating cleanly at long ones. An inclusion-exclusion attention merge and an on-the-fly RoPE correction rotation make the bifocal construction essentially free at inference; fused into a single CuTe kernel, long-context prefill reaches up to $1.39\times$ FA2 throughput on H100 (approaching the Hopper-only FA4), and single-batch generation incurs $\le 4\%$ overhead at every length. On Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B up to 128K context, Jet-Long leads RULER by $+4.79$/$+2.18$/$+2.03$~pp over the strongest baseline at 1.7B/4B/8B, achieves the best overall accuracy on HELMET-RAG (a benchmark identified by HELMET as the most efficient predictor of downstream long-context performance) and attains the lowest PG-19 perplexity. Jet-Long also generalizes to hybrid attention architectures such as Jet-Nemotron for further long-context improvement without retraining, and remains hyperparameter-resilient for ease of deployment.

</details>


### [157] [Architecture Generalization with MetaNCA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07743)
*Meet Barot,Daniel Berenberg,Sina Khajehabdollahi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaNCA is a meta-learning framework where a learned rule network (using a Weight Transformer with linear attention) iteratively updates weights of a task network via local interactions on the computation graph, enabling self-organization of neural network weights without backpropagation. It scales to 2M parameters and generalizes to unseen architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological neurons learn efficiently through local synaptic interactions, inspiring the use of neural cellular automata (NCA) for morphogenesis. The goal is to achieve similar local, self-organizing weight adaptation in artificial neural networks without relying on global backpropagation.

Method: MetaNCA introduces a rule network (Weight Transformer) that uses linear attention to aggregate local signals from neighboring weights and hidden states on the computation graph, iteratively updating task network weights through local update rules. The framework is meta-trained to generate weights for diverse architectures.

Result: MetaNCA generates weights for MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to 2M parameters. It generalizes to architectures unseen during meta-training, and architectural diversity in training enhances this generalization.

Conclusion: Local self-organizing rules can effectively generate functional neural network weights without backpropagation, with strong generalization and scalability properties, offering a biologically-inspired alternative to global gradient-based learning.

Abstract: Self-organization is an emergent property of life, driven by the collective behavior of individual components acting on local information. Biological neurons, through local interactions transmitted through synapses, are able to learn efficiently and can adapt their connections over an organism's lifespan. Motivated by these desirable properties of adaptability and local interaction, neural cellular automata (NCA) models have been successful at learning morphogenesis solely through local update rules, demonstrating stability over many updates and robustness to perturbations. In this work, we introduce Meta Neural Cellular Automata (MetaNCA), a framework that learns local rules which self-organize the weights of artificial neural networks. A learned rule network iteratively updates the weights of a task network using only local interactions on the computation graph. We propose a novel Weight Transformer architecture for the local rule network, which uses linear attention to aggregate signals from neighboring weights and hidden states. Once trained, the rule network generates task networks of diverse architectures without backpropagation. We show that MetaNCA generates weights for feedforward MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to networks of 2 million parameters. We further show that MetaNCA generalizes to architectures not seen during meta-training, and that architectural diversity in the training phase strengthens this generalization.

</details>


### [158] [LiST: Lipschitz Scaling Training for Robust and Calibrated Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07745)
*Arthur Chiron,Franck Mamalet,Thomas Massena,Thomas Deltort,Mathieu Serrurier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LiST (Lipschitz Scaling Training) is a method that achieves accurate, robust, and calibrated neural networks by exploiting the link between Lipschitz constraints and Temperature Scaling, iteratively adjusting the Lipschitz constant to reach a principled operating point on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front while maintaining calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy, robustness, and calibration are essential but often studied separately; Lipschitz-constrained models guarantee robustness but lack principled selection of the constraint L, and their calibration properties are underexplored.

Method: LiST iteratively adjusts the global Lipschitz constant to reach an operating point where calibration is naturally achieved (L*), uses a margin parameter in the training loss to construct a fully calibrated Pareto front, and reintegrates calibration data into training at convergence.

Result: Validated on CIFAR-10/100 and Tiny-ImageNet, LiST achieves competitive accuracy and robustness against constrained and unconstrained baselines while remaining calibrated out of the box.

Conclusion: Calibration acts as a principled criterion for selecting the Lipschitz constraint, and LiST enables navigating the accuracy-robustness trade-off while staying calibrated throughout, improving sample efficiency.

Abstract: While accuracy, robustness, and calibration are all essential for reliable neural networks, they are often studied separately; developing models that satisfy all three simultaneously remains a central challenge. Lipschitz-constrained models guarantee robustness by design, yet the manual selection of the Lipschitz constraint L governs the resulting accuracy-robustness trade-off, and their calibration properties remain largely underexplored. In this work, we highlight a theoretical and empirical link between the enforced Lipschitz constraint and Temperature Scaling, a state-of-the-art calibration method. Specifically, we find that for a given training scheme, there exists a non-trivial value L* that yields an out-of-the-box calibrated network, and that calibration acts as a principled criterion to select a well-defined operating point on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front. Leveraging these insights, we introduce Lipschitz Scaling Training (LiST), a novel training paradigm that iteratively adjusts the global Lipschitz constant to reach this operating point. Through a margin parameter in the training loss, LiST further enables the construction of a fully calibrated Pareto front, allowing users to navigate the accuracy-robustness trade-off while remaining calibrated throughout. At convergence, LiST also enables the reintegration of calibration data into training, improving sample efficiency without sacrificing calibration. We validate LiST on CIFAR-10/100 and Tiny-ImageNet, demonstrating competitive accuracy and robustness against constrained and unconstrained baselines, while remaining calibrated out of the box. Code is available at GitHub.

</details>


### [159] [Selective Left-Shift: Turning Test-Time Compute and Difficulty-based Curation into Training Data for Low-Resource Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07748)
*Didula Samaraweera,Anjana Supun,Srinath Perera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A three-phase pipeline (offline data synthesis → SFT → RLVR) decouples syntax from algorithmic reasoning to overcome the trilemma of data scarcity, inference cost, and ineffective RL for SLMs on low-resource programming languages. Applied to Qwen3-8B, it achieves +7.6 pass@1 on MultiPL-E and +14.2 on Agnostics LiveCodeBench for Julia, with 1/3 data and 1/6 cost of prior SOTA, and generalizes to Ballerina (49.7% Pass@1).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform well on high-resource programming languages (Python, Java) but degrade sharply on low-resource programming languages (LRPLs) like Julia. Improving SLMs for LRPLs faces a trilemma: SFT suffers data scarcity, inference-time scaling is too expensive, and RL from scratch yields near-zero gains.

Method: A three-phase pipeline: (1) Left-shift inference-time compute to an offline data synthesis engine that uses iterative compiler and test feedback to generate verified training examples. (2) Fine-tune an SLM on this synthetic verified data to embed strong syntactic priors. (3) Apply RLVR grounded by language-agnostic I/O tests, where the SFT prior constrains exploration away from syntax errors.

Result: On Qwen3-8B, improves pass@1 by +7.6 points on MultiPL-E and +14.2 points on Agnostics LiveCodeBench for Julia vs SOTA. Uses only 1/3 data and 1/6 cost. Generalizes to Ballerina achieving 49.7% MultiPL-E Pass@1, a language with near-zero pretraining representation.

Conclusion: The pipeline resolves the trilemma for LRPLs by decoupling syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning, verified through ablations showing both SFT phase and execution-grounded rewards are necessary for stable training.

Abstract: Large Language Models achieve strong code generation for high resource languages like Python and Java but suffer sharp performance drops on Low-Resource Programming Languages~(LRPLs) such as Julia. Improving Small Language Models~(SLMs) for these languages faces a trilemma: Supervised Fine-Tuning~(SFT) is bottlenecked by data scarcity, inference-time scaling is too expensive for deployment, and Reinforcement Learning from scratch yields near zero advantages. We propose a three-phase pipeline that resolves this trilemma by decoupling syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning. First, we \emph{left-shift} inference-time compute to an offline data synthesis engine that uses iterative compiler and test feedback to generate verified training examples. Second, we fine-tune an SLM on this synthetic, verified data to embed strong syntactic priors. Third, we apply Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward~(RLVR) grounded by language-agnostic Input/Output tests, where the SFT prior constrains exploration away from syntax errors. Applied to Qwen3-8B, our pipeline improves pass@1 by up to +7.6 points on MultiPL-E and +14.2 points on the Agnostics LiveCodeBench for Julia compared to SOTA results. Furthermore, the pipeline only used $\frac{1}{3}$ data and $\frac{1}{6}$ cost over the previous state-of-the-art. We further demonstrate that the pipeline generalizes to Ballerina achieving 49.7\% MultiPL-E Pass@1, a language with near-zero pretraining representation. Ablations confirm that both the SFT phase and execution-grounded rewards are necessary for stable training.

</details>


### [160] [A Transdiagnostic Space of Disorder Like Phenotypes in Reinforcement Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07753)
*Hari Prasad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a dose-controllable framework for inducing seven psychological disorders (anxiety, mania, OCD, depression, impulsivity, addiction, PTSD) in an appraisal-guided PPO agent by manipulating cognitive appraisal signals. Across 1000+ runs, each disorder shows graded dose-response curves. Key emergent findings include: disorders self-organize into a 2D affective space; remission treatment differs between reward distortion vs. avoidance disorders; dual-knob interactions produce nonadditive comorbidity effects. Three disorder knobs transfer to a 3D pixel environment with a standard convolutional agent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a systematic, controllable framework for modelling multiple psychological disorders in artificial agents using computational psychiatry-grounded mechanisms, moving beyond ad hoc reward shaping and single-run demonstrations.

Method: Seven disorders are each modelled as a single knob manipulating cognitive appraisal signals in an appraisal-guided PPO agent. Each disorder's symptom is measured by a preregistered assay mapped to a recognized paradigm. Experiments span 1000+ runs (10 seeds, four controls, 95% CI). Transfer tested in MiniWorld (3D pixel environment) with a standard convolutional agent without appraisal critic.

Result: Every disorder shows graded, monotone dose-response not reproduced by controls. Disorders self-organize into a 2D affective space where mania mirrors anxiety. Removing a knob remits reward distortion disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which recover under graded exposure. Dual-knob interactions are nonadditive, yielding testable comorbidity predictions. Three knobs transfer to 3D pixel environment with cross-assay dissociation confirmed.

Conclusion: Appraisal weights parameterize a controllable space of affective phenotypes in artificial agents, where the same knobs that induce a disorder can model its treatment. The framework generalizes beyond grid worlds and PPO's appraisal critic to standard convolutional agents in pixel environments.

Abstract: Modelling psychological disorders in artificial agents offers both a testbed for computational psychiatry and a lens on the failure modes of affective control. Prior work induces one or two disorders in a reinforcement learning (RL) agent by hand-tuned reward shaping, labels the behaviour post hoc, and reports single runs. We recast disorder modelling as dose-controllable manipulation of cognitive appraisal signals in an appraisal-guided PPO agent, expressing seven disorders (anxiety, mania, obsessive-compulsive checking, depression, impulsivity, addiction, and post-traumatic stress) each as a single knob grounded in a computational psychiatry account, with each symptom measured by a preregistered assay mapped to a recognised paradigm. Across more than a thousand runs (10 seeds, four controls, 95% confidence intervals) every disorder shows a graded, monotone dose-response that no control reproduces. Beyond these induced effects, three findings emerge that were not written into the reward: the disorders self-organise into a two-dimensional affective space in which mania mirrors anxiety; removing a knob remits reward distortion disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which instead recover under a graded exposure curriculum; and two simultaneous knobs interact nonadditively, yielding testable comorbidity predictions. Appraisal weights thus parameterise a controllable space of affective phenotypes in which the same knobs that induce a disorder can model its treatment. We also show that three disorder knobs (depression, addiction, anxiety) transfer to a three-dimensional pixel environment (MiniWorld) with a standard convolutional agent and no appraisal critic, with cross-assay dissociation confirmed across both domains, indicating the framework is not specific to grid worlds or to PPO's appraisal critic.

</details>


### [161] [Image classification via a quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07754)
*Kumari Jyoti,Rohith Babu,Apoorva D. Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hybrid classical-quantum framework for image recognition that uses a mixture of experts with quantum-inspired processing (amplitude encoding, local unitary convolutions, stabiliser code feature extraction) and classical fully connected neural networks, achieving improved accuracy and reduced failure rates on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST benchmarks with moderate computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a more efficient alternative to classical convolutional neural networks for pattern recognition tasks by leveraging quantum-inspired techniques and a mixture-of-experts strategy.

Method: A hybrid classical-quantum framework where the quantum part performs amplitude encoding, local unitary convolution, multi-expert parallel processing with different parameters, and stabiliser code-based feature extraction; the classical part uses a fully connected neural network to jointly process features from all experts for classification.

Result: The joint expert analysis outperforms individual experts and reduces the image classification failure rate by approximately a factor of two on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST datasets, with only moderate overhead on GPU workstations.

Conclusion: The proposed quantum-inspired mixture-of-experts framework is a practical and efficient alternative to classical schemes, and the quantum components are also executable on actual quantum processors.

Abstract: Pattern recognition problems arise in a variety of physical image processing situations, and convolutional neural networks are a popular scheme for the required feature extraction and classification tasks. The classical networks use diffusion-based smearing and block-wise pooling to downsample the image data and capture important structural features. In this work, we propose and demonstrate a more efficient quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts. It is a hybrid classical-quantum framework. The quantum part consists of amplitude encoding of the images, convolution using local unitary operations, multiple experts processing the same image with different parameters, and feature extraction using quantum stabiliser codes. The classical part then jointly processes the features extracted by different experts using a standard fully connected neural network for image class prediction. Using MNIST and Fashion-MNIST datasets as benchmarks, we demonstrate that the joint expert analysis outperforms the individual expert one, as well as reduces the failure rate of image class prediction by around a factor of two. The overhead of our quantum-inspired strategy is only moderate on GPU workstations, which makes our proposal a practical alternative to existing classical schemes. We also point out how the quantum part of our framework can be executed on a quantum processor.

</details>


### [162] [The Importance of Encoder Choice:A Tabular-Image Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07756)
*Ilia Koloiarov,Diego Coello de Portugal Mecke,Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi,Tom Hanika,Lars Schmidt-Thieme*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models (specifically In-Context Learning models) as encoders for image-tabular multimodal learning, addressing the challenge that these models require labels for processing instances, and highlighting the importance of encoder choice in multimodal settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior multimodal learning research uses plain MLPs for tabular modality despite known weaknesses of deep learning on tabular data. This work aims to highlight the encoder factor's importance by testing stronger tabular encoders.

Method: The authors evaluate state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in an image-tabular multimodal setting, addressing the challenge that In-Context Learning models require labels for processing, making test/train embedding non-trivial.

Result: Not specified in abstract, but the study demonstrates successful adaptation of In-Context Learning models for multimodal encoders by solving the label-dependency issue.

Conclusion: The encoder choice matters significantly in multimodal learning; tabular-specific strong encoders (like ICL models) can replace plain MLPs, but require careful handling of label-dependent processing.

Abstract: Multimodal learning usually requires a dedicated encoder per modality. When a tabular modality is involved, prior work has been mostly using a \emph{plain MLP} as the encoder. Yet if it were a strong encoder, the tabular domain would not be ``the last unconquered castle for deep learning''. This study evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in the image-tabular setting for the first time. An obstacle stands out. In-Context Learning models, among the best performing methods in the tabular domain, require labels to process instances, making it non-trivial to embed training and test instances the same way. We addressed this problem across multiple models of this family. With this study, we would like to highlight the importance of encoder factor in the multimodal learning.

</details>


### [163] [Scalable and Trustworthy Earth Observation Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07758)
*Syed Usama Imtiaz,Mitra Nasr Azadani,Nasrin Alamdari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This chapter reviews domain-specific design principles for Remote Sensing Foundation Models (RSFMs), highlighting that EO data governed by measurement physics and operational constraints prevents direct transfer of general foundation models. It synthesizes current model landscapes, pretraining objectives, architectures, downstream adaptation, and trustworthiness, with benchmark evidence showing no single universal best model and inconsistent evaluation. Two case studies (physics-informed spectral masking for algal blooms, RL for adaptive station selection) illustrate domain-guided principles. The chapter argues next-generation RSFMs should be evaluated by modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations beyond benchmark accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models have transformed ML by enabling general-purpose pretraining and downstream adaptation, but EO data is governed by measurement physics and operational decision constraints, preventing reliable/optimal transfer of RSFMs without domain-specific adaptation. This motivates reviewing design principles arising from these domain-specific constraints.

Method: The chapter defines the FM paradigm in RS, synthesizes current model landscape, pretraining objectives, architecture designs, downstream adaptation and trustworthiness requirements, incorporates recent benchmark evidence, and presents two case studies: physics-informed spectral targeted masking for harmful algal bloom prediction, and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection.

Result: No single geospatial foundation model is universally best; inconsistent evaluation remains a major issue for fair comparison and reliable deployment. Domain-specific constraints (measurement physics, operational decisions) prevent direct transfer of general FMs.

Conclusion: Next-generation RSFMs should be evaluated not only by benchmark accuracy, but also by modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations for trustworthy EO decisions.

Abstract: Foundation models (FMs) have transformed machine learning from isolated task-specific model development toward general-purpose models pretrained on broad data and adapted to multiple downstream tasks. Earth observation (EO) is an important domain for this paradigm because satellite and airborne archives are large, high-revisit, and increasingly multimodal, while reliable field labels are often sparse. Remote sensing foundation models (RSFMs) cannot be transferred reliably/optimally without domain-specific adaptation. This is because EO data are governed by measurement physics and operational decision constraints. This chapter reviews the design principles arising from these domain-specific constraints. It first defines the FMs paradigm in remote sensing (RS), then synthesizes the current model landscape, pretraining objectives, architecture designs, downstream adaptation and trustworthiness requirements. The chapter also incorporates recent benchmark evidence showing that no single geospatial foundation model is universally best and that inconsistent evaluation remains a major issue to fair comparison and reliable deployment. In addition, two brief environmental monitoring case studies; physics-informed spectral targeted masking for harmful algal bloom prediction and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection to illustrate the FMs domain-guided principles in practice. This chapter posits that next-generation RSFMs should be evaluated not only by benchmark accuracy, but also by modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations for trustworthy EO decisions.

</details>


### [164] [Trustworthy Machine Learning through the Lens of Combinatorial Optimization: Survey and Research Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07762)
*Thibaut Vidal,Julien Ferry*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey examines how combinatorial optimization (CO) frameworks can enhance trustworthiness in machine learning—including interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and certifiability—by providing global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit trade-off handling beyond what heuristic (e.g., gradient-based) methods offer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex ML models with similar empirical performance can have vastly different properties regarding transparency, interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and certifiability. There is a need for frameworks that can systematically analyze and certify these differences.

Method: The paper surveys and synthesizes recent advances at the intersection of combinatorial optimization (CO) and trustworthy ML, covering both training and post-training tasks such as interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, and privacy attacks and protections.

Result: CO formulations provide global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit treatment of trade-offs that purely heuristic (e.g., gradient-based) approaches cannot offer. However, scalability remains a key challenge.

Conclusion: Continued progress in solvers and hybrid algorithms suggests a growing role for combinatorial optimization in the design and deployment of trustworthy ML systems.

Abstract: Modern machine learning (ML) increasingly relies on complex models whose behavior is difficult to characterize beyond empirical performance metrics. Across a wide range of tasks, including prediction, generation, and decision-making, models with similar empirical performance can exhibit markedly different properties in terms of their transparency, interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and certifiability. This survey highlights how optimization- and certification-oriented reasoning can provide a useful framework for reasoning about such differences, supporting tasks ranging from model training and selection to auditing and certification. We review and synthesize recent advances at the intersection of combinatorial optimization (CO) and trustworthy ML, covering both training and post-training tasks, including interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, and privacy attacks and protections. Across these domains, CO formulations offer additional capabilities over purely heuristic approaches, e.g., gradient-based ones, notably global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit treatment of trade-offs. While scalability remains an important challenge, continued progress in solvers and hybrid algorithms suggests a growing role for CO in the design and deployment of trustworthy ML systems.

</details>


### [165] [Unlocking Temporal Generalization in Hamiltonian Video Dynamics Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07763)
*Eli Laird,Corey Clark*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hamiltonian Generative Networks (HGN) fail to generalize to different temporal resolutions in non-conservative (dissipative, forced) environments due to latent magnitude growth and truncation error accumulation; targeted fixes enable stable prediction at unseen step sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models typically use fixed-step discrete-time predictions, limiting their use for hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and multi-timescale applications. HGNs offer continuous-time grounding via Hamiltonian mechanics but break down in non-conservative settings where energy is not conserved.

Method: Identify two failure modes of HGNs in dissipative/forced environments: (1) unconstrained action-force map causing latent magnitude growth, and (2) global truncation error from under-resolved numerical integration. Propose targeted fixes for each mechanism.

Result: Demonstrate stable dynamics prediction at temporal step sizes well outside the training distribution after applying the identified fixes.

Conclusion: Recommends strategies for enabling temporal generalization in continuous-time video generation, with practical fixes for non-conservative HGN rollouts.

Abstract: World models are typically trained to predict discrete-time physical dynamics with a fixed step size baked into the model weights, preventing prediction at variable temporal resolutions. This matters for hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and scientific or game-engine applications that must query the same dynamics at multiple timescales. Hamiltonian Generative Networks (HGN) offer a principled path forward, grounding predictions in a continuous-time energy function that is, in principle, independent of the observation frame rate. In practice, however, their temporal generalization breaks down in non-conservative settings. We show that in externally forced, dissipative environments, HGN rollouts at step sizes beyond the training regime fail due to distinct failure modes, including latent magnitude growth driven by an unconstrained action-force map, and global truncation error accumulation from an under-resolved integrator. We identify a targeted fix for each mechanism and demonstrate stable dynamics prediction at temporal resolutions well outside the training distribution. In a detailed analysis, we recommend several strategies for enabling temporal generalization in continuous-time video generation.

</details>


### [166] [Principled Analysis of Deep Reinforcement Learning Evaluation and Design Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07769)
*Ezgi Korkmaz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes canonical evaluation and design paradigms in reinforcement learning, introduces theoretical foundations of scaling laws, shows non-monotone relationships between performance rankings and data-regimes, and demonstrates through large-scale experiments that canonical paradigms have led to incorrect conclusions in RL research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and correct flawed conclusions in reinforcement learning research that stem from canonical evaluation and design paradigms, particularly regarding how performance rankings shift across different data-regimes.

Method: The authors (1) introduce theoretical foundations of scaling laws in RL, (2) analyze the asymptotic performance relationship with data-regimes, showing non-monotonicity, and (3) conduct large-scale experiments to validate their theoretical analysis against canonical RL paradigms.

Result: The asymptotic performance of RL algorithms does not have a monotone relationship between performance rankings and data-regimes; canonical design and evaluation paradigms have produced incorrect conclusions in RL research.

Conclusion: Scaling, capacity, and complexity analysis of deep RL reveals that conclusions drawn under canonical evaluation paradigms can be misleading, necessitating a re-evaluation of how RL algorithms are designed and benchmarked.

Abstract: Starting from the utilization of deep neural networks to approximate the state-action value function that led to winning one of the most challenging games, to algorithmic advancements that allowed solving problems without even explicitly stating the rules of the challenge at hand, reinforcement learning research has been the center of remarkable scientific progress for the past decade. In this paper, we focus on the key ingredients of this research progress and we analyze the canonical evaluation and design paradigms in reinforcement learning. We introduce the theoretical foundations of scaling laws in reinforcement learning and show that the asymptotic performance of reinforcement learning algorithms does not have a monotone relationship between performance rankings and data-regimes. We conduct large-scale experiments and our results demonstrate that a line of reinforcement learning research under the canonical design and evaluation paradigms resulted in incorrect conclusions. Our analysis and results provide a core analysis on scaling, capacity and complexity of deep reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [167] [Graph-Regularized Deep Learning for EEG-Based Emotion Recognition with Psychologically-Grounded Label Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07773)
*Dongyang Kuang,Zizheng Ma,Yushan Zhang,Xiaocong Zeng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a graph-regularized learning framework for EEG-based emotion recognition that models emotion interdependencies via three regularization strategies (Graph Label Smoothing, Commuting Distance, Sliced Wasserstein Distance), achieving up to +5.42% accuracy and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications across multiple backbones and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning approaches for EEG-based emotion recognition treat emotion classes as isolated labels, ignoring their psychological interdependencies based on dimensional emotion theories.

Method: Proposes a graph-regularized framework with emotions as graph nodes and edges encoding proximity from dimensional theories. Employs three regularization strategies (Graph Label Smoothing, Commuting Distance via Graph Laplacian, Sliced Wasserstein Distance) ordered by increasing complexity. Evaluated on AudioTransformer, Conformer, and DCGNN backbones.

Result: Consistent improvements on SEED-IV and SEED-V datasets: up to +5.42% accuracy and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications. Raises upper bound of standard approaches.

Conclusion: The graph-regularized framework effectively leverages emotion topology to improve both accuracy and psychological plausibility of predictions, with architecture-agnostic benefits.

Abstract: EEG-based emotion recognition is critical for mental health monitoring and affective brain-computer interfaces, yet existing deep learning approaches often treat emotion classes as isolated labels, ignoring their psychological interdependencies. We propose a graph-regularized learning framework that conceptualizes emotions as nodes in a graph where edges encode proximity based on dimensional emotion theories. We adapt three complementary regularization strategies--Graph Label Smoothing (intuitive soft labeling), Commuting distance on graph via Graph Laplacian (spectral graph theory), and Sliced Wasserstein Distance (optimal transport on graph)--ordered by increasing computational complexity. These strategies penalize model predictions that deviate from the established emotion topology. Our framework is evaluated across three representative backbone architectures: AudioTransformer (pure transformer), Conformer (CNN-transformer hybrid), and DCGNN (causal graph neural network), demonstrating architecture-agnostic benefits. Experiments on SEED-IV (4 classes) and SEED-V (5 classes) datasets show consistent improvements: best case up to +5.42% accuracy and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications. Ultimately, our framework help raise the upper bound of performance achievable with standard approaches. Code will be released.

</details>


### [168] [A law of robustness for two-layer neural networks with arbitrary weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07778)
*Yitzchak Shmalo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves the Bubeck-Li-Nagaraj conjecture that two-layer neural networks fitting noisy labels must have Lipschitz constant at least ~√(n/m), up to a log factor, for ReLU activations and generic high-dimensional data. The proof uses function-space covering (since weight-space covering fails for unbounded weights) and a rigidity lemma controlling kink coefficients via Lipschitz constant. The bound is tight: at d=2 or with width 2n, O(1) Lipschitz is achievable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To verify the conjectured law that fitting noisy labels with a two-layer neural network forces a lower bound on the Lipschitz constant, with no restriction on weight magnitudes, which was previously unresolved due to unbounded weights making parameter-space covering impossible.

Method: Function-space covering instead of parameter-space covering; a rigidity lemma showing that on B₂ or S^{d-1} (d≥3), the coefficient of each canonical kink is controlled by the Lipschitz constant because kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel at generic points. Analysis covers continuous piecewise-linear activations, data from uniform on S^{d-1} or Gaussian N(0,I_d/d), and networks with arbitrary weights, biases, and skip connections.

Result: For width-m two-layer networks fitting n noisy labels ε below the noise floor, Lip(f) ≥ c ε √(n/(\bar m log(C\bar m nd/ε))) with high probability, where \bar m=(K-1)m+1. A realized-kink-count version holds: any piecewise-linear function with k(f) distinct kink hyperplanes obeys the bound with \bar m replaced by k(f)+1.

Conclusion: The conjectured Lipschitz law holds up to one logarithmic factor for all continuous piecewise-linear activations. Rigidity fails at d=2, where an explicit two-layer ReLU interpolant with O(1) Lipschitz at width 2n matches the law at the overparameterized endpoint, showing the bound is sharp.

Abstract: Bubeck, Li and Nagaraj conjectured that, for generic data, any two-layer neural network with $m$ neurons that fits $n$ noisy labels must have Lipschitz constant at least of order $\sqrt{n/m}$, with no restriction on the size of the weights. Bubeck and Sellke proved a universal version of this law for Lipschitz-parameterized classes, but under a polynomial bound on the parameters; at depth three that boundedness hypothesis is genuinely necessary. The two-layer unbounded-weight case requires a different argument. We prove the conjectured law, up to one logarithmic factor, for every continuous piecewise-linear activation, in particular for ReLU networks. For data drawn uniformly from $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$, $d\ge3$, or from $N(0,I_d/d)$, labels in $[-1,1]$ with noise level $σ^2>0$, and any width-$m$ two-layer network with arbitrary real weights, biases and affine skip connection, fitting the data $\varepsilon$ below the noise floor forces $\mathrm{Lip}(f)\ge c\,\varepsilon\sqrt{n/(\bar m\log(C\bar m nd/\varepsilon))}$, $\bar m=(K-1)m+1$, with high probability. A realized-kink-count version holds on the same event: every realized two-layer piecewise-linear function with $k(f)\le n$ distinct kink hyperplanes obeys the bound with $\bar m$ replaced by $k(f)+1$, irrespective of how many redundant hidden units parameterize it. The proof replaces parameter-space covering, impossible for unbounded weights, by a function-space covering. The central deterministic ingredient is a rigidity lemma: on $B_2$, and on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ for $d\ge3$, the coefficient of each canonical kink is controlled by the Lipschitz constant of the realized function, because kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel at generic points. Rigidity genuinely fails at $d=2$, and an explicit two-layer ReLU interpolant with $O(1)$ Lipschitz constant at width $2n$ matches the law at the overparameterized endpoint.

</details>


### [169] [Predicting Pseudo-nitzschia harmful algal blooms along the Portuguese Coast using satellite-derived predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07834)
*Ayman Bnoussaad,El Khalil Cherif,Ligia Pinto,Ramiro Neves,Alexandra D. Silva,Alexandre Bernardino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine-learning framework using satellite-derived predictors (1,000+ features) predicts Pseudo-nitzschia HAB occurrence along the Portuguese Atlantic coast, achieving 0.74-0.77 AUC with ensemble tree methods under realistic spatio-temporal cross-validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pseudo-nitzschia diatoms pose recurrent risks to coastal ecosystems and shellfish harvesting along the Portuguese Atlantic coast, necessitating predictive early-warning systems.

Method: Spatio-temporal machine-learning framework using 1,440 observations (2013-2023), 1,000+ satellite predictors (SST, upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, plankton functional types), river-aware spatial clustering, and stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation withholding entire years and spatial clusters.

Result: Random Forest achieved 0.74±0.05 AUC with environmental predictors; Extra Trees reached 0.77±0.06 with biological variables added. Seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominate predictions.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems along eastern boundary upwelling coasts.

Abstract: Pseudo-nitzschia diatoms pose recurrent risks to coastal ecosystems and shellfish harvesting along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. Here we develop and evaluate a spatio-temporal machine-learning framework to predict harmful algal bloom (HAB) occurrence using exclusively satellite-derived predictors under realistic forecasting constraints. We characterised environmental and biological variability across shellfish production zones (L1-L9) using 5,882 observations, providing system-wide context. Predictive models were developed for zones L1-L2, a hotspot for Pseudo-nitzschia and domoic acid events, using a decade-long dataset (2013-2023; 1,440 observations; more than 1,000 satellite-based predictors including sea surface temperature, an upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, and plankton functional types). Sampling locations were partitioned into ecologically meaningful sub-regions using a river-aware spatial clustering scheme. A stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation strategy that simultaneously withholds entire years and spatial clusters prevents leakage and closely mimics real-world forecasting conditions. HAB occurrence proved moderately predictable across model classes and feature configurations. Ensemble tree-based methods achieved the strongest discrimination: Random Forest reached 0.74 +/- 0.05 with environmental predictors; Extra Trees reached 0.77 +/- 0.06 with biological variables added. Feature-importance analyses revealed that seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominate model decisions, while biological indicators refine bloom likelihood within physically favourable periods. The framework demonstrates operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems along eastern boundary upwelling coasts.

</details>


### [170] [Explaining Near-Zero Hessian Eigenvalues Through Approximate Symmetries in Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07845)
*Marcel Kühn,Bernd Rosenow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Hessian's small eigenvalues in neural networks originate from weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of continuous symmetries in the network parametrization. Deep linear networks have exact zero modes from these symmetries; ReLU nonlinearities weakly break these symmetries, creating the bulk of near-zero eigenvalues orthogonal to high-curvature directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the largest Hessian eigenvalues are understood, the origin of the vast number of vanishingly small eigenvalues remains unexplained. This paper aims to clarify the source of the Hessian bulk spectrum.

Method: The authors construct eigenvectors of exact zero modes in deep linear networks. They treat ReLU as a weak perturbation that explicitly but weakly breaks continuous symmetries. They analyze the Hessian eigenvector alignment in a two-layer ReLU student-teacher model, a CIFAR-10 trained network, and a convolutional example.

Result: High-curvature Hessian directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace, while the bulk of near-zero eigenvalues lies almost entirely within it. The mechanism is demonstrated across fully connected and convolutional networks.

Conclusion: The Hessian bulk is linked to weakly broken continuous symmetries, clarifying the origin of near-zero modes in neural network loss landscapes.

Abstract: The Hessian of the training loss governs the local geometry of the loss landscape, yet despite existing explanations for its largest eigenvalues, the origin of the vast multitude of vanishingly small eigenvalues remains elusive. We argue that the bulk consists of the weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of the continuous symmetries of the network parametrization. In deep linear networks these symmetries are exact: they generate flat directions and hence exact zero modes, whose eigenvectors we construct explicitly. Introducing a ReLU nonlinearity as a perturbation, we show that it breaks these symmetries weakly and explicitly. Resolving the spectrum at the level of eigenvectors, we find that the high-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace, while the bulk lies almost entirely within it. We demonstrate the mechanism in a two-layer ReLU student--teacher model and in a network trained on CIFAR-10. A convolutional example demonstrates that the same diagnostic extends beyond fully connected layers. Together, these results link the Hessian bulk to weakly broken symmetries and clarify the origin of near-zero modes.

</details>


### [171] [When Does Continual Learning Require Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07847)
*Anne Harrington,Nayan Saxena,Michael Murphy,Anastasia Borovykh,Zeyu Yun,Sridhar Kamath,Ara Eindra Kyi,Trevor Darrell,Jitendra Malik,Yutong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues continual learning for LLMs should be framed as increasing competence under changes in space (new domains) and time (data drift). It benchmarks prompt-based, supervised, RL, and compression methods across realistic sequential settings, finding no single method excels universally—each suits different types of environmental change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current continual learning research focuses narrowly on context management and mitigating forgetting, which is incomplete. The authors argue for a broader framing: enabling LLMs to increase competence as the world changes, encompassing new domains, knowledge drift, and accumulating agentic state.

Method: The paper disentangles continual learning along two axes: space (new domains) and time (data drift under fixed tasks). It recasts standard LLM benchmarks as sequential problems and introduces a protocol to compare seven methods: prompt-based (GEPA, ACE), supervised learning (SFT, SDFT), reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT).

Result: Prompt-based methods fit quickly but degrade on future tasks; distillation methods accumulate knowledge stably but struggle to update outdated facts; context compression improves efficiency without substantially boosting new task learning; online RL adapts best to knowledge updates but remains sensitive to noisy rewards.

Conclusion: Continual learning is not a single capability—different patterns of environmental change require different update behaviors. Some changes require adaptation inside model weights, while others can be handled via external scaffolding. Understanding these success/failure patterns can guide stronger continual learning system design.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly capable, the next question is how can we enable models to continually learn? Today, the field largely frames this as a problem of context management and mitigating forgetting. We argue this framing is incomplete: continual learning is fundamentally about increasing model competence as the world changes. We disentangle this change along two axes -- space, where the model encounters new domains, and time, where the underlying data drifts under a fixed task. This framing lets us study continual learning under realistic conditions: new domains arrive over time, facts drift past their training cutoff, and agentic interactions accumulate state across episodes. To evaluate methods under this setting, we recast widely used LLM benchmarks as sequential problems and introduce a single mechanism-agnostic protocol that compares prompt-based methods (GEPA, ACE), supervised learning (SFT, SDFT), reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT). Prompt-based methods fit each new stage quickly but degrade on future tasks. Distillation-based methods accumulate knowledge stably but struggle to update outdated facts. Context compression improves efficiency without substantially improving the ability to learn new tasks. Online reinforcement learning adapts most effectively to knowledge updates but remains sensitive to noisy reward signals. Overall, our results suggest that continual learning is not a single capability: different patterns of environmental change require fundamentally different update behaviors, determining when adaptation must be learned inside model weights and when it can be achieved through external scaffolding. We hope that understanding where each method succeeds and fails will guide the design of stronger continual learning systems.

</details>


### [172] [NFTR: From Provable Mode-Averaging to Geodesic Subgoal Selection in Offline Goal-Conditioned RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07855)
*Erdemt Bao,Xing Lei,Jun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NFTR (Normalizing Flows subgoal policies with Triangle-slack Reweighting) replaces Gaussian subgoal policies in Hierarchical Implicit Q-Learning with conditional Normalizing Flows, and uses a triangle slack score to correct reweighting, addressing optimistic bias and mode collapse in offline goal-conditioned RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hierarchical Implicit Q-Learning (HIQL) suffers from two coupled failure modes: optimistic bias (treating lucky stochastic outcomes as skillful) and mode collapse (reducing multi-modal subgoal distributions to a single Gaussian mean that falls into unreachable regions). These stem from using a Gaussian policy conditioned on value-function advantages alone.

Method: NFTR introduces two components: (1) a conditional Normalizing Flow replaces the Gaussian policy for subgoal selection, identified via a closed-form mode-averaging result as the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection; (2) a triangle slack score, built on the architectural triangle inequality without relying on distance accuracy, multiplicatively corrects the AWR weight to downweight subgoals with excessive detour cost. The RWDR objective preserves monotonic improvement and admits a three-term suboptimality decomposition.

Result: Triangle-slack vanishes on geodesics in deterministic MDPs and remains a conservative upper bound on composability violation under stochastic dynamics. The method provably avoids Gaussian mode collapse and remains stable under stochastic dynamics.

Conclusion: NFTR provides provable subgoal selection that avoids the two failure modes of HIQL by combining Normalizing Flows for expressive multi-modal subgoal distributions with a triangle slack reweighting mechanism that corrects for optimistic bias.

Abstract: Hierarchical Implicit Q-Learning (HIQL), an offline goal-conditioned RL method, selects subgoals by value-function advantages alone. This rule has two coupled failure modes. Optimistic bias treats lucky stochastic outcomes as skillful choices, and mode collapse reduces a multi-modal subgoal distribution to a single Gaussian mean that often falls in unreachable regions. We propose NFTR (Normalizing Flows subgoal policies with Triangle-slack Reweighting). A conditional Normalizing Flow replaces the Gaussian policy, and a closed-form mode-averaging result identifies NFs as the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection. A triangle slack score, built on the architectural triangle inequality without relying on distance accuracy, multiplicatively corrects the AWR weight to downweight subgoals whose detour cost exceeds average reachability. Triangle-slack vanishes on geodesics in deterministic MDPs and remains a conservative upper bound on composability violation under stochastic dynamics. The RWDR objective preserves AWR's population-level monotonic improvement and admits a three-term suboptimality decomposition. Together, these two ingredients yield subgoal selection that provably avoids the Gaussian collapse described above and remains stable under stochastic dynamics. GitHub page: https://github.com/erdemtbao/NFTR

</details>


### [173] [Physics-Informed Machine Learning Under Small-Data Constraints: Lessons from Abrasive Waterjet Milling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07863)
*Sarah Grewe,Jörg Frochte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies small, expensive machining datasets (abrasive waterjet milling, n=155) and finds that data curation strategies, evaluation design (e.g., hold-out instability), and forms of physics integration significantly impact model ranking and performance. Gaussian Processes with residual physics learning and Bayesian tuning perform best; tree-based models degrade with hybrid pipelines; GP uncertainty intervals are well-calibrated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In physically dominated machining processes, experimental data is small, costly, and material-specific, making data curation, evaluation design, and physics integration as critical as the learning algorithm itself.

Method: Three contributions: (1) Separating physics-based data cleaning from statistical curation as competing modeling hypotheses; (2) Comparing single hold-out vs. 10-fold cross-validation revealing ranking instability; (3) Evaluating a spectrum of physics integration levels (residual learning, multi-stage hybrid pipelines) with Bayesian hyperparameter tuning across GP, gradient boosting, SVR, and tree-based models.

Result: Single-split winner dropped from rank 1 to rank 7 under 10-fold CV; GP variants dominated top ranks. Residual learning on a compact physics baseline benefited GP (lower variance, interpretable decomposition) but degraded tree-based models. Bayesian tuning improved GB and SVR but harmed multi-stage hybrids at n=155. GP 90% nominal intervals achieved ~86% empirical coverage.

Conclusion: For small, expensive process datasets, reliable model comparison requires explicit curation hypotheses, robust evaluation methods (e.g., cross-validation over single splits), and careful physics integration choices.

Abstract: In physically dominated machining processes, experimental datasets are small, expensive, and material-specific; in this regime, data curation, evaluation design, and the form of physics integration can matter as much as the learning algorithm. Using an abrasive waterjet milling dataset ($n{=}155$, Inconel\,718), we make three methodological contributions. First, we separate physics-based data \emph{cleaning} from statistical \emph{curation} and treat the latter as competing modelling hypotheses rather than silent preprocessing. Second, we find that model rankings from a 15-point hold-out set can be unstable: the single-split winner drops from rank~1 to rank~7 under 10-fold cross-validation, while Gaussian Process (GP) variants occupy the top ranks. Third, we study a spectrum of physics integration levels and find that residual learning on a compact physics baseline is competitive for GP, yielding lower variance and an interpretable decomposition, but degrades tree-based models. Bayesian hyper parameter tuning improves parameter-sensitive baselines such as gradient boosting and SVR, yet harms multi-stage hybrid pipelines at this sample size. GP uncertainty intervals are approximately calibrated ($86\%$ empirical coverage at nominal $90\%$). The resulting picture is methodological: for small, expensive process datasets, our results suggest that, in this setting, reliable model comparison benefits from explicit curation hypotheses, robust evaluation, and careful choices about how physics enters the model.

</details>


### [174] [Optimal Learning Rate Scaling Depends on Data in Deep Scalar Linear Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07884)
*Yedi Zhang,Peter E. Latham,Leena Chennuru Vankadara,Andrew Saxe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies gradient descent dynamics in deep scalar linear networks, showing that optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling depends on data, and under data-dependent scaling, learning dynamics become independent of data and weakly dependent on depth, achieving constant linear convergence across all depths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how learning rate should scale with depth in deep linear networks and whether data-agnostic scaling rules transfer across depths.

Method: Theoretical analysis of gradient descent dynamics in deep scalar linear networks with exact time-course solutions, examining both standard and residual architectures.

Result: Optimal learning rate scaling is data-dependent; data-agnostic scaling fails across depths; data-dependent scaling yields data-independent, depth-insensitive dynamics with constant linear convergence rate.

Conclusion: Data-dependent optimal scaling enables consistent convergence across all depths including infinite depth; similar effects observed in residual networks.

Abstract: In this short note we consider the gradient descent dynamics of deep scalar linear networks, $f(x) = \prod_{l=1}^L w_l x$, which enjoy exact time-course solutions for any integer depth. We show that even in this minimal model, the optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling depends on data, whereas data-agnostic scaling rules fail to transfer across depths. Under the data-dependent optimal scaling, the learning dynamics is independent of data and weakly dependent on depth, resulting in a constant linear convergence rate across all depths including infinity. We further show similar data-dependent effects in deep scalar linear networks with residual connections.

</details>


### [175] [Distributed Sketching on Data Partitions for OLS Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07888)
*Luyuan Yang,Brayden Garner,Shayan Shafaei,Chao Lan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies distributed sketching for OLS regression using partitioned subsets to reduce computational cost, and characterizes the exact excess loss of the averaged estimator, showing it is comparable to whole-dataset sketching when subset covariances diverge minimally.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce computational cost of OLS regression on large datasets by distributing small sketches of partitioned subsets across multiple machines, rather than sketching the whole dataset as in prior work.

Method: Distributed sketching on partitioned subsets of a large dataset; fixed design setting; exact excess loss characterization of the averaged OLS estimator.

Result: The excess loss of the averaged OLS estimator from partitioned sketching is comparable to that of whole-dataset sketching when divergence among subset covariances is small.

Conclusion: Partitioned distributed sketching is a viable computationally cheaper alternative to whole-dataset sketching for OLS regression, with similar performance under low covariance divergence.

Abstract: This paper studies distributed sketching for ordinary least squares (OLS) regression, an approach that distributes small sketches of a large data set over multiple machines to separately construct OLS estimators and average them. Unlike prior studies that consider sketching on the whole data set, we consider sketching on partitioned subsets to further reduce computational cost. Under the fixed design setting, we characterize the exact excess loss of the averaged OLS estimator. Results show that this loss is comparable to the established loss for sketching on the whole data set when the divergence among subset covariances is small.

</details>


### [176] [Multimodal Unlearning Across Vision, Language, Video, and Audio: Survey of Methods, Datasets, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07907)
*Nobin Sarwar,Shubhashis Roy Dipta,Zheyuan Liu,Vaidehi Patil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey provides a unified, system-oriented perspective on multimodal unlearning—selectively removing specific knowledge (e.g., sensitive, copyrighted, biased content) across vision, language, audio, and video modalities from large-scale foundation models (VLMs, DMs, LLMs, AFMs)—without full retraining, organized via a taxonomy that compares deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal foundation models encode sensitive, copyrighted, biased, or unsafe cross-modal associations from training data. Retraining is impractical, and targeted forgetting is hard due to knowledge distributed across shared representations, motivating the need for selective multimodal unlearning.

Method: The paper offers a unified, system-oriented survey with a taxonomy enabling systematic comparison across model architectures and modalities, grounded in recent advances, emerging applications, and open problems.

Result: The taxonomy clarifies trade-offs among deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness across modalities. A curated repository is released.

Conclusion: The survey highlights open problems and practical considerations to guide future research and deployment of multimodal unlearning.

Abstract: With the growing adoption of VLMs, DMs, LLMs, and AFMs, these multimodal foundation models can inadvertently encode sensitive, copyrighted, biased, or unsafe cross-modal associations that originate from their training data. Retraining after deletion requests or policy updates is often impractical, and targeted forgetting remains difficult because knowledge is distributed across shared representations. Multimodal unlearning addresses this challenge by enabling selective removal across modalities while retaining overall utility. This survey offers a unified, system-oriented view of multimodal unlearning across vision, language, audio, and video, grounded in recent advances, emerging applications, and open problems. Our taxonomy enables systematic comparison across model architectures and modalities, clarifying trade-offs among deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness. This survey highlights open problems and practical considerations to support future research and deployment of multimodal unlearning. We release a curated repository: https://smsnobin77.github.io/Awesome-Multimodal-Unlearning/

</details>


### [177] [Efficient Safety Alignment of Language Models via Latent Personality Traits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07918)
*Mohamed Amine Merzouk,Nolan Smyth,Damiano Fornasiere,Linh Le,David Williams-King,Adam Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent Personality Alignment (LPA) defends LLMs against jailbreak attacks by adversarial training on just 66 harm-agnostic personality statements instead of harmful content, achieving near-zero attack success rates with minimal computational cost and no utility loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety methods like Latent Adversarial Training (LAT) are effective but degrade utility and require large datasets of harmful prompts for training. The authors aim to design a defense that avoids exposure to harmful content, preserves model utility, and is computationally efficient.

Method: LPA replaces explicit harm refusal with adversarial training on only 66 harm-agnostic statements from psychometric personality literature. It hypothesizes that personality-anchored representations share latent structure with harm avoidance, so stabilizing them via adversarial training implicitly constrains the subspace exploited by jailbreak attacks.

Result: Near-zero attack success rates on HarmBench across direct requests and five jailbreak methods, with no loss of performance on standard benchmarks. Training uses 75x fewer examples than standard LAT and completes in minutes on a single GPU.

Conclusion: LPA is a robust, efficient, and generalizable safety method for LLMs that avoids harmful training data and utility degradation, demonstrating the feasibility of personality-anchored adversarial training as a lightweight alternative to existing defenses.

Abstract: Current safety methods for large language models are known to be vulnerable to adversarial attacks, motivating research into robust alternatives. Latent Adversarial Training (LAT) is among the most effective defenses, but can degrade utility and requires training on large datasets of harmful prompts. We introduce Latent Personality Alignment (LPA), which replaces explicit harm refusal with adversarial training on just 66 harm-agnostic statements drawn from psychometric personality literature. We hypothesize that personality-anchored representations share latent structure with harm avoidance, so adversarially stabilizing them implicitly constrains the subspace exploited by jailbreak attacks. LPA achieves near-zero attack success rates on HarmBench across direct requests and five jailbreak methods, despite never seeing harmful content during training and no loss of performance on standard benchmarks. Moreover, the training process is lightweight; the entire procedure completes in minutes on a single GPU and uses 75x fewer examples than standard LAT. Extensive ablations demonstrate the robustness, efficiency, and generalization of our method.

</details>


### [178] [path_boost: A Python Package for Interpretable Graph-Level Prediction using Path-Based Gradient Boosting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07935)
*Claudio Meggio,Johan Pensar,Riccardo De Bin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Path_boost is a Python package for interpretable graph-based supervised learning that uses gradient boosting to discover predictive labeled paths in graphs, offering an interpretable alternative to graph neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph neural networks are difficult to interpret, motivating the need for interpretable supervised learning methods on graph-structured data that can reveal which substructures drive predictions.

Method: PathBoost uses a gradient boosting algorithm that iteratively selects and extends paths based on their predictive power, combining weak learners into a strong ensemble without exhaustive path enumeration. It supports custom base learners/selectors, automatic starting node selection, parallel training, and variable importance computation.

Result: Demonstrated on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds and benchmarked against graph neural networks and graph kernel methods across six molecular datasets.

Conclusion: Path_boost provides an interpretable, scikit-learn-compatible alternative for graph-based supervised learning, available as open-source on PyPI and GitHub.

Abstract: We present path_boost, a Python package for interpretable supervised learning on graph-structured input data. The package implements PathBoost, a gradient boosting algorithm that automatically discovers predictive labeled paths within graphs during the learning process. Unlike graph neural networks, which are generally difficult to interpret, PathBoost produces an additive prediction model over path-based features that explicitly reveals which substructures drive predictions. To avoid an exhaustive enumeration of all possible paths, the algorithm iteratively selects and extends paths during learning based on their predictive power, using boosting to combine weak learners into a strong ensemble. The package supports both regression and binary classification. Key features include compatibility with scikit-learn workflows, support for custom base learners and selectors, automatic starting node selection, parallel training across anchor nodes, and built-in variable importance computation. We demonstrate PathBoost on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds, where atoms serve as nodes and bonds as edges, and further benchmark PathBoost against an established graph neural network and a graph kernel method across six molecular datasets. The package is available on PyPI and GitHub under an open-source license.

</details>


### [179] [Evaluating the Generalizability of Foundation Models for Extreme Environmental Events: Case Study of California Wildfire PM2.5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07951)
*Yongcan Huang,Li Jiang,Ze Yu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) underperform trained recurrent baselines (especially BiLSTM) in forecasting wildfire PM2.5 spikes. Zero-shot TSFMs show instability and limited improvement over persistence; LoRA fine-tuning helps but cannot match BiLSTM across all metrics, including exceedance F1 for hazardous levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wildfire smoke produces extreme PM2.5 concentrations with severe public health risks, but forecasting these rare hazardous spikes is fundamentally challenging. TSFMs perform well on general benchmarks yet their behavior under extreme out-of-distribution conditions (wildfire events) is poorly understood, motivating a systematic benchmark.

Method: Six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, Time-MoE; plus LoRA fine-tuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) are compared against fully-trained LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer, and naive persistence on a 12-year hourly PM2.5 dataset of 1,375 wildfire incidents across 79 California sites. A leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) protocol evaluates generalization across 6/12/24-hour horizons using MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds.

Result: BiLSTM achieves the lowest MAE (5.16 μg/m³) and highest exceedance F1 at all thresholds, including Hazardous band (0.63 vs. ≤0.54 for TSFMs). Zero-shot TSFMs only modestly improve over persistence; zero-shot Chronos-2 shows severe RMSE tail instability (23.4 μg/m³, negative R²). LoRA fine-tuning improves both adapted families but no foundation model surpasses trained recurrent baselines on any metric.

Conclusion: The assumption that larger pretrained models universally dominate environmental forecasting is challenged. Trained recurrent models (especially BiLSTM) remain superior for extreme event prediction. The study provides actionable deployment guidance for wildfire air quality prediction.

Abstract: Wildfire smoke events produce extreme PM$_{2.5}$ concentrations that pose severe public health risks, yet forecasting rare, hazardous-level spikes remains a fundamental challenge. Time series foundation models (TSFMs), pretrained models offering zero-shot inference and efficient adaptation, perform strongly on general benchmarks, but their behavior under extreme out-of-distribution conditions is poorly understood. We present the first systematic benchmark comparing six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, and Time-MoE, plus LoRA fine-tuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) against fully-trained baselines (LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer) and naive persistence on a 12-year (2013--2025) hourly PM$_{2.5}$ dataset covering 1,375 wildfire incidents across 79 California monitoring sites. A leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) protocol evaluates generalization to unseen fires, using MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds across 6-, 12-, and 24-hour horizons. Results reveal a consistent hierarchy. The BiLSTM achieves the lowest MAE ($5.16\,μg/m^3$) and the highest exceedance F1 at every threshold, including the Hazardous band ($>225.5\,μg/m^3$), reaching 0.63 versus at most 0.54 for any foundation model. Zero-shot TSFMs improve on persistence only modestly, and zero-shot Chronos-2 exhibits severe RMSE tail instability ($23.4\,μg/m^3$, negative $R^2$) from sporadic large errors. LoRA fine-tuning substantially improves both adapted families and largely repairs this instability, yet no foundation model surpasses the trained recurrent baselines on any metric. These findings challenge the assumption that larger pretrained models universally dominate environmental forecasting and provide actionable deployment guidance for wildfire air quality prediction.

</details>


### [180] [Linear Attention Architectures: Mechanisms, Trade-offs, and Cross-Layer Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07953)
*Tommaso Cerruti,Tim Rieder,George Rowlands,Lingfeng Jin,Imanol Schlag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Comparative study of softmax attention vs. four recurrent linear-attention architectures (DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, Gated DeltaNet-2) at 350M-3B parameters, finding Kimi Delta Attention with Muon achieves lowest validation loss, pure Gated DeltaNet with AdamW has highest training throughput, and introducing Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) modestly improves DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratic cost of self-attention in sequence length limits training and inference for long-context tasks, motivating exploration of more efficient recurrent linear-attention alternatives.

Method: Expresses four recurrent linear-attention mechanisms in a common recurrent-memory notation; trains 350M-parameter models on 15B tokens with optimizer (Muon vs. AdamW) and LR comparisons, hybrid-vs-pure stacks; scales DeltaNet to 1.3B and 3B; introduces and evaluates Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) for DeltaNet-style memories.
