Result: Kimi Delta Attention with Muon reaches lowest final validation loss at 350M/15B; pure Gated DeltaNet with AdamW has highest normalized training throughput; hybrid stacks improve loss at a throughput cost; Muon consistently lowers validation loss vs. AdamW; CLVR yields modest improvement for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.

Conclusion: Recurrent linear-attention variants offer viable alternatives to softmax attention with different trade-offs; CLVR provides a lightweight improvement; choice of optimizer (Muon vs. AdamW) significantly impacts final loss; no single architecture dominates across all metrics.

Abstract: Self-attention lets each token retrieve information from the full context, but its quadratic cost in sequence length limits training and inference at long context. This paper presents a comparative study of softmax attention and four recent recurrent linear-attention architectures: DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, and Gated DeltaNet-2. We express these mechanisms in a common recurrent-memory notation, making explicit how they differ in expressivity, memory decay, erase and write control, training throughput, and implementation complexity. Our experiments center on 350M-parameter models trained for 15B tokens, and include optimizer and learning-rate comparisons, hybrid-versus-pure stack comparisons, sequence-length runtime measurements, larger DeltaNet runs at 1.3B and 3B parameters, and a small set of downstream evaluations. The reported speed results measure training throughput and iteration time; we do not provide an empirical inference-speed benchmark. Within the reported 350M-parameter, 15B-token sweep, Kimi Delta Attention with Muon reaches the lowest final validation loss, a pure Gated DeltaNet stack trained with AdamW has the highest normalized training throughput, hybrid stacks generally improve loss at a throughput cost, and Muon consistently lowers final validation loss relative to AdamW in the matched architecture settings we evaluate. We introduce and evaluate lightweight cross-layer routing mechanisms for DeltaNet-style memories. The most natural DeltaNet-inspired formulation, forwarding a lower layer's delta-rule write error into the next layer's value target, does not improve over matched baselines. Routing into the aligned hidden stream and forwarding the write value instead yields a modest improvement in the matched runs we report: Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) lowers final validation loss for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.

</details>


### [181] [KronQ: LLM Quantization via Kronecker-Factored Hessian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07964)
*Donghyun Lee,Yuhang Li,Ruokai Yin,Priyadarshini Panda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KronQ improves LLM post-training quantization by incorporating gradient covariance (via Kronecker-factored Hessian) for bidirectional incoherence processing and gradient-aware mixed-precision allocation, achieving 7.93 perplexity on 2-bit LLaMA-3-70B where GPTQ/GPTAQ fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing second-order PTQ methods (e.g., GPTQ) assume uniform output-channel importance in layer-wise reconstruction. KronQ challenges this by arguing that quantization loss depends jointly on activation and gradient covariances, which existing methods ignore.

Method: (1) Bidirectional incoherence processing: extends input-side random rotation to output dimension using gradient covariance derived from Kronecker-factored Hessian, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input/output dimensions. (2) New sensitivity metric for inter-layer mixed-precision allocation driven by gradient and activation Hessian traces.

Result: On 2-bit weight-only LLaMA-3-70B, GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge (>2000 perplexity), while KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity on WikiText-2.

Conclusion: Incorporating gradient covariance into PTQ via Kronecker-factored Hessian significantly improves extreme low-bit quantization performance, enabling functional 2-bit weight quantization where prior methods fail catastrophically.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is a widely adopted technique for compressing large language models (LLMs) without retraining. Existing second-order PTQ methods, including GPTQ, construct quantization objectives exclusively from input activation statistics, effectively assuming that all output channels contribute equally to the layer-wise reconstruction objective. We propose KronQ, a PTQ framework that challenges this assumption by introducing the gradient covariance into the quantization pipeline. Under the Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation, the quantization loss depends jointly on both the activation and gradient covariances, and KronQ exploits this at two complementary levels. (1) KronQ introduces bidirectional incoherence processing, extending the existing input-side random rotation to the output dimension using the gradient covariance, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input and output dimensions. (2) KronQ derives a new sensitivity metric for inter-layer mixed-precision allocation, driven by the gradient and activation Hessian traces. Notably, in the case of 2-bit weight-only quantization on LLaMA-3-70B, while GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or produce degenerate quantizations (>2000 perplexity on WikiText-2), KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity.

</details>


### [182] [Provably Optimal Learning Algorithms for Assistance Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08012)
*Nivasini Ananthakrishnan,Mark Bedaywi,Michael I. Jordan,Stuart Russell,Nika Haghtalab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games, achieving a (1-1/e)-approximate assistance regret of Õ(T^{3/4}) with polynomial runtime, and showing that better than (1-1/e) approximation is computationally intractable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between assistance games theory and practical human-AI interaction by providing the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games, where a human (informed agent) and an assistant (uninformed agent) repeatedly interact to optimize a common reward function.

Method: Introduces the notion of assistance regret (gap between cumulative utility and optimal joint policies in hindsight), proposes decentralized algorithms achieving Õ(T^{3/4}) assistance regret with (1-1/e) approximation, accommodates any no-regret algorithm for the assistant, and adapts to a pseudo-decentralized setting using a shared random string to achieve Õ(T^{1/2}) rate.

Result: Achieves (1-1/e)-approximate assistance regret of Õ(T^{3/4}) with polynomial runtime; proves computational intractability for approximation better than (1-1/e); achieves optimal Õ(T^{1/2}) rate in pseudo-decentralized setting.

Conclusion: Repeated assistance games are tractable with near-optimal regret bounds, but there is a fundamental computational barrier at (1-1/e) approximation; the pseudo-decentralized setting achieves the optimal rate up to log factors.

Abstract: This paper studies an online variant of the assistance games framework, where an informed agent and an uninformed agent repeatedly interact over $T$ timesteps to optimize a common reward function. While the informed agent (the human) observes a latent state of the world, the uninformed agent (the assistant) observes only the human's actions. We provide the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games. We introduce the notion of assistance regret: the gap between the cumulative utility of interactions and that of the optimal joint policies in hindsight, which map latent states to action pairs. We present decentralized algorithms for both the human and the assistant that achieve a $(1-1/e)$-approximate assistance regret rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{3/4})$, with runtime polynomial in the size of the action and state spaces. These algorithms are general; in particular, they accommodate any no-regret algorithm for the assistant. We prove that achieving a regret approximation factor better than $(1-1/e)$ is computationally intractable. Furthermore, we demonstrate how these generic no-regret algorithms can be tailored to a pseudo-decentralized setting -- using a shared random string -- to achieve a rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/2})$, optimal up to logarithmic factors.

</details>


### [183] [Collate: Collaborative Neural Network Learning for Latency-Critical Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08013)
*Shuo Huai,Di Liu,Hao Kong,Xiangzhong Luo,Weichen Liu,Ravi Subramaniam,Christian Makaya,Qian Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Collate is a federated learning framework that collaboratively learns heterogeneous models to meet latency constraints of multiple edge systems, using dynamic zeroizing-recovering for model architecture adjustment and proto-corrected federated aggregation, achieving ~2-3% accuracy improvements over state-of-the-art methods with negligible training overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FL optimizations focus on training efficiency but fail to speed up inference under latency constraints in heterogeneous real-time edge systems, leaving a gap in meeting diverse system-level latency requirements simultaneously.

Method: Collate employs (1) a dynamic zeroizing-recovering method to adjust local model architectures to meet per-system latency constraints, and (2) a proto-corrected federated aggregation scheme that aggregates heterogeneous local models in a single training process while respecting varying latency bounds.

Result: Under latency constraints, Collate achieves 1.96% average accuracy improvement for extended models and 3.09% for shrunk models compared to state-of-the-art methods, with almost no extra training overhead.

Conclusion: Collate demonstrates that heterogeneous models can be collaboratively learned in one FL training process to simultaneously satisfy multiple edge systems' latency constraints while maintaining high accuracy, effectively addressing the inference-time heterogeneity problem in real-time edge FL deployment.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) empowers multiple clients to collaboratively learn a model, enlarging the training data of each client for high accuracy while protecting data privacy. However, when deploying FL in real-time edge systems, the heterogeneity of devices among systems has a severe impact on the performance of the inferred model. Existing optimizations on FL focus on improving the training efficiency but fail to speed up inference, especially when there is a latency constraint. In this work, we propose Collate, a novel training framework that collaboratively learns heterogeneous models to meet the latency constraints of multiple edge systems simultaneously. We design a dynamic zeroizing-recovering method to adjust each local model architecture for high accuracy under its latency constraint. A proto-corrected federated aggregation scheme is also introduced to aggregate all heterogeneous local models, satisfying the latency constraint of different systems with only one training process and maintaining high accuracy. Extensive experiments indicate that, compared to state-of-the-art methods and under a latency constraint, our extended models can improve the accuracy by 1.96% on average, and our shrunk models can also obtain a 3.09% accuracy improvement on average, with almost no extra training overhead. The related codes and data will be available at https://github.com/ntuliuteam/Collate

</details>


### [184] [PGD-NO: A Neural Operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition for 3D Million-scale Physics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08025)
*Weiheng Zhong,Jing Bi,Victor Oancea,Hadi Meidani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PGD-NO is a neural operator that uses precomputed geometry decomposition to achieve linear memory scaling, enabling processing of meshes with over 10 million nodes while maintaining competitive accuracy and interpretability for industrial simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural PDE solvers suffer from high memory consumption and a single-node VRAM bottleneck, limiting the maximum mesh resolution they can process. This prevents their application to large-scale, high-fidelity industrial simulations.

Method: PGD-NO (Precomputed Geometry Decomposition Neural Operator) shifts geometric encoding overhead to a deterministic pre-computation phase. It uses an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm to extract geometry tokens, decoupling feature extraction from solution querying, enabling linear memory scalability.

Result: The architecture achieves linear memory scalability, allowing high-fidelity learning on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes (where existing architectures fail due to memory exhaustion). It demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy on diverse industrial benchmarks and provides intrinsic interpretability via attention mechanisms.

Conclusion: PGD-NO effectively overcomes traditional mesh-size constraints, offering a robust and efficient solution for large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

Abstract: While neural PDE solvers have demonstrated significant potential for accelerating engineering simulations, existing architectures remain constrained by high memory consumption and the single node bottleneck, where the maximum processable mesh resolution is strictly limited by the VRAM of a single compute unit. To address these challenges, we propose PGD-NO, a neural operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition, that relocates the computational overhead of geometric encoding to a deterministic pre-computation phase. By utilizing an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm to extract geometry tokens, our model decouples feature extraction from solution querying. This architecture enables linear memory scalability, allowing high fidelity learning on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes, a scale where existing architectures typically encounter memory exhaustion. PGD-NO demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy across diverse industrial benchmarks and provides intrinsic interpretability through attention mechanisms. By effectively overcoming traditional mesh-size constraints, PGD-NO offers a robust and efficient solution for the next generation of large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

</details>


### [185] [Rethinking Small VLM Quantization: From Component-Wise Analysis to Hardware-Aware Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08029)
*Hyeju Shin,Chorwon Kim,Ryangsoo Kim,Hark Yoo,Jaein Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates component-wise quantization (vision encoder, projector, LLM backbone) across six configurations on Jetson Orin NX/AGX, finding that quantization sensitivity depends on structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense), SigLIP encoders face deployment-specific latency issues, INT4 reduces VRAM but slows token generation due to dequantization overhead, composite quantization errors are largely additive except along modality-alignment paths, and intelligence-per-joule varies significantly across platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While sub-3B vision language models enable on-device multimodal AI, a detailed understanding of component-wise quantization effects is missing, creating a bottleneck for optimal deployment.

Method: A systematic evaluation framework empirically validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on Jetson Orin NX and AGX, with separated analysis of vision encoder, projector, and LLM backbone components.

Result: (1) Quantization sensitivity depends on structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense) not just scale; (2) SigLIP encoders show high INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere due to deployment-specific interactions; (3) INT4 LLM quantization reduces VRAM but increases token generation time via dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive except on modality-alignment paths; (5) Intelligence-per-joule varies across platforms due to memory bandwidth constraints.

Conclusion: Optimal quantization for on-device VLMs requires considering architecture-specific and deployment-specific interactions beyond model scale alone, with platform-aware component-wise strategies needed for efficient deployment.

Abstract: The emergence of vision language models with fewer than 3 billion parameters has accelerated the implementation of on-device multimodal intelligence. However, a detailed understanding of component-wise quantization remains a bottleneck for optimal deployment. This paper presents a systematic evaluation framework for empirically validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on the Jetson Orin NX and AGX. By separating the vision encoder, projector, and large language model backbone yields the following results: (1) Quantization sensitivity is governed by the structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense) rather than scale alone, with MoE backbones mitigating INT4 noise where dense backbones degrade; (2) SigLIP encoders incur disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere--a deployment-specific encoder-kernel-hardware interaction, not a SigLIP flaw; (3) Although INT4 quantization of LLMs greatly reduces VRAM consumption, it also causes slower token generation due to dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive, except along the modality-alignment path, which is architecture-dependent; (5) The intelligence-per-joule profile varies significantly across platforms owing to memory bandwidth constraints.

</details>


### [186] [What to Keep, What to Forget: A Rate--Distortion View of Memory Compaction in LLMs and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08032)
*Ashwin Gerard Colaco,Nada Lahjouji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper unifies four disparate research areas (KV cache management, prompt compression, state bounding, and agent memory consolidation) under a single rate-distortion framework for context compaction, presents a seven-axis taxonomy, identifies attention magnitude and recency as universal but flawed compaction signals, highlights the lack of multi-turn compaction benchmarks, and proposes design principles and open problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory in LLMs and LLM agents is growing rapidly in cost, yet four separate research communities independently develop compaction methods without cross-pollination. The paper seeks to unify these efforts under a single theoretical lens to enable knowledge transfer and identify shared failure modes.

Method: The authors formalize a rate-distortion compaction objective with a layer-agnostic lower bound, construct a seven-axis taxonomy classifying methods from KV cache eviction to agent memory consolidation, and perform cross-layer mechanism transfer analysis. They also conduct a small reference experiment and propose a benchmark design.

Result: Two universal patterns emerge: (1) all layers rely on attention magnitude or recency as the compaction signal, and (2) this signal universally fails by discarding query-needed information prematurely before the query is known. Additionally, no existing benchmark measures repeated compaction across turns or holds budget axes constant across all layers simultaneously.

Conclusion: The compaction lens enables cross-stack mechanism transfer (e.g., KV cache techniques applied to agent memory). The authors propose compaction-aware design principles, a benchmark for multi-turn multi-layer compaction, and map open problems for future work.

Abstract: Large language models, and the agents built on them, spend an ever-growing share of their compute and memory on remembering: caching attention keys and values, carrying long prompts, maintaining recurrent state, and storing what happened in previous turns and sessions. Because none of this memory is free, four largely separate research communities have each learned to compact it. They evict or quantize the KV cache, prune or distill prompts, bound architectural state, and consolidate agent memory. We argue that these are instances of one problem: a rate--distortion decision about what context-derived information to retain versus discard, at what fidelity, under a resource budget, so as to preserve downstream task utility. We make this lens precise with a single compaction objective and a layer-agnostic lower bound, use it to build a seven-axis taxonomy that classifies methods from across the stack uniformly, and use it to transfer mechanisms between layers that have never been connected, from serving-stack KV management to agent long-term memory. Two patterns hold across the survey. At every layer the signal that decides what to keep is attention magnitude or recency, and it fails in the same way everywhere, by discarding, before the query is known and with no way to undo it, information the query later needs. And while compression is measured carefully on single-turn long context, the repeated compaction that agents actually perform is almost never measured, and no benchmark holds one budget axis across all the layers at once. We turn both observations into a benchmark proposal, a small reference experiment, and a set of compaction-aware design principles, and we map the open problems.

</details>


### [187] [An exact information theory of generalization phase transitions in Bayesian diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08041)
*Henry Hunt,Mason Kamb,Surya Ganguli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Bayesian Information Restricted Diffusion (BIRD) models to analytically study how diffusion models avoid memorization and generalize in high-dimensional spaces. It identifies an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization, showing that generation proceeds near this boundary via progressive information restriction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how diffusion models learn complex high-dimensional distributions from finite training sets without memorizing them, i.e., how they circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

Method: Introduces BIRD models where each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data, using Bayesian inference to time-reverse diffusion. These generalize existing spatially local analytical diffusion models. Information-theoretic analysis derives a phase boundary based on mutual information between restricted noisy observations and training data relative to log number of training points.

Result: Spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models early in training across architectures (UNets, DiTs). An information-theoretic phase boundary exists: memorization occurs when mutual information exceeds log training set size, generalization otherwise. Experiments confirm the predicted transition location. Generation tracks near this boundary by increasingly restricting information over time.

Conclusion: Information restriction plays a fundamental role in enabling generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality, with diffusion models operating near the memorization-generalization phase boundary.

Abstract: How diffusion models circumvent the curse of dimensionality to learn complex distributions over high dimensional spaces from a finite training set, instead of memorizing it, remains a fundamental mystery. To address this, we introduce analytically tractable Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models, in which each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data. A BIRD model time-reverses diffusion by inferring which past training sample produced its current restricted observation using the Bayesian posterior. This model class generalizes existing analytical diffusion models that use spatially local information restriction. We show that spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models \textit{early in training}, across different architectures such as UNets and DiTs. Under minimal assumptions on the data distribution, we identify an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization in the joint space of amount of training data, time in the reverse generative process, and amount of information restriction: a BIRD model memorizes when the mutual information between its restricted noisy observations and the training data exceeds the log number of training points, and it generalizes otherwise. Experiments across a range of datasets confirm our theoretically predicted location for the transition. We find that generation proceeds near the edge of memorization: both spatially local BIRD models and early-training diffusion models track the memorization-generalization phase boundary by increasingly restricting information over time. Overall, our results reveal a fundamental role for information restriction in generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

</details>


### [188] [Who Analyses the Analyser? Self-Validating LLM Hazard Analysis with Constitutional Meta-STPA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08054)
*Samuel Tetteh,Udip Shrestha,Joshua R. Waite,Cody Fleming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a blind spot in LLM-assisted safety analysis tools: they analyze every system except themselves. The authors address this by applying STPA (Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis) to the analysis tool itself, creating "Constitutional Meta-STPA" - a tool that derives its own governance constitution through a closed-loop meta-analysis, yielding 21 Tool Principles and 8 Meta-Safety Principles. They demonstrate self-derivation capabilities using frontier LLM ensembles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-assisted safety analysis tools are themselves safety-relevant systems that can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and lack audit trails. The field has overlooked the question of who analyzes the analyser, creating a critical blind spot in safety assurance.

Method: The authors turn STPA on the analysis tool itself via a closed-loop "Constitutional Meta-STPA" framework. The tool runs a meta-STPA of the class of AI-assisted safety tools and derives its governance constitution from the loss→hazard→UCA→constraint chain. They formalize the measured object as a constitution-marginal coverage operator over a principle set P (|P|=29) with a soundness lemma that isolates coverage from model and scanner.

Result: (i) Self-derivation: a frontier ensemble (claude-opus-4.8+claude-sonnet-4) recovers 18/21 canonical and all 8/8 governance principles from the tool's own design. A weaker pair recovers 12/21 and 3/8, showing the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited. The same 8/8 principles re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool.

Conclusion: Constitutional Meta-STPA demonstrates that self-analysis of LLM-assisted safety tools is feasible, producing a published constitution of 21 Tool Principles and 8 Meta-Safety Principles, each bound to code enforcement points. The approach is model-limited rather than constitution-limited, suggesting that stronger models enable more complete self-governance derivation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted to draft the artifacts of safety analysis such as, losses, hazards, Unsafe Control Actions (UCAs), and safety constraints, inside rigorous processes such as Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA). Yet a blind spot runs through this fast-growing literature: every system gets analysed except the LLM-assisted tool doing the analysing, which is itself a safety-relevant system that can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and leave no audit trail from prompt to artifact. We take seriously the question the field has skipped -- {who analyses the analyser?} and answer it by turning STPA on the tool itself. We present \{Constitutional Meta-STPA}, an LLM-assisted STPA tool built around a closed loop: the tool runs a {meta-STPA} of the class of AI-assisted safety tools and {derives} rather than asserts, its governance constitution from the resulting loss$\to$hazard$\to$UCA$\to$constraint chain, yielding a published constitution of $21$ Tool Principles and $8$ Meta-Safety Principles, each bound to a code enforcement point. We formalise the measured object as a constitution-marginal coverage operator over a principle set $P$ ($|P|{=}29$) with a soundness lemma that isolates coverage from model and scanner, and report four findings. {(i)~Self-derivation:} a frontier ensemble ({claude-opus-4.8}${+}${claude-sonnet-4}) recovers $18/21$ canonical and all $8/8$ governance principles from the tool's own design, while a weaker pair recovers $12/21$ and $3/8$, so the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited, and the same $8/8$ re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool.

</details>


### [189] [Reinforcing the Generation Order of Multimodal Masked Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08056)
*Yidong Ouyang,Zhe Wang,Sourav Bhabesh,Dmitriy Bespalov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a learnable control module trained via GRPO to optimize generation order in Diffusion Language Models, improving text-to-image alignment by 4.08% on GenEval and multimodal understanding by 4.85% on VLMEvalKit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed adaptive generation ordering improves DLMs for mathematical reasoning and code synthesis, but model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding tasks.

Method: The authors introduce a learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to determine the generation order in Diffusion Language Models for text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding.

Result: The framework achieves 4.08% relative improvement on GenEval (text-to-image alignment) and 4.85% relative improvement on VLMEvalKit (multimodal understanding).

Conclusion: Learning the generation order control block substantially improves text-to-image alignment, fine-grained spatial relationship capture, and multimodal reasoning/comprehension in DLMs.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) have recently achieved substantial progress in natural language generation tasks. Recent research demonstrates that adaptive token generation ordering can significantly improve performance in mathematical reasoning and code synthesis applications. In this work, we investigate the optimization of generation order for both text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. We first establish that, unlike structured problems in language generation such as Sudoku puzzles, model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding. To address this challenge, we introduce a learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to determine the generation order. Our results demonstrate that learning this control block substantially improves both text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding in DLMs. In particular, it enhances the model's ability to capture fine-grained spatial relationships in generated images while also strengthening performance on multimodal reasoning and comprehension tasks. We evaluate our framework on GenEval, an object-focused benchmark for text-to-image alignment, where it achieves 4.08% relative improvements. In addition, experiments on VLMEvalKit confirm 4.85% relative improvements in multimodal understanding, highlighting the broad effectiveness of our approach.

</details>


### [190] [Towards Efficient Large Language Model Serving: A Survey on System-Aware KV Cache Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08057)
*Jiantong Jiang,Peiyu Yang,Rui Zhang,Feng Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey systematizes KV cache infrastructure for LLM serving from a system behavior perspective, categorizing existing work into temporal (execution/scheduling), spatial (placement/migration), and structural (representation/retention) dimensions, while analyzing cross-behavior co-design and future opportunities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly despite rapid LLM advancements; the KV cache is crucial for low-latency, high-throughput inference but lacks a unified system-level understanding of its design space.

Method: The authors conduct a survey organizing recent work into three dimensions: (1) temporal: execution and scheduling, (2) spatial: placement and migration, and (3) structural: representation and retention. They further analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links.

Result: A systematized taxonomy of KV cache infrastructure from a system behavior perspective, with identified cross-dimensional co-design opportunities and links between system behaviors and optimization objectives.

Conclusion: This work provides a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure, highlighting future research opportunities in cross-behavior co-design.

Abstract: Despite the rapid advancements of large language models (LLMs), LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly. The key-value (KV) cache, which stores KV tensors during autoregressive decoding, is crucial for enabling low-latency, high-throughput LLM inference serving. In this survey, we focus on system-aware KV infrastructure for serving LLMs (abbreviated as sKis). We revisit recent work from a system behavior perspective, organizing existing efforts into three dimensions: execution and scheduling (temporal), placement and migration (spatial), and representation and retention (structural). Furthermore, we analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links, highlighting future opportunities. Our work systematizes a rapidly evolving area, providing a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure.

</details>


### [191] [When Thinking Hurts: Epistemic Signals in the Reasoning Chains of Visual Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08059)
*Mayank Singal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First empirical characterization of answer entropy behavior in thinking-mode VLMs across three families, showing that thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy for uncertainty quantification, with structured abstention patterns and a practical abstention gate that raises accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLM uncertainty quantification focuses on answer token distribution, but thinking-mode VLMs generate explicit reasoning chains before answers, raising the question of whether chain signals provide better uncertainty estimates than answer entropy alone.

Method: Evaluated four thinking-mode VLMs (Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking, GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking, InternVL3-8B, Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Thinking) on POPE adversarial samples to characterize answer entropy patterns; additionally tested on VQAv2 (300 samples) and HallusionBench for harder reasoning tasks. Measured AUROC for answer entropy and thinking chain entropy, and documented abstention patterns.

Result: Three distinct answer entropy patterns: complete collapse (Qwen3-VL, AUROC=0.492), no collapse (GLM-4.1V, 0.716), selective thinking (InternVL3, 0.675/0.602). Thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy when chains exist (0.647, 0.759, 0.608 vs 0.492, 0.716, 0.602). On VQAv2, chain entropy (0.680) > answer entropy (0.595), largest gap for free-form answers (0.733 vs 0.467). Structured abstention affects 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries. Abstention gate raises accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage.

Conclusion: Thinking chain entropy is a more reliable uncertainty signal than answer entropy whenever thinking chains are generated, though benefits vary across model families. Structured abstention is a practical, cost-free mechanism for improving VLM reliability, with strong gains in accuracy at reduced coverage.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification for visual language models (VLMs) conventionally targets the answer token distribution. We provide the first three-family empirical characterisation of answer entropy behaviour in thinking-mode VLMs. Running four models on identical POPE adversarial samples, we find three qualitatively distinct patterns: Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking shows complete collapse (ans H AUROC = 0.492); GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking shows no collapse (0.716); and InternVL3-8B shows selective thinking (chains on only 50% of samples, ans H = 0.675 full / 0.602 thinking-only). Across all three thinking-mode models, thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy on the subset where chains are generated (0.647, 0.759, 0.608 vs. 0.492, 0.716, 0.602 respectively), suggesting chain signals are the more reliable predictor whenever chains are present. This holds strongly for Qwen and GLM, but with only marginal and statistically unreliable advantage for InternVL3 (n_FP = 17). A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot confirms chain entropy (0.680) outperforms answer entropy (0.595) on VQAv2 questions, with the gap largest for free-form answers (0.733 vs. 0.467). On harder reasoning tasks (HallusionBench) both Qwen models show moderate signal (approx. 0.64), consistent with incomplete pre-commitment on difficult questions. We additionally document structured abstention affecting 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries, and a practical abstention gate raising accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with no additional inference cost.

</details>


### [192] [Cross-Modal Generative Framework for Signal Translation from Fetal-Maternal Electrocardiograms to Fetal Doppler Waveforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08073)
*Tongli Su,Alireza Rafiei,Marly van Assen,Reza Sameni,Gari D. Clifford,Faezeh Marzbanrad,Nasim Katebi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a cross-modal generative framework using dilated convolutions, cross-modal attention, and self-attention to synthesize fetal Doppler ultrasound envelopes from dual-lead (fetal and maternal) ECG, achieving significant improvements in reconstruction quality and demonstrating how analysis of recoverable vs. unrecoverable Doppler components can quantify purely mechanical contributions to fetal hemodynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the complementary relationship between fECG (electrical activity) and Doppler ultrasound (mechanical hemodynamics) in fetal cardiovascular function, and to computationally model maternal-fetal cardiac coupling by determining which Doppler components can be recovered from electrical recordings and which are purely mechanical.

Method: A cross-modal generative framework combining dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention (to incorporate maternal ECG) and self-attention (for long-range temporal dependencies), trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies.

Result: Doppler envelope synthesis achieved PSD MSE of 49.9 +/- 15.8 dB^2 (51% lower than two-channel baseline) and heart-rate error of 4.71 +/- 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline). Cross-modal attention yielded a 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation.

Conclusion: The framework advances computational modeling of maternal-fetal cardiovascular dynamics by enabling Doppler envelope synthesis from dual-lead ECG, and analysis of recoverable vs. residual Doppler components allows quantification of purely mechanical contributions to Doppler waveforms, facilitating more comprehensive fetal assessment.

Abstract: Fetal electrocardiogram (fECG) and Doppler ultrasound provide complementary views of fetal cardiovascular function: fECG captures electrical activity while Doppler reflects mechanical hemodynamics shaped by factors such as placental resistance and vascular compliance. Understanding the recoverable and unrecoverable Doppler components through reconstruction from fECG offers insight into the relative contributions of electrical versus mechanical factors in fetal circulation, thereby informing clinical decisions. In addition, clinical evidence of maternal-fetal cardiac coupling suggests that maternal cardiovascular dynamics may also inform fetal hemodynamics. To computationally model these relationships, we propose a cross-modal generative framework combining dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention to selectively incorporate maternal ECG and self-attention to capture long-range temporal dependencies. Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies, our model synthesizes Doppler envelopes with power spectral density mean squared error (PSD MSE) of 49.9 +/- 15.8 dB^2 (51% lower than two-channel baseline) and heart-rate error of 4.71 +/- 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline; negligible relative to the 110-160 bpm physiological range). Cross-modal attention yields a 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation, quantifying the contribution of maternal-fetal coupling. Our proposed framework advances computational modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling the synthesis of Doppler envelopes from dual-lead ECG. By analysis of both recoverable and residual Doppler components, this approach enables quantification of the purely mechanical contributions to Doppler waveforms -- those not recoverable from electrical recordings -- ultimately facilitating a more comprehensive fetal assessment.

</details>


### [193] [Modular Pretraining Enables Access Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08077)
*Ethan Roland,Murat Cubuktepe,Erick Martinez,Stijn Servaes,Keenan Pepper,Mike Vaiana,Diogo Schwerz de Lucena,Judd Rosenblatt,Addie Foote,Cem Anil,Alex Cloud*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRAM is a pre-training method using gradient-routed auxiliary modules to selectively disable targeted AI capabilities at inference time, enabling cost-effective access control for dual-use AI without training separate models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma where the same capability can be used for beneficial or harmful purposes. Access control via separate models is prohibitively expensive, motivating a cheaper alternative for selective capability removal.

Method: Gradient-routed auxiliary modules (GRAM) are added during pre-training and selectively updated to induce specialization. Modules can be ablated at inference time to remove specific capabilities from the network, approximating a model trained on filtered (capability-excluded) data.

Result: GRAM disables targeted capabilities while preserving remaining performance, resists capability recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Chinchilla-optimal scaling from 50M to 5B parameters shows the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens with scale on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones, with GRAM closely tracking data filtering. Training cost is independent of the number of capability profiles, yielding 5x cost reduction over data filtering in a 5-profile setting.

Conclusion: GRAM provides an effective and scalable alternative to data filtering for access control in dual-use AI, achieving significant cost savings while maintaining comparable capability isolation and resistance to recovery.

Abstract: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma. An AI capability that helps one user cure a disease can help another synthesize one. This dilemma could be resolved with access control, limiting dual-use AI capabilities to trusted deployments with a legitimate need. A gold standard for access control would be to serve separate models with different capabilities to different users. However, training and deploying multiple models is prohibitively expensive. To address this challenge, we propose gradient-routed auxiliary modules (GRAM), a pre-training method that adds modules to a neural network and selectively updates them to induce specialization. Ablating a module at inference time removes its capability from the network, approximating a model trained on filtered data. We evaluate GRAM on synthetic stories and realistic dual-use data spanning virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, and specialized code. These experiments show that GRAM disables targeted capabilities while preserving the rest, and resists their recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Most importantly, a Chinchilla-optimal scaling analysis from 50M to 5B parameters shows that the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens with scale on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones, and that GRAM closely tracks data filtering. GRAM's training cost is independent of the number of supported capability profiles, yielding a 5x reduction over data filtering in our 5-profile setting.

</details>


### [194] [Deep Learning Method for Stationary Distribution of Reflected Brownian Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08091)
*Jim Dai,Zhanhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a deep learning approach to learn the Laplace transform of high-dimensional reflected Brownian motion (RBM) using the basic adjoint relationship (BAR), achieving near-perfect prediction of tail probabilities in high-dimensional settings beyond analytically tractable regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed-form solutions for the stationary distribution of reflected Brownian motion (RBM) are only known for a few special cases, and computing important performance metrics like tail probabilities is even more intractable, despite their practical relevance in high-dimensional stochastic systems.

Method: A deep learning framework that learns the Laplace transform of high-dimensional RBMs based on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR), incorporating careful design of the loss function, training data sampling procedure, and neural network architecture.

Result: Near-perfect prediction of tail probabilities in high-dimensional settings when evaluated on RBM instances with known ground-truth values.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates potential as a general tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes.

Abstract: The stationary distribution of reflected Brownian motion (RBM) plays an important role in the analysis of high-dimensional stochastic systems, yet closed-form solutions are known only for a few special cases. Computing important performance metrics, such as tail probabilities, is even more intractable, despite their practical relevance. In this paper, we develop a deep learning approach that accurately and efficiently learns the Laplace transform of high-dimensional RBMs based on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR). Our framework combines a careful design of the loss function, training data sampling procedure, and neural network architecture. We evaluate the proposed method on RBM instances with known ground-truth tail probabilities and demonstrate near-perfect prediction in high-dimensional settings, highlighting its potential as a general tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes. Our code can be found at https://github.com/zhangz73/NN4MGF.

</details>


### [195] [Stochastic Order Learning: An Approach to Rank Estimation Using Noisy Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08103)
*Chaewon Lee,Seon-Ho Lee,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SOL reformulates rank estimation under label noise as a stochastic ordering problem, capturing ordinal label uncertainty via an embedding space with discriminative and stochastic order losses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty rather than simple label corruption, making standard rank estimation unreliable under noise.

Method: Proposes Stochastic Order Learning (SOL) with two complementary objectives: a discriminative loss for instance-centroid interactions and a stochastic order loss enforcing probabilistic ordering relations between instances.

Result: SOL enables reliable rank estimation across diverse datasets under various types and levels of label noise.

Conclusion: Modeling multiple plausible ranks per instance via stochastic ordering improves robustness to ordinal label noise.

Abstract: Rank estimation under label noise poses a fundamental challenge, as ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty rather than simple label corruption. In this paper, we reformulate rank estimation with noisy ordinal labels as a stochastic ordering problem, in which each instance is inherently associated with multiple plausible ranks instead of a single deterministic label. Based on this view, we propose stochastic order learning (SOL), a learning framework that captures ordinal label uncertainty and learns an embedding space through two complementary objectives: a discriminative loss that structures instance--centroid interactions and a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering relations between instances. Extensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate that SOL enables reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/SOL.

</details>


### [196] [Vanilla SGD with Momentum Survives Heavy-Tailed Noise: Convergence Analysis without Gradient Clipping or Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08104)
*Ryusei Yamada,Naoki Sato,Hideaki Iiduka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes vanilla SGD (with and without momentum) under heavy-tailed noise without gradient clipping/normalization, proving convergence rates that are suboptimal compared to clipped/normalized variants, with theoretical results validated on synthetic functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how vanilla SGD—particularly with momentum—performs under heavy-tailed noise without any gradient control mechanisms, addressing a gap where existing work focuses on modified variants like clipping or normalization.

Method: Theoretical convergence analysis of vanilla SGD and SGD with momentum for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives under heavy-tailed noise, without employing gradient clipping or normalization.

Result: Convergence rates for vanilla SGD (with and without momentum) under heavy-tailed noise are inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized SGD variants.

Conclusion: Vanilla SGD methods have inherent limitations under heavy-tailed noise, and gradient control mechanisms (clipping/normalization) are necessary to achieve optimal convergence rates.

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is a cornerstone of modern optimization. While its performance under heavy-tailed noise is often addressed through specialized modifications such as gradient clipping or normalization, we investigate a more fundamental question: how does vanilla SGD, particularly with momentum, perform in the presence of heavy-tailed noise? In this paper, we refine existing convergence results for vanilla SGD and, more importantly, provide the first comprehensive convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives, without employing any gradient control mechanisms. Our results demonstrate that the obtained convergence rates are inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized variants of SGD, thereby revealing inherent limitations of vanilla methods under heavy-tailed noise. The theoretical findings are supported by experiments on synthetic functions.

</details>


### [197] [Contrastive Order Learning: A General Framework for Ordinal Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08109)
*Chaewon Lee,BeomJun Shim,Kwang Pyo Choi,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConOrd is a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that combines contrastive learning with order learning via a novel contrastive order loss using soft affinity/disparity weights based on rank differences, achieving SOTA on age estimation, image quality assessment, and video quality assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contrastive learning ignores inherent ordering among rank labels, while order learning relies on local margin-based comparisons that fail to capture global ordinal structure. ConOrd addresses both limitations.

Method: Introduces a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, enabling fine-grained modeling of ordinal relationships across all sample pairs within a batch.

Result: Consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance on facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment tasks.

Conclusion: ConOrd generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios and outperforms existing methods.

Abstract: We propose contrastive order learning (ConOrd), a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that integrates the strengths of contrastive learning and order learning. While contrastive learning effectively leverages all samples in a batch, it typically ignores the inherent ordering among rank labels. Conversely, order learning explicitly models label ordinality but often relies on local, margin-based comparisons, limiting its ability to capture global ordinal structure. ConOrd addresses these limitations by introducing a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, enabling fine-grained modeling of ordinal relationships across all sample pairs within a batch. Extensive experiments on a range of ordinal regression tasks, including facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment, demonstrate that ConOrd consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance and generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/ConOrd.

</details>


### [198] [Workload-Preserving Differentially Private Synthetic Data for Causal Inference via Maximum-Entropy Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08122)
*Amir Asiaee,Kaveh Aryan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes causal workloads—DP query sets designed around orthogonal moments for doubly robust causal estimators—to improve causal inference (ATE, ATT, subgroup analyses) from differentially private synthetic data. The authors introduce Causal-AIM (adaptive workload selection) and NA+MI (noise-aware multiple imputation) for valid confidence intervals. They find causal workloads excel at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty, while generic workloads often yield better point RMSE under looser privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generic DP synthetic data methods preserve broad distributional fidelity but fail to adequately preserve causal estimands like ATE, which depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments that generic marginals may not protect.

Method: The paper proposes causal workloads—DP query sets based on orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators. These workloads can be used directly by stable moment-map estimators or reconstructed via maximum-entropy calibration. The authors introduce Causal-AIM (adaptive workload selector) and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for confidence intervals. A theory decomposes ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms.

Result: Causal workloads are most useful under strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty (valid confidence intervals). Generic workloads often retain an advantage for point RMSE under looser privacy. The same DP synthetic table can support ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental tradeoff: distributional fidelity aids point accuracy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and properly propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic rows as real data.

Abstract: Workload-based differentially private (DP) synthetic data methods privately measure aggregate queries and post-process the noisy answers into synthetic records. Generic workloads can achieve strong distributional fidelity, but causal estimands such as the average treatment effect (ATE) depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments that generic marginals need not preserve. We propose causal workloads: DP query sets designed around the orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators. The released workload can be used directly by stable moment-map estimators or reconstructed by maximum-entropy calibration into reusable synthetic data; our theory decomposes ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms. We also introduce Causal-AIM, an adaptive workload selector, and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for confidence intervals from DP synthetic data. Because the workload is released once, the same DP synthetic table can support ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending. Empirically, causal workloads are most useful at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty, while generic workloads often retain an advantage for point RMSE as privacy relaxes. The broader lesson is a tradeoff: distributional fidelity can help point accuracy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic rows as real.

</details>


### [199] [DeepPySR -- A Symbolic Regression Framework with Dynamic Pruning, Pareto Selection, and Hierarchical Composition for Real-World Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08150)
*Fuling Chen,Kevin Vinsen,Phillip Melton,Rae-Chi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepPySR is a symbolic regression method with dynamic variable pruning, exponential Pareto selection, and hierarchical composition that outperforms PySR on biomedical and social science benchmarks while producing interpretable formulas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic regression offers interpretable glass-box models crucial for clinical medicine and social science, but faces challenges with high-dimensional inputs, principled Pareto-front selection, and data irregularities like multicollinearity and class imbalance.

Method: DeepPySR introduces (1) a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to remove irrelevant features during search, (2) an exponential Pareto selection criterion balancing accuracy and complexity, and (3) a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic composition.

Result: Outperforms PySR on body fat (R²: 0.794 vs. 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs. 0.787), student performance (R²: 0.964 vs. 0.948), and Raine BMI (R²: 0.525 vs. 0.370) across Feynman physics, biomedical, and social-science benchmarks.

Conclusion: DeepPySR produces interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors, demonstrating that SR can be effectively applied to high-dimensional, irregular real-world data while maintaining transparency.

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) discovers analytical equations from data, yielding glass-box models with directly interpretable formulas, unlike black-box methods that rely on unstable post-hoc tools such as SHAP or LIME. This transparency is crucial in clinical medicine and social science, but SR faces three challenges: high-dimensional inputs, principled selection of Pareto-front formulae, and data irregularities such as multicollinearity and class imbalance. We introduce DeepPySR, which addresses these issues with a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to remove irrelevant features during search, an exponential Pareto selection criterion that eliminates trade-offs between accuracy and complexity, and a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic composition. On four Feynman physics benchmarks and seven biomedical and social-science datasets, DeepPySR outperforms PySR and baselines on body fat (R$^2$: 0.794 vs.\ 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs.\ 0.787), student performance (R$^2$: 0.964 vs.\ 0.948), and Raine BMI (R$^2$: 0.525 vs.\ 0.370), producing interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors.

</details>


### [200] [Understanding Layer Patching in Model Size Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08170)
*Sara Kangaslahti,Jonathan Geuter,Nihal V. Nayak,Marco Fumero,Francesco Locatello,David Alvarez-Melis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies student-layer selection for zero-shot model size interpolation via layer patching, formulates it as a shortest-path problem, shows sequential patching strategies work well in practice, and introduces KLPatch—a greedy KL-divergence-based algorithm that approximates the optimal solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on boomerang distillation demonstrates iterative layer patching for model size interpolation, but lacks a systematic understanding of how student-layer selection affects interpolation behavior; this paper aims to fill that gap.

Method: The authors cast layer subset selection as an optimization problem and prove it reduces to a shortest-path problem in an acyclic graph. They empirically evaluate sequential patching strategies (first-to-last, last-to-first) and propose KLPatch, a greedy algorithm using KL divergence to select layers.

Result: Simple sequential patching strategies often achieve strong performance. KLPatch improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization problem. Patching effects vary substantially across model families.

Conclusion: The results provide a principled understanding of how layer patching affects zero-shot model size interpolation and offer practical guidelines for constructing near-optimal interpolated models without additional training.

Abstract: Zero-shot model size interpolation aims to create new models of intermediate target sizes by combining existing models without additional training. Recent work on boomerang distillation [Kangaslahti et al., 2026] shows that a student language model distilled from a larger teacher can be expanded by iteratively patching its layers, replacing student layers with contiguous blocks of teacher layers to obtain models whose size and performance interpolate between the student and the teacher. In this work, we provide the first systematic study of student-layer selection for model size interpolation. We cast finding the optimal layer subset for each model size as an optimization problem and prove it can be viewed as a shortest-path problem in a certain acyclic graph. In experiments, we show that patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior, with effects that vary substantially across model families. We find that simple sequential strategies--patching either from the first layer to the last or from the last to the first--often achieve surprisingly strong performance in practice. We further introduce KLPatch, a greedy patching algorithm based on KL divergence, which often improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization problem. Together, our results provide a principled understanding of how layer patching affects model size interpolation and offer practical guidance for constructing near-optimal interpolated models.

</details>


### [201] [Open-ended Multi-agent Autocurricula via Visual Inspection of Policies with Multi-modal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08193)
*Lorenzo Pantè,Andrea Fanti,Roberto Capobianco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VIP (Visual Inspection of Policies) uses a Video Language Model to directly analyze policy behavior videos for curriculum generation in RL, outperforming text-only and scalar-based methods in StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing curriculum learning methods in RL rely on scalar task scores or textual summaries to assess task difficulty, which may not capture rich behavioral information. The authors explore directly inspecting policy behavior via recorded episode videos as a more informative alternative.

Method: VIP leverages a lightweight Video Language Model (VideoLLaMa2-7B) to process recorded policy videos and provide curriculum recommendations, replacing scalar scores or text summaries with direct visual inspection of agent behavior.

Result: VIP generates more effective curricula than text-only ablation and scalar-score-based methods in the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC) environment.

Conclusion: Video-based policy inspection using VLMs is a promising approach for open-ended curriculum design in RL, particularly in multi-agent settings where visual information captures complex behavioral dynamics.

Abstract: Open-ended curricula in Reinforcement Learning (RL) aim to train generally-capable agents by identifying tasks that facilitate learning increasingly complex skills. A major challenge when designing such curricula is assessing task difficulty relative to the agent's current learning progress. While previous work has explored using scalar task scores or textual summaries of the agent's behavior, here we study a different approach: directly inspecting policy behavior via recorded episode videos. We introduce a simple yet effective instantiation of this approach which leverages a Video Language Model (VLM) to both process these videos and provide curriculum recommendations, which we call Visual Inspection of Policies (VIP). Since videos can naturally contain any number of controllable agents, we empirically study VIP on the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC). We show that even with a lightweight and openly accessible VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B), VIP can use policy videos to generate more effective curricula than both its text-only ablation and methods that rely on scalar task scores.

</details>


### [202] [PIT-SUN: A Deployable Empirical Marginal Transform Framework with Expectation-Consistent Recovery for Regression in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08202)
*Mingyu Zhao,Zhaohan Li,Zhenxiong Miao,Xu Zhang,Dewei Leng,Yanan Niu,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PIT-SUN is a framework that uses probability-integral transformation and unbiased recovery to estimate original-space conditional expectations for value-driven recommender systems, avoiding the expectation consistency loss caused by nonlinear marginal transforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MSE suffers from unstable gradients on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, and multimodal targets (mean collapse, tail shrinkage), while nonlinear target transformations lose expectation consistency under direct inversion. Existing conditionally linear recovery methods exist but leave open choices for sparse complex marginals.

Method: PIT-SUN uses an empirical marginal table to define a bounded normal-score coordinate, its inverse-quantile lookup, a variance-controlled recovery base, and drift monitoring, then applies multiplicative SUN recovery to estimate the original-space expectation instead of directly inverting transformed predictions.

Result: Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment show robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

Conclusion: PIT-SUN provides a deployable empirical marginal recovery framework that resolves the expectation consistency issue caused by nonlinear transforms, improving accuracy and calibration for value-driven recommender systems.

Abstract: Estimating original-space conditional expectations is central to value-driven recommender systems, including dwell time, GMV, and LTV forecasting. Standard MSE is expectation-consistent in principle, but its gradients become unstable on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, and multimodal targets, causing mean collapse and tail shrinkage. Target transformation alleviates this scale conflict, yet any useful nonlinear marginal transform loses expectation consistency under direct inversion. This is not an implementation oversight: a direct inverse-transform estimator is universally expectation-consistent only when the inverse transform is affine, which cannot simultaneously provide bounded tail compression. Existing conditionally linear recovery methods restore expectation consistency, but still leave open which coordinate, inverse lookup, recovery base, and deployment monitor should be selected for sparse complex marginals. We propose \textbf{P}robability-\textbf{I}ntegral-\textbf{TranS}formed \textbf{Un}biased recovery (\textbf{PIT-SUN}), a deployable empirical marginal recovery framework. PIT-SUN uses one empirical marginal table to define a bounded normal-score coordinate, its inverse-quantile lookup, a variance-controlled recovery base, and drift monitoring, then applies multiplicative SUN recovery to estimate the original-space expectation instead of directly inverting transformed predictions. Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment show robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

</details>


### [203] [RhyMix: A Lightweight Adaptive Multi-Rhythm Network for Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08234)
*Sumit Satishrao Shevtekar,Chandresh Kumar Maurya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RhyMix is a lightweight hybrid neural architecture for time series forecasting that uses parallel dual-path modeling (Cyclic Path with seasonal inductive bias + Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention Path) and adaptive gating mechanisms to capture multiple temporal patterns simultaneously. It achieves state-of-the-art on 10/12 benchmarks with only ~40K parameters and <5ms inference latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world time series exhibit complex dynamics with multiple simultaneous temporal patterns (short-term fluctuations, seasonal cycles, long-term trends, abrupt changes). Existing forecasting architectures rely on single-path temporal modeling that cannot capture all these patterns effectively: transformers smooth local variations, convolutions have limited receptive fields, and linear models cannot capture nonlinear dynamics.

Method: RhyMix introduces a parallel dual-path architecture: (1) a Cyclic Path with learnable cyclic embeddings for seasonal inductive bias, and (2) a lightweight Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention (MSTCN-CA) Path using multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions. Adaptive gating operates at multiple levels: a path gate dynamically combines four forecasting heads (Direct, Trend-Seasonal Decomposition, Local Convolution, Periodic Fusion) per sample/channel, and a hybrid gate balances the two paths based on input characteristics. The model maintains linear complexity in sequence length, channels, and prediction horizon.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on 10 out of 12 real-world long-term forecasting datasets. Model is lightweight (~40K parameters) with linear complexity and low-latency inference (<5ms), making it suitable for resource-constrained edge devices and real-time deployment.

Conclusion: RhyMix effectively addresses the limitations of single-path temporal models by using parallel dual-path encoding with adaptive gating, enabling comprehensive capture of diverse temporal patterns while remaining computationally efficient enough for edge deployment.

Abstract: Real-world time series exhibit complex dynamics characterized by multiple simultaneous temporal patterns: short-term fluctuations, periodic seasonal cycles, long-term trends, and irregular abrupt changes. However, many existing forecasting architectures rely on single-path temporal modeling--transformers capture long-range dependencies but smooth local variations, convolutions capture local patterns but have limited receptive fields, and linear models are efficient but cannot capture nonlinear dynamics. To address this, we introduce RhyMix (RHYthm MIXture), a hybrid neural architecture designed around a parallel dual-path modeling paradigm with adaptive gating mechanisms. RhyMix integrates two complementary encoding branches: (i) a Cyclic Path that incorporates explicit seasonal inductive bias through learnable cyclic embeddings, capturing predictable rhythmic patterns; and (ii) a lightweight Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention Path that employs multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions to capture temporal dependencies across different receptive fields. A key innovation is the use of adaptive gating at multiple levels: a path gate dynamically combines four specialized forecasting heads (Direct, Trend-Seasonal Decomposition, Local Convolution, and Periodic Fusion) per sample and channel, while a hybrid gate adaptively balances the Cyclic and MSTCN-CA Paths based on input characteristics. This design ensures the model adapts to specific temporal patterns while maintaining linear complexity in sequence length, channels, and prediction horizon. Across extensive benchmarks on 12 real-world datasets for long-term forecasting, RhyMix achieves state-of-the-art performance on 10 of 12 datasets. The model remains lightweight (~40K params) with linear complexity and low-latency inference (<5ms),suitable for resource-constrained edge devices and real-time deployment.

</details>


### [204] [Structure Learning on Clustered Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08238)
*Ryan Thompson,Matt P. Wand,Veerabhadran Baladandayuthapani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a mixed-effects structure learning approach for DAGs that estimates a global causal structure while accounting for cluster-level variations via a differentiable graph coupling mechanism, with provable convergence and asymptotic consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DAG structure learning methods assume homogeneous populations and cannot handle clustered data with cluster-specific variations (e.g., patient-specific effects), limiting their applicability in real-world settings.

Method: The authors extend the fixed- and random-effects framework from classical mixed models to structure learning by proposing a differentiable graph coupling mechanism that ensures the union of fixed- and random-effects graphs remains acyclic, along with a provably convergent first-order optimization method using efficient batched updates across clusters.

Result: The method recovers the true structure asymptotically with established identifiability, and experiments on real and synthetic data show it detects dependencies missed by alternative estimators.

Conclusion: The proposed mixed-effects DAG learning approach effectively handles clustered data by jointly estimating global and cluster-specific causal structures, outperforming homogeneous-population alternatives.

Abstract: Recent algorithmic advances have made directed acyclic graph (DAG) structure learning scalable for causal discovery. Yet, the currently available techniques assume a completely homogeneous population, precluding their application to clustered data where cluster-specific variations (e.g., patient-specific effects) are common. We address this issue by introducing a new approach that estimates a global structure while accounting for local cluster-level effects. The key idea is to extend the fixed- and random-effects framework of classical mixed models to the structure learning setting. Towards this end, we present a differentiable graph coupling mechanism that guarantees the union of the fixed- and random-effects graphs remains acyclic. Computationally, we provide a provably convergent first-order method and leverage efficient batched updates across clusters. Statistically, we establish identifiability of the model and show that our approach recovers the true structure asymptotically. In experiments on real and synthetic data, our proposal detects dependencies missed by alternative estimators, underscoring its value for structure learning in clustered settings.

</details>


### [205] [An interpretable Good--Turing restart criterion for k-means++](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08243)
*Renato Cordeiro de Amorim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GTRC is a principled restart criterion for k-means++ that uses Good-Turing estimation and confidence bounds to automatically determine when further restarts are unlikely to improve results, adapting the number of restarts to dataset difficulty rather than using a fixed arbitrary number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The number of restarts for k-means++ is almost always chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly, wasting computation on easy datasets while under-serving hard ones, and undermining comparisons relying on such arbitrary choices.

Method: GTRC combines a Good-Turing estimate, a proven unconditional bound, and a confidence-based bound on the probability that a further restart would improve the current result, stopping when this probability falls below a user-specified tolerance ε.

Result: Across 36 datasets, GTRC reached clustering quality competitive with well-chosen fixed restart counts, while the number of restarts used varied considerably and appropriately with dataset difficulty, governed by an interpretable, data-dependent signal.

Conclusion: GTRC offers a principled and reportable alternative to fixing the number of k-means++ restarts in advance.

Abstract: The k-means++ algorithm is commonly restarted multiple times to avoid poor local optima, yet the number of restarts is almost always chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly regardless of data set difficulty. This undermines any comparison relying on such a choice and wastes computation on easy data sets while potentially under-serving hard ones. We introduce GTRC, a restart criterion combining a Good-Turing estimate, a proven unconditional bound, and a confidence-based bound on the probability that a further restart would improve on the current result, stopping once this probability falls below a user-specified tolerance $\varepsilon$. Across 36 data sets, GTRC reached clustering quality competitive with well-chosen fixed restart counts, while the number of restarts used varied considerably and appropriately with data set difficulty, governed by an interpretable, data-dependent signal rather than a fixed rule. GTRC offers a principled and reportable alternative to fixing the number of $k$-means++ restarts in advance. Software:https://github.com/RCdeAmorim/Good-Turing-Restart-Criterion.

</details>


### [206] [CASL-VAE: Learning Structured Latent Variables from Unpaired Data for Semi-supervised Clustering and Paired Sample Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08254)
*Sai Spandana Chintapalli,Pratik Chaudhari,Christos Davatzikos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CASL-VAE is a deep contrastive latent variable model that learns structured latent factors from unpaired data, separating shared common variation from target-specific heterogeneity (modeled as discrete subtypes and continuous within-subtype variation), enabling improved subtype recovery and cross-domain analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
