Motivation: Decentralized federated learning (DFL) removes central servers but existing gossip-based methods lack provenance finality and resilience to Byzantine or lazy participants; ledger-assisted FL improves auditability but reintroduces global coordination costs conflicting with DFL locality.

Method: Nodes exchange model payloads with neighbors while full nodes collect event certificates and receiver-endorsed accepted gossip proofs, reconstruct a compact Topology DAG, and run Hashgraph-style virtual voting followed by compact full-node certificates. Finality is over unique model-origin tuples. Combines payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation.

Result: Experiments on MNIST classification and Penn Treebank language modeling (N up to 100) show learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL while reducing coordination bottlenecks, improving throughput, and maintaining high invalid-origin detection under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation.

Conclusion: gspDAG-FL achieves secure, auditable DFL with proven safety and conditional liveness of the control plane, convergence guarantees for certified perturbed gossip under time-varying effective mixing, and practical efficiency gains over ledger-based alternatives.

Abstract: Decentralized federated learning (DFL) removes the central server by letting nodes exchange model updates through peer-to-peer gossip, but existing gossip-based methods often lack provenance finality and resilience to Byzantine or lazy participants. Ledger-assisted federated learning (FL) improves auditability, yet blockchains, shards, or settlement committees can reintroduce global coordination costs that conflict with DFL locality. This paper proposes \emph{gspDAG-FL}, a secure DFL framework that derives consensus from the same gossip history used to disseminate models. Nodes exchange model payloads only with neighbors, while full nodes collect event certificates and receiver-endorsed accepted gossip proofs, reconstruct a compact Topology directed acyclic graph (DAG), and run Hashgraph-style virtual voting followed by compact full-node certificates. Finality is over unique model-origin tuples, not identical local parameter states. To improve resilience, gspDAG-FL combines payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation. We formalize the adversarial setting, prove safety and conditional liveness of the control plane, and give a convergence guarantee for certified perturbed gossip under time-varying effective mixing. Experiments on MNIST classification and Penn Treebank language modeling, using fair held-out validation/audit data and networks up to \(N=100\), show that gspDAG-FL achieves learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL while reducing coordination bottlenecks, improving throughput, and maintaining high invalid-origin detection under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation.

</details>


### [233] [EdgeRefine: Privacy-Utility Balance for Graphs via Jaccard Sampling under Edge Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08659)
*Wenxiu Ding,Muzhi Liu,Zheng Yan,Mingjun Wang,Yifan Zhao,Qiao Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EdgeRefine is a local differential privacy framework for graph neural networks that improves the privacy-utility trade-off by adaptively refining edges through Jaccard similarity-based probability estimation, noise-calibrated true/false edge sampling, and sparsity control, achieving strong privacy protection (with resilience against reconstruction attacks) while maintaining competitive accuracy across node and graph classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph structure can leak sensitive link information, and existing edge-level differential privacy methods inject noise into all adjacency matrix elements, where stronger privacy requires more noise that significantly degrades utility, making the privacy-utility balance a major barrier to practical privacy-preserving graph learning.

Method: EdgeRefine first estimates edge-existence probabilities using Jaccard similarity and ranks edges for noisy edge removal. It uses the privacy budget ε to determine the ratio of true to false edges, samples them separately based on the probability ranking, and controls the total number of edges with a separate sampling rate k.

Result: Under ε=2.5, EdgeRefine improves node classification accuracy over SOTA baselines by 17.8% on ACM (GAT) and 19.7% on Cora (GCN). Graph classification achieves ~5% average accuracy degradation vs. noise-free baseline. Reconstruction attack resilience shows relative absolute error levels above 1 across all budgets, averaging 1.962 on Cora and 1.472 on AMAP.

Conclusion: EdgeRefine effectively addresses the privacy-utility trade-off in privacy-preserving graph learning, achieving accuracy comparable to noise-free baselines and substantially outperforming other privacy-preserving methods across diverse datasets and GNN architectures while maintaining strong resilience against privacy leakage.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have shown considerable success in learning from graph-structured data, but their use in privacy-sensitive areas remains difficult because graph structure can leak sensitive link information. To satisfy edge-level differential privacy, a common approach is to inject noise into all elements of the graph's adjacency matrix, thereby obfuscating the existence of any single edge. However, stronger privacy requires more noise, and excessive noise reduces utility, making the privacy-utility balance a major barrier to practical privacy-preserving graph learning.
  To address this issue, we propose EdgeRefine, a local differential privacy framework that improves this trade-off through adaptive edge refinement. EdgeRefine first estimates edge-existence probabilities using Jaccard similarity and ranks edges for noisy edge removal. To ensure the sparsity and reliability of the final graph, it uses the privacy budget $ε$ to determine the ratio of true to false edges, samples them separately based on this probability ranking, and controls the total number of edges with a separate sampling rate $k$. Extensive experiments show that EdgeRefine achieves accuracy comparable to the noise-free baseline and substantially outperforms other privacy-preserving methods across datasets and GNN architectures. Under privacy budget $ε= 2.5$, EdgeRefine improves node classification accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines by 17.8\% on ACM under GAT and 19.7\% on Cora under GCN. In graph classification, it achieves an average accuracy degradation of around 5\% compared to the noise-free baseline. Under graph reconstruction attacks, EdgeRefine maintains relative absolute error levels above 1 across all privacy budgets, averaging 1.962 on Cora and 1.472 on AMAP, indicating strong resilience against privacy leakage.

</details>


### [234] [Resample or Reroute? Budget-Aware Test-Time Model Selection for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08665)
*Teng-Ruei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a budget-aware test-time model selection policy (Resample-or-Route, RoR) that optimally allocates a per-query cost budget between resampling the current LLM and rerouting to a different LLM, using an imperfect verifier to maximize expected correctness. Experiments on 11 models and 4 benchmarks show RoR achieves a better cost-quality Pareto front than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No prior work treats resampling the committed model and rerouting to an alternative model as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget. Existing routers are compared against a per-instance oracle, but such oracles require correctness labels and unlimited budget—neither available in deployment.

Method: Proposes an online Resample-or-Route (RoR) allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost, grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Replay experiments use newly regenerated multi-draw correctness tensors from 11 open-weight LLMs across 4 benchmarks.

Result: RoR attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines. Largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark. Gains are verifier-gated (shrink with degraded verifier quality). Robustness shown under provider price vectors and label-free agreement verifiers.

Conclusion: Budget-aware test-time model selection that jointly considers resampling and rerouting is feasible and effective. The RoR policy provides a principled way to allocate cost budgets between the two actions, with performance contingent on verifier quality.

Abstract: Routing among large language models (LLMs) trades response quality against serving cost, motivated by the reported gap between deployed routers and a per-instance oracle. Recent analysis shows that test-time resampling can recover per-instance selection headroom that no single-commit router captures; however, that guarantee holds only under an idealized oracle equipped with correctness labels and an unconstrained budget, neither of which a deployed system has. To the best of our knowledge, no previous work treats resampling the committed model and rerouting to an alternative model as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget. Therefore, this work formulates budget-aware test-time model selection: given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate each unit of budget between resampling and rerouting so that expected correctness is maximized. An online resample-or-reroute (RoR) allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost is proposed, and its behavior is grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Replay experiments on newly regenerated multi-draw correctness tensors from an eleven-model open-weight pool over four benchmarks of differing difficulty show that the proposed RoR policy attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines for the tested pools, with the largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark; an ablation further shows the gains are verifier-gated, shrinking as verifier quality degrades, and robustness replays under a provider price vector and a label-free agreement verifier delineate where the conclusions carry over.

</details>


### [235] [A Practical Investigation of Training-free Relaxed Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08690)
*Guoxuan Xia,Luka Ribar,Paul Balanca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques, unifies existing approaches in a shared framework, benchmarks them, and provides practical takeaways, including that relaxation requires careful capability evaluation and many methods need a drafter that is a good language model, limiting their use with lightweight multi-token-prediction drafters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether relaxing the lossless guarantee of standard speculative decoding can yield further speed-ups, controlled capability-speed trade-offs, or capability gains, and to provide practical guidance for practitioners.

Method: The authors practically investigate training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques, unify existing approaches within a shared framework, and benchmark them on contemporary settings.

Result: Relaxation can require considerable capability evaluation unlike lossless speculative decoding, and many relaxed approaches rely on a drafter that is a good language model, making them unsuited for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

Conclusion: The paper distills takeaways and empirical findings for practitioners regarding relaxed speculative decoding.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates sampling from an autoregressive LLM by using a faster auxiliary model to draft tokens which are then verified in parallel by the LLM. Standard speculative decoding is lossless: its rejection and resampling steps exactly preserve the LLM's sampling distribution. Recent work argues that relaxing this strict guarantee can yield further speed-ups, controlled capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability gains. We practically investigate training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques, unify existing approaches within a shared framework, benchmark them on contemporary settings, and distil takeaways and empirical findings for practitioners. Important takeaways include: relaxation can require considerable capability evaluation unlike lossless speculative decoding, and many relaxed approaches rely on a drafter that is a good language model, making them unsuited for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

</details>


### [236] [MPFlow: Learning Budgeted Max-Flow Optimization on the Lightning Network with Deep Graph Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08703)
*Harrison Rush,Vincent Davis,Simone Antonelli,Vikash Singh,Jesse Shrader,Emanuele Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a graph reinforcement learning approach for optimal liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network, formulated as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem to maximize s-t max-flow through channel selection. The method uses a message-passing policy network with PPO and hub-exclusion curriculum training, outperforming heuristics on real LN snapshots and deployed in production for 4640 channel-open decisions allocating 267.3 BTC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To solve the problem of liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network: given a fixed budget, determine which channels a node should open to maximize its routing capacity.

Method: Formulated as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem selecting k edge additions to maximize s-t max-flow; solved with graph reinforcement learning using a message-passing policy network, PPO, action masking, and a hub-exclusion curriculum training strategy.

Result: The method consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple seeds and unseen graphs on real Lightning Network snapshots. Deployed in production for 4640 channel-open decisions, cumulatively allocating 267.3 BTC (~$16M) across 30 managed nodes.

Conclusion: Graph RL with capacity-aware learning (via hub-exclusion curriculum) is effective for practical LN liquidity placement, with demonstrated real-world production deployment and financial impact.

Abstract: We address liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network (LN): given a fixed budget, which channels should a node open to maximize its routing capacity? We cast this as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem on graphs, selecting $k$ edge additions that maximize $s$--$t$ max-flow, a theory-grounded measure of routing capacity, and solve it with graph reinforcement learning. Our lightweight agent combines a message-passing policy network with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and action masking, and is trained under a hub-exclusion curriculum: the network's top hubs are removed from training subgraphs, forcing the policy to learn capacity-aware placement rather than hub attachment. In extensive experiments on real Lightning Network snapshots, our method consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple seeds and unseen graphs. The agent has been deployed in production for peer recommendations, executing 4640 channel-open decisions that cumulatively allocate 267.3 BTC over $16 million across 30 managed nodes.

</details>


### [237] [Deep Learning for Joint Narrowband Interference Cancellation and Soft Demodulation in OFDM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08717)
*Emmanouil Kavvousanos,Francky Catthoor,Vassilis Paliouras*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified deep learning framework (NBI-CNet + LLR-CNet) jointly performs narrowband interference cancellation and robust soft demodulation for OFDM, eliminating error floors, reducing complexity by up to 60%, and achieving near-optimal performance without retraining across FFT sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional compressed-sensing NBI mitigation suffers from high sequential latency and leaves structured non-Gaussian residuals that cause LLR unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when using classical Gaussian demappers.

Method: Two-stage deep learning pipeline: (1) NBI-CNet uses a physics-informed convolutional architecture for single-pass multi-tone interference estimation and removal without prior interferer count knowledge; (2) LLR-CNet acts as a structural whitener mapping non-Gaussian residuals onto calibrated soft metrics.

Result: 60% complexity reduction vs. EOMP-IDS (N=2048, Q=64); eliminates error floors; within 0.2–0.5 dB SNR of optimal iterative baseline at BLER=10⁻⁴ under severe interference (SIR=−10 dB); >3 dB coding gain under mild interference (SIR=10 dB, Q=12); removes 2×10⁻⁴ error floor; scale-invariant across FFT sizes.

Conclusion: The joint DL framework successfully resolves the pipeline mismatch between CS-based NBI mitigation and classical soft demapping, delivering efficient, robust, and generalizable interference cancellation for OFDM systems.

Abstract: Narrowband interference (NBI) severely degrades orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM) systems by corrupting subcarriers and rendering classical soft demodulation ineffective. Conventional compressed-sensing (CS) mitigation exhibits high sequential latency and leaves structured, non-Gaussian residuals that cause log-likelihood ratio (LLR) unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when employing classical Gaussian demappers. We resolve this pipeline mismatch using a unified deep learning framework for joint NBI cancellation and robust soft demodulation. First, NBI-CNet employs a physics-informed convolutional architecture to estimate NBI parameters and remove multi-tone interference in a single forward pass. Without requiring prior knowledge of the active interferer count, NBI-CNet reduces computational complexity by up to 60% ($N{=}2048, Q{=}64$) compared to the state-of-the-art EOMP-IDS algorithm. Second, LLR-CNet acts as a structural whitener by mapping non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated soft metrics. Simulations demonstrate that this joint framework eliminates the error floors inherent to traditional baselines across dense grids. Under severe interference ($\text{SIR}{=}{-}10$ dB), the pipeline operates within a $0.2$ to $0.5$ dB SNR margin of the optimal iterative baseline at a target block error rate (BLER) of $10^{-4}$. Under mild interference ($\text{SIR}{=}10$ dB) with heavy spectral overlap ($Q{=}12$), where classical greedy algorithms erroneously subtract valid data components and corrupt the payload, NBI-CNet avoids signal-peak confusion to deliver a coding gain exceeding $3$ dB. Finally, the architecture circumvents the $2{\times}10^{-4}$ error floor triggered by interferer-estimation errors, while its scale-invariant design enables robust generalization across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

</details>


### [238] [Latent Memory Palace: Reasoning for Control as Autoregressive Variational Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08724)
*Chuning Zhu,Eva Xu,Jose Barreiros,Krishnan Srinivasan,Paarth Shah,Abhishek Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LMP formulates reasoning in continuous control as variational inference with an autoregressive latent space (a "memory palace"), enabling adaptive test-time compute and yielding a strong policy (LMP-π) and a variable-length action tokenizer (LMP-tok).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transferring adaptive reasoning capabilities (akin to language models' chain-of-thought) to continuous control policies is challenging because direct language-space reasoning lacks the granularity needed for spatial understanding and precise motions.

Method: LMP formulates reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution, and derives a latent-space RL technique to optimize its variational lower bound, producing a policy (LMP-π) and a variable-length action tokenizer (LMP-tok).

Result: LMP-π achieves strong empirical performance in both simulation and real-world domains with interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute; LMP-tok significantly improves downstream autoregressive policies.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning for control can be effectively viewed through the lens of variational inference, enabling adaptive computation and improved performance in continuous control tasks.

Abstract: Human decision-making is highly flexible -- some actions are taken immediately; others require longer deliberation. Language models have exhibited a similar capacity for adaptive "reasoning." However, transferring this capability to continuous control policies has been challenging, as directly reasoning in language space may lack the granularity for spatial understanding and precise motions. In this work, we show that reasoning for control policies can emerge by organizing information in an autoregressive latent space reminiscent of a memory palace, where retrieval is iterative and adaptive. Our method, Latent Memory Palace (LMP), formulates reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution. We derive a latent-space reinforcement learning technique to tractably optimize its variational lower bound. The resulting policy, LMP-$π$, achieves strong empirical performance in simulation and real-world domains while exhibiting interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. We further show that the same framework yields a variable-length action tokenizer, LMP-$\texttt{tok}$, which significantly improves the performance of downstream autoregressive policies. Together, these results present a new perspective on latent reasoning for control through the lens of variational inference.

</details>


### [239] [Super Weights in LLMs and the Failure of Selective Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08733)
*Shreyas Subramanian,Adewale Akinfaderin,Akarsha Sehwag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Super Weights (high-importance individual parameters) are not universally critical for LLM performance; training them in isolation collapses accuracy, while structured low-rank updates (e.g., LoRA) over entire layers succeed, showing parameter importance ≠ trainability in isolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether Super Weights—individual parameters whose removal drastically degrades LLM performance—are actually trainable in isolation, and whether targeting them is a viable fine-tuning strategy.

Method: Training only Super Weight coordinates (100–8,192 params) and their local neighborhoods (up to 36K params) in OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B; comparing against random parameter training in the same layers and against vanilla LoRA (low-rank updates over entire layers); ablation with 10 seeds constraining LoRA updates to Super Weight coordinates.

Result: Training Super Weights in isolation drops accuracy to random-guessing levels; local neighborhoods provide no improvement. Random parameter training outperforms Super Weight training. Vanilla LoRA with 0.16% parameters succeeds, while constraining LoRA to Super Weight coordinates yields results statistically indistinguishable from the collapse.

Conclusion: Parameter importance (pruning sensitivity) does not imply parameter trainability in isolation; effective fine-tuning requires structured low-rank decompositions over entire layers, not targeted updates of individually important weights.

Abstract: Recent work identified Super Weights, individual parameters whose removal degrades model performance by orders of magnitude. We show that this degradation due to pruning Super Weights does not universally apply to all LLMs. Furthermore, if these parameters are so important, Super Weight-aware training should be effective. We show the opposite. Training Super Weights in isolation (100 to 8,192 parameters) drops accuracy to random-guessing levels on both OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B, and expanding to local neighborhoods of up to 36K parameters provides no improvement. The failure is specific to Super Weight coordinates: training an equal number of randomly chosen positions in the same down_proj layers instead improves over the baseline, so the collapse comes from targeting Super Weights, not from sparsity itself. Vanilla LoRA, updating every position in attention weight matrices through low-rank structure, succeeds with only 0.16% of parameters, and applying the same low-rank update to down_proj succeeds as well. A 10-seed ablation confirms that constraining LoRA updates at positions corresponding to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results. These findings establish that parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability in isolation, and that effective fine-tuning relies on structured decompositions over entire layers rather than targeting individually important weights.

</details>


### [240] [Dimensionality Reduction Meets Network Science: Sensemaking on UMAP's kNN Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08746)
*Duen Horng Chau,Donghao Ren,Fred Hohman,Dominik Moritz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UMAP's internal kNN graph, typically overlooked, can be analyzed with standard graph algorithms (PageRank, k-core decomposition, clustering coefficient) to enhance data sensemaking, offering competitive/complementary results to purpose-built methods on MNIST and Fashion MNIST.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typical UMAP workflows focus on the lower-dimensional embedding and ignore the rich kNN graph constructed internally, which encodes the data manifold before distortion from 2D projection.

Method: Applied standard graph algorithms to UMAP's internal kNN graph: (1) PageRank for identifying representative data points, (2) k-core decomposition for revealing dense core vs. sparse periphery, and (3) clustering coefficient for detecting tight-knit neighborhoods.

Result: Quantitative and qualitative evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST shows that these graph-based analyses are practical and competitive with or complementary to purpose-built methods (e.g., k-medoids for exemplar selection, HDBSCAN for density-based clustering).

Conclusion: The internal kNN graph in UMAP has untapped potential; standard graph algorithms applied to it enhance data sensemaking effectively.

Abstract: While UMAP is widely used for exploring high-dimensional data, typical workflows focus on its lower-dimensional embedding, largely overlooking the rich k-nearest-neighbor (kNN) graph that UMAP constructs internally. This graph encodes the data manifold in its original high-dimensional space, before the distortion that UMAP's 2D projection introduces. We demonstrate the untapped potential of this internal representation, showing how standard graph algorithms applied to this graph enhance data sensemaking: (1) PageRank identifies representative data points, (2) k-core decomposition reveals dense core regions versus sparse periphery, and (3) clustering coefficient detects tight-knit neighborhoods with highly-similar data points. Through quantitative and qualitative evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST, we show that these graph-based analyses are not only practical but also competitive with or complementary to purpose-built methods (e.g., k-medoids for exemplar selection, HDBSCAN for density-based clustering).

</details>


### [241] [SLORR: Simple and Efficient In-Training Low-Rank Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08754)
*David González-Martínez,Shiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLORR is a simple, stateless, architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization that uses GPU-friendly approximations of Hoyer sparsity and nuclear norm metrics to induce compressibility in neural networks without modifying model architecture or requiring SVD decompositions, demonstrating effectiveness on ImageNet-1K classification and LLM pretraining up to 560M parameters with minimal training overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-time low-rank regularizers have limitations: they require expensive SVDs of large weight matrices, modify model architecture by introducing additional trainable parameters, or rely on stateful cached quantities. The authors aim to address these issues with a simpler, more efficient approach.

Method: SLORR directly regularizes original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for forward and backward passes of two regularizer variants: Hoyer sparsity metric and nuclear norm. The framework is stateless (no cached quantities), architecture-preserving (no additional trainable parameters), and comes with approximation guarantees.

Result: On ImageNet-1K, SLORR induces compressibility with less than 8% training overhead for ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, ViT-L/16, and ResNet-18. In LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales, SLORR-Hoyer preserves model performance substantially better than unregularized models while adding less than 1% average training overhead.

Conclusion: SLORR provides an efficient, practical solution for in-training low-rank regularization that avoids the drawbacks of prior methods, making it suitable for large-scale model compression across both vision and language domains.

Abstract: Low-rank factorization is widely used to compress neural networks, but modern models are often not naturally amenable to aggressive factorization without significant accuracy loss. Existing training-time low-rank regularizers can improve compressibility, but they often require SVDs of large weight matrices, modify the model architecture (introducing additional trainable parameters), or rely on stateful cached quantities. To address these limitations, we introduce SLORR, a simple, stateless, and architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization, instantiated with two main variants based on the Hoyer sparsity metric and the nuclear norm. SLORR directly regularizes the original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for the forward and backward passes of the regularizers, for which we provide approximation guarantees. We first evaluate SLORR on ImageNet-1K across short-horizon continued training of ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, and ViT-L/16, and pretraining of ResNet-18, where SLORR induces compressibility while introducing less than 8% training overhead. We further evaluate SLORR-Hoyer in LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales: SLORR-trained compressed models preserve performance substantially better than unregularized models while adding less than 1% average training overhead.

</details>
